Singular set estimates for solutions to elliptic equations in higher co-dimension Thanks: M.E. was partially supported by NSF DMS CAREER 2143719. He would also like to thank Eugenia Malinnikova for her encouraging words at the beginning of the project. Y.S. was partially supported by NSF DMS 2154219. C.J. was partially supported by Simons Foundation grant 563916 SM, NSF DMS CAREER 2143719, the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Union’s Horizon 2020 research and innovation programme (grant agreement 101018680), and would like to thank Eugenia Malinnikova and Mariana Smit Vega Garcia for helpful conversations regarding the Almgren frequency function.
Max Engelstein, Cole Jeznach, and Yannick Sire

Abstract
. Recent advances in quantitative unique continuation properties for solutions to uniformly elliptic, divergence form equations (with Lipschitz coefficients) has led to a good understanding of the vanishing order and size of singular and zero set of solutions. Such estimates also hold at the boundary, provided that the domain is sufficiently regular. In this work, we investigate the boundary behavior of solutions to a class of elliptic equations in the higher co-dimension setting, whose coefficients are neither uniformly elliptic, nor uniformly Lipschitz. Despite these challenges, we are still able to show analogous estimates on the singular set of such solutions near the boundary. Our main technical advance is a variant of the Cheeger-Naber-Valtorta quantitative stratification scheme using cones instead of planes.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 28A78, 35J70. Secondary: 35A02

1.
Introduction

The question of quantitative unique continuation, i.e. what can the zero or singular set of a non-trivial solution to an elliptic equation look like, has a long history, starting with foundational work in
[
HHL98
,
HS89
,
GL86
,
Lin91
,
DF88
]
. In more recent years, motivated both by applications to geometric problems and by conjectures of Yau and Nadirashvili on the size of nodal domains for eigenfunctions in terms of their eigenvalues, there has been a tremendous amount of progress. In particular, the development of quantitative-stratification techniques introduced by Cheeger, Naber and Valtorta in e.g.
[
CNV15
,
CHN15
,
CN13
,
NV17b
]
(see also the recent paper dealing with PDEs with Hölder coefficients
[
HJ23
]
) as well as the more combinatorial arguments introduced by Logunov, Malinnikova and their collaborators in
[
LM18
,
Log18a
,
Log18b
,
LMNN21
]
, has led to several breakthroughs in quantitative unique continuation properties for solutions to linear and nonlinear elliptic PDE. The above-mentioned works concern
interior
unique continuation and geometric measure estimates of
interior
nodal sets.

Many of the above techniques have also been applied to the related questions of
boundary
unique continuation. Collectively, these sorts of results say that if
u
satisfies

(1.1)

\displaystyle-\Delta u

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\text{ on }V\subset\partial\Omega,

where
V\subset\partial\Omega
is a relatively open subset of the boundary, and
\partial\Omega\cap V
is sufficiently regular, then the set where normal derivative
\partial_{n}u
vanishes,
\mathcal{S}(u)\coloneqq V\cap\{\partial_{n}u=0\}
cannot be too large. The precise smoothness required on
\partial\Omega
(and what too large means) are major open questions with applications to control theory (see
[
SW78
]
). To date it has been proven that:

(1)

if
\partial\Omega\cap V
is given by a
C^{1,\mathrm{dini}}
graph, then
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap V)<\infty

[
KZ22
]
,

(2)

if
\partial\Omega\cap V
is given by small-constant Lipschitz graph, then
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\mathcal{S}(u))=0

[
Tol23
]
.

Furthermore, an example of
[
KZ23
]
shows that the Dini-smoothness assumption is sharp if by small one means finite
(n-2)
-Hausdorff measure. If by small one means zero surface measure, then examples of domains with mutually singular harmonic and surface measure show that some rectifiability of the boundary must be assumed. We described above the latest developments leading to some optimal smoothness of the boundary but previous results with smoother boundaries are proved in
[
AE97
,
AEK95
,
McC23
]
for instance.

In this article, we study an analogous boundary unique continuation problem for a class of degenerate-singular elliptic PDEs in domains whose boundary is of low dimension. Roughly speaking, we consider domains
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose boundaries
\partial\Omega
are quantitatively
d
-dimensional with
d<n-1
, and operators of the form

(1.2)

\displaystyle-\divv(\mathcal{A}\dist(X,\partial\Omega)^{-n+d+1}\nabla u)=0,

where
\mathcal{A}
is uniformly elliptic and satisfies a certain smoothness and structure conditions. Such operators were introduced in
[
DFM21
]
in order to provide an analogue of harmonic measure (the hitting measure of Brownian motion) which detects sets of co-dimension larger than 2. Notice that since
d<n-1
, the operator above is neither uniformly elliptic, nor uniformly Lipschitz no matter the choice of the (elliptic) matrix
\mathcal{A}
. Despite this, recent work (see for example,
[
DFM19
,
DM23
,
DFM23a
]
) has shown that solutions of (
1.2
) in many ways exhibit similar behavior to their co-dimension one counterparts (i.e., harmonic functions). In this setting, it turns out we can also prove a boundary-unique continuation type result for solutions provided that
\partial\Omega
and
\mathcal{A}
are sufficiently regular. Before we state the theorem, we need to introduce two key notions:

For a
C^{1}(\Omega)
solution
u
of (
1.2
), define the
singular
set of
u
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\coloneqq\{X\;:\;u(X)=\left\lvert\nabla u(X)\right\rvert=0\}.

Of course the choice of the word singular here is to refer to the fact that
\mathcal{S}(u)
contains the set where the nodal set of
u
,
Z(u):=\{X\;:\;u(X)=0\}
, is not given locally by a smooth hypersurface. Our main concern shall be uniform estimates on the size of
\mathcal{S}(u)
near
\partial\Omega
, since a standard re-scaling argument allows one to reduce analogous estimates on compact subsets of
\Omega
to the works in, say,
[
NV17b
]
. Finally, we introduce the notion of doubling index (à la
[
GL86
]
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{N}_{u}(X_{0},r)\coloneqq\dfrac{\int_{B_{8r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\dist(X,\partial\Omega)^{-n+d+1}\;dX}{\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\dist(X,\partial\Omega)^{-n+d+1}\;dX}.

Theorem 1.1

(Main result)
.

Suppose:

(1)

0\in\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
d
-dimensional
C^{1,1}
graph parametrized by
\phi\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{n-d})
with
\left\lVert\nabla\phi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq{C_{2}}
,

(2)

Let
u\in C(B_{1})
solve

\displaystyle L_{\infty}(u):=-\divv(\dist(X,\Gamma)^{-n+d+1}\nabla u)=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad\text{in}\,\,B_{1}\setminus\Gamma

\displaystyle u|_{\Gamma\cap B_{1}}=

\displaystyle 0.

Then there exists
r_{0}=r_{0}(n,d,C_{2})\in(0,1)
small enough and
C=C(n,d,C_{2},\mathscr{N}_{u}(0,100r_{0}))>1
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{s}(\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{r_{0}})\leq Cs^{2},\qquad\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}})\leq C.

Moreover,
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}}
is countably
(n-2)
-rectifiable: there exists countably many
(n-2)
-dimensional Lipschitz graphs
\Sigma_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n-2}\left(\left(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}}\right)\setminus\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{i}\right)=0.

We remark first that our Theorem
1.1
also holds for operators as in (
1.2
) with
\mathcal{A}
satisfying certain smoothness and structure conditions (see Corollary
6.5
for a precise statement). In fact, after a change of variables argument, Theorem
1.1
will follow from Theorem
6.4
, which proves a similar result when
\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We show that this change of variables leads to an operator which satisfies the conditions of Theorem
6.4
in Appendix
B
, see also Corollary
6.5
. Unfortunately, we do not know how to show that the pushforward of the
L_{\infty}
operators through this change of variables is sufficiently smooth without passing through estimates associated to the so-called regularized distances introduced in Appendix
B
. That the euclidean distance does not behave well with respect to this change of variables is a phenomenon also encountered in the work of David-Feneuil-Mayboroda, see the end of Section 1.4 in
[
DFM19
]
. Finally, we should mention that we do not expect assumption on the smoothness of
\phi
to be sharp but, given that the higher co-dimension setting already contains many new technical difficulties, we chose to work with
C^{1,1}
-graphs in order to highlight the main ideas. In line with the sorts of boundary unique continuation results that hold for harmonic functions
[
KZ22
]
, it is reasonable to expect that the sharp regularity on
\phi
should be that
\nabla\phi
has a Dini modulus of continuity. However, extending Theorem
1.1
to such geometries would require new techniques and ideas which are not present in either this paper or the co-dimension 1 literature.

The main Theorem above says that the singular set
\mathcal{S}(u)
of solutions to
L_{\infty}(u)=0
is at most
(n-2)
-dimensional, and comes with
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}
measure estimates in
\overline{\Omega}
, at least locally. In the case of harmonic functions
u
in (
1.1
), however, the results of
[
KZ22
,
Gal23
,
Tol23
]
say that for smooth enough domains, the singular set
\mathcal{S}(u)
only saturates a small portion of
\partial\Omega
(i.e., has zero surface measure, or is a co-dimension one subset of
\partial\Omega
). In our setting, since
\Gamma=\partial\Omega
is of dimension
d\leq n-2
, the
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}
estimate provided by Theorem
1.1
says nothing about the relative size of
\mathcal{S}(u)

inside

\Gamma
, and indeed the conclusion obtained here is in some sense the best possible. In our study of solutions to equations of the type (
1.2
), we construct examples of nontrivial,
\Lambda
-homogeneous solutions of an equation of the type (
1.2
) in the flat-space
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose singular set
\mathcal{S}(u)
consists of a union of
(n-2)
-planes. For example, there exists solutions of the form
u(x,t)=v(x)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{\gamma}\phi(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
where
v
is a homogeneous harmonic polynomial,
\gamma>0
and
\phi
is a spherical harmonic on
\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}
. Moreover, for an appropriate choice of
\gamma>2
these planes contain
\partial\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and so these examples show both that
\mathcal{S}(u)
can be
(n-2)
dimensional, and moreover, it is possible that
\mathcal{S}(u)\supset\Gamma
. This is one of several phenomena which distinguishes the higher co-dimension setting from the classic co-dimension one problem. We explore these differences through a series of examples in Section
2
below. We should also state explicitly that these examples show the estimates in Theorem
1.1
are sharp in the sense that
\mathcal{H}^{n-2-\varepsilon}(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}})=+\infty
is possible for any
\varepsilon>0
(in fact, we show something even stronger, see the discussion at the end of Section
2
). The exact dependence of the constants
C>0
on the doubling index
\mathscr{N}_{u}
is an open problem even in the classical setting, and we make no attempt to optimize that dependence here. However, the new covering arguments introduced here do not introduce fundamentally different errors than those in
[
NV17b
]
, and we expect the same (non-optimal) dependence in terms of
\mathscr{N}_{u}
.

It is worth remarking that the (countable) rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}(u)
is a short consequence of the work of
[
NV17b
]
, and the fact that
d\leq(n-2)
. Indeed, we will see that in regions far from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e., balls
B_{r}(Y)
so that
B_{2r}(Y)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}=\varnothing
),
u
satisfies a uniformly elliptic equation with Lipschitz coefficients, and so we can apply the main Theorem of
[
NV17b
]
to deduce the
(n-2)
-rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}(u)
there. Since
B_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
is contained in a countable of such balls
B_{r}(Y)
, we have no problem in deducing the countable
(n-2)
-rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}(u)\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
. On the other hand, the techniques of this article do not immediately show that
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{1/2}
is contained in
finitely
many Lipschitz graphs of dimension
(n-2)
, which is indeed the conclusion shown in
[
NV17b
]
. The main reason our result differs here is the necessity of a quantitative stratification using cones instead of planes, so while locally finite rectifiability seems plausible, it remains outside the scope of this paper.

In the work
[
STT20
]
, the third author with Terracini and Tortone also studied solutions to a class of degenerate-singular elliptic equations and introduced methods to study the nodal set structure of solutions to

(1.3)

-\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{\alpha}\nabla u)=0\text{ in }B_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1},

where
X=(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{t}
and
\alpha\in(-1,1)
. There, they prove nodal set estimates for such solutions
u

inside
the characteristic manifold,
\{t=0\}
, where the equation becomes singular or degenerate, and as an application, they prove quantitative unique continuation results for solutions of the fractional Laplacian. While the spirit of their result is certainly related to that of Theorem
1.1
, a key difference is that we provide estimates on the singular set
in a neighborhood of
the boundary. Of course, in
[
STT20
]
the authors were motivated by the celebrated result of Caffarelli and Silvestre
[
CS07
]
identifying fractional powers of the Euclidean Laplacian as the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map of operators of the type above, and thus were not so interested in the singular set outside of the characteristic manifold. In the case of the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map as in
[
CS07
]
the parameter ranges
\alpha\in(-1,1)
and the weight falls into the well-known class of
A_{2}
-weights as investigated in e.g.
[
FKS82
]
. For further applications, it is however important to consider larger powers. We believe that the tools and techniques we develop in the present work could be used to investigate finer properties of the geometric measure for the singular set (and nodal sets ) of solutions to (
1.3
) in a neighborhood of the set
\{t=0\}
. We emphasize that in this context the characteristic manifold is a hypersurface. Interestingly, as a follow-up of
[
STT20
]
, Terracini, Tortone and Vita investigated in the very nice paper
[
TTV24
]
applications of such degenerate equations (and the regularity of their solutions) to higher order boundary Harnack. Indeed in
[
DSS15
]
, De Silva and Savin proved a very unexpected higher order (in Hölder spaces) Boundary Harnack Principle. The starting point in
[
TTV24
]
, is that the quotient of two solutions satisfies a similar PDE with coefficients which on the zero set are degenerate.

1.1.
Technical comparison to prior work

As mentioned above, away from
\partial\Omega
our equation is uniformly elliptic (with smooth coefficients) and inside
\partial\Omega
our estimates are trivial. Thus Theorem
1.1
is most interesting in that it gives uniform estimates on
\mathcal{S}(u)
in a
neighborhood
of the boundary,
\partial\Omega
. There has been some work in the classical case which also gives estimates in a neighborhood of the boundary, in particular
[
McC23
,
KZ22
]
. Our work (and that of
[
KZ22
]
) differs from
[
McC23
]
in that there is no quantity which is uniformly monotone both in the interior and on the boundary. That is to say, for interior points, our variant of the Almgren frequency formula, see (
4.8
), is only monotone at scales far smaller than the distance from the point to the boundary.

This challenge is also present in
[
KZ22
]
who overcome it through a clever change of coordinates at the boundary and adapting the powerful rectifiable-Reifenberg scheme of Naber-Valtorta
[
NV17a
]
. However, in our problem there is an additional technical challenge, that the blowups on the boundary solve a completely different equation (and have different qualitative properties) from the blowups in the interior, see Section
2
below. Indeed, in the interior, the model equation is an elliptic constant coefficient PDE, but on the boundary, the model equation is more akin to the degenerate operator (
1.3
). This leads to many more technical difficulties, for example, at interior points the solution cannot “look like its blowup” except at scales far smaller than the distance from the point to the boundary. We should mention that while this challenge is not present in
[
KZ22
]
, they do work with rougher boundaries and prove finer structure (i.e. rectifiability) of the singular set.

To overcome this technical difficulty, we introduce a new variation on the quantitative cone-splitting argument of Cheeger-Naber-Valtorta (see, e.g.
[
CNV15
]
). Briefly
1
1

1

for a more in-depth discussion, see the beginning of Section
6
, the original argument quantifies the fact that if a solution is homogeneous around two different points, it must be invariant along the line connecting those points. It goes on to use this quantification to conclude that singular points which are near one another and “look singular” at comparable scales must both lie close to a lower dimensional affine space. From there a covering argument shows that the singular set must be lower dimensional. As mentioned above, we cannot meaningfully apply this observation in our setting, since no interior singular point will “look singular” at scales comparable to its distance from a boundary singularity. Instead, we show that in a neighborhood of a singular point on the boundary, singularities not on the boundary are constrained to live in a specific cone dictated by the blowup of the boundary singular point. See Corollary
5.8
for the precise statement. The fact that the boundary is smooth tells us that these cones all point in approximately the same direction. This then gives us enough linear structure to employ a variant of the Cheeger-Naber-Valtorta covering argument and prove our Theorem
1.1
.

1.2.
Outline of the argument and the paper

As alluded to above, Theorem
1.1
is proved by applying a careful change of variables that flattens
\Gamma
and instead studying solutions to equations of the type (
1.2
) with certain smoothness and structure assumptions on
\mathcal{A}
in the flat space,
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see Theorem
6.4
and Corollary
6.5
). Since this change of variables introduced in
[
DFM19
]
is quite technical, we leave its presentation to the Appendix
B
, but the main takeaway is the existence of such a change of variables (Theorem
B.5
). We mention again that the reason we need to work with the regularized distances at all here is to ensure that after the change of variables the new equation has coefficients with sufficient regularity.

Once we are in the flat setting, we show that an Almgren-type quantity is monotone increasing when centered on the boundary ( see Lemma
4.6
). As usual in these arguments, the existence of this monotone quantity controls the rate of vanishing of solutions. This argument is inspired by and closely follows the work of
[
GL86
]
, but with extra technical difficulties caused by the co-dimension
>1
setting. The monotone quantity also allows us to perform blow-ups: taking a sequence of rescalings of a solution to the equation and showing it limits to a homogeneous solution,
u_{\Lambda}
, of the “constant coefficient” operator
-\mathrm{div}(\delta^{-n+d+1}(X)\nabla u_{\Lambda})=0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\mathbb{R}^{d}
(where
\delta(X)
is the distance to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
). The proof of monotonicity and the blowup analysis is done in Section
4
.

When we classify the homogeneous solutions (in Section
3
) the analysis departs substantially from the classical case (where the blowups are essentially homogeneous harmonic polynomials). In fact, an extremely rich array of behavior can take place depending on the relative values of
n,d
and the homogeneity. Still, we are able to classify all such solutions, in Theorem
3.3
. Our argument here is inspired by an analogous classification in
[
CSS08
]
, but again the higher co-dimension introduces substantial difficulties and necessitates new arguments.

In Section
5
we quantify the blowup analysis of the previous section. In particular, we show that general solutions are well approximated by homogeneous solutions whenever the frequency is approximately constant in a large range of scales. It is in this section that we prove the key geometric estimate Corollary
5.8
, which shows that singular points in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\mathbb{R}^{d}
which are close to a singularity in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, must be contained in a cone around a lower dimensional affine space.

In Section
6
we combine our estimates in the novel covering argument explained above to prove our main results.

Notations and definition of weak solutions

Let us recall rapidly for the convenience of the reader some notation regarding the correct function spaces in studying solutions of equations of the type (
1.2
), though for a more in-depth review, we point the reader to
[
DFM21
]
. In addition, we introduce here useful notation that will be used in the remainder of the paper.

For
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega
is the graph of a Lipschitz function
\phi\in\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{n-d})
we
2
2

2

for the elliptic theory we only need
\Gamma
to be
d
-Ahlfors regular but for our purposes, the Lipschitz graph assumption is not restrictive
consider operators of the form

(1.4)

\displaystyle L\coloneqq-\divv(A\nabla\,\cdot\,\,).

Here we make the standing assumption that the matrix
\mathcal{A}\coloneqq A\delta(X)^{n-d-1}
is assumed to be symmetric, uniformly elliptic, and bounded, where
\delta(X)\coloneqq\dist(X,\Gamma)
.
The important function spaces are

\displaystyle W=W(\Omega)\coloneqq\{u\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)\;:\;\nabla u\in L^{2}(\Omega,w)\}

where
w(X)=\delta(X)^{-n+d+1}
for
X\in\Omega
. Since these weighted measures come up quite frequently, we write for convenience

dm(X)=w(X)dX

and

d\sigma_{w}(X)=w(X)d\sigma(X)=w(X)d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(X)

for the weighted Lebesgue measure and weighted surface measure respectively.

Finally we define a local version of
W
suitable for defining solutions of (
1.4
) as follows: for
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, the set
W_{r}(E)
is defined as

\displaystyle W_{r}(E)\coloneqq\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E)\;:\;\phi f\in W\text{ for all }\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(E)\}.

It can be shown
[
DFM21
]
that each
W_{r}(E)
is also characterized as

\displaystyle W_{r}(E)\equiv\{f\in L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E)\;:\;\nabla f\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(E,w)\}.

Finally, we say that
u\in W_{r}(E)
is a solution to the equation
Lu=0
in
E
provided that for any
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(E)
, one has

\displaystyle\int A(X)\nabla u(X)\cdot\nabla\phi(X)\;dX=0.

Similarly, we say that
u
is a subsolution in
E
provided that

\displaystyle\int A(X)\nabla u(X)\cdot\nabla\phi(X)\;dX\leq 0

for any such
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(E)
with
\phi\geq 0
, and
u
is a supersolution in
E
provided that
-u
is a subsolution in
E
.

Much of the standard elliptic theory (i.e., existence and uniqueness of solutions, Caccioppoli’s inequality, Hölder continuity and De-Giorgi-Nash-Moser bounds for solutions, etc.) holds for equations of the type (
1.4
) under these mild standing assumptions. In the end, we shall need several of these elliptic estimates (as well as others not proven in
[
DFM21
]
). To avoid cluttering the beginning of this paper with these standard arguments, we refer the reader to Appendix
A
.

2.
Examples of homogeneous solutions in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}

Here we give some examples of homogeneous solutions to the system:

(2.1)

\displaystyle-\divv(\delta^{-n+d+1}(X)\nabla u(X))=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad\forall X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle u(X_{0})=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad\forall X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.

As mentioned above we hope these examples illustrate how solutions to these degenerate PDEs can have singular sets which differ in behavior dramatically from the singular sets of solutions to uniformly elliptic PDE.

The trivial codimension-one case

Whenever
a(x,r)
is a homogeneous harmonic polynomial in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
vanishing on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
, then
u(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
is a homogeneous solution to (
2.1
) vanishing on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Frequencies can be non-integral

Let
\Lambda>0
be chosen, let
\lambda_{j}
be an eigenvalue of the Laplace-Beltrami operator on
\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}
and let
\phi_{j}
be a eigenfunction associated to that eigenvalue. If
v(x)
is a homogeneous harmonic polynomial of degree
\Lambda-\gamma_{j}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\gamma_{j}
is chosen so that
\gamma_{j}(\gamma_{j}-1)=\lambda_{j}
, then the function

\displaystyle u(x,t)\coloneqq v(x)|t|^{\gamma_{j}}\phi_{j}(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert)

is a
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution vanishing on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. As
\gamma_{j}
is generally not an integer, this shows that the space of homogeneities,
\mathcal{F}
, is not contained in
\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
. Furthermore, since
\lambda_{j}\rightarrow\infty
as
j\rightarrow\infty
(so then
\gamma_{j}\rightarrow\infty
as well), this shows also that there exists homogeneous solutions of arbitrarily large (but finite) order of vanishing on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular, that
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
is possible for homogeneous solutions.

Non trivial structure of homogeneous solutions.

In the above construction, it is interesting to ask whether given a single homogeneity
\Lambda
, it is possible to find multiple different eigenvalues such that
\Lambda-\gamma_{j}
is an integer. This would then allow us to create non-trivial linear combinations of solutions as above and indeed, such solutions may not necessarily be even or odd.

Even when
n-d=2
, this is possible, but to simplify computations we will give an example when
n-d-2=8
. In this dimension we look at the eigenvalues associated to harmonics of homogeneity two
\lambda_{2}=2(2+8)
and twelve
\lambda_{12}=12(12+8)
. If we let
\gamma_{2}=5
and
\gamma_{12}=16
then
\gamma_{i}(\gamma_{i}-1)=\lambda_{i}
. Then for any pair of integers
k_{i}
such that

\displaystyle k_{2}+\gamma_{2}=k_{12}+\gamma_{12},

and any homogeneous harmonic polynomials
v_{i}
of degree
k_{i}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
vanishing on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
, we have that the function

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=2,12}v_{i}(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{\gamma_{i}}\phi_{i}(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert)

is a
(k_{2}+\gamma_{2})
-homogeneous solution to (
2.1
) vanishing on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that is neither even nor odd. This last fact can be checked from the observation that
\phi_{i}
are even (since they come from the second and twelfth eigenfunctions on the sphere), while
k_{i}
must have opposite parities since the
\gamma_{i}
have opposite parities. Thus one of the terms
v_{i}(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{\gamma_{i}}\phi_{i}(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
is even, while the other is odd.

Large singular sets.

Given that in higher co-dimension the singular set can contain all of the boundary, it is natural to ask whether Hausdorff dimension or measure is really the correct way to capture the size of the singular set. That is, perhaps it is better to use a measure which varies as one approaches the boundary, to better reflect the degeneracy in the equation. The following examples shows that doing so naively will not work.

Consider the radial extension of
u(x,y,z)\coloneqq z(xy^{2}-yx^{2})
, a homogeneous harmonic polynomial in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. If
v(x,y,t)=u(x,y,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
, then
v
is a homogeneous solution of (
2.1
) in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
which vanishes on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
whose singular set contains
\{0,0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
. This example demonstrates that the singular set of solutions vanishing on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
can be
n-2
dimensional, and in fact, that the
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}
–measure of such solutions can be scale-invariant in
\left\lvert t\right\rvert
. In particular, this example shows that if one wants to replace
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}
in Theorem
1.1
with a measure
\nu
defined by
d\nu(X)=g(\delta(X))d\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(X)
, then
g
must satisfy
\int_{\{0,0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-2}}g(\delta(X))\;d\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(X)<\infty
. In a similar manner, if one considers solutions of the form
a(x)r^{\gamma}\phi(\omega)
where
a
is harmonic in
x
,
\phi
is a Laplace eigenfunction on the sphere, and
\gamma
is appropriately chosen, one can construct examples where the singular set for this solution contains
\mathcal{S}(a)\times\mathbb{R}^{n-d}
as well as
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty)\times\mathcal{S}(\phi)
, extending the previous example to produce examples of homogeneous singular sets having more complicated geometries (and in particular, showing that there can be a plane of singular points which is not perpendicular to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
).

3.
Homogeneous solutions to “constant coefficient” equations

In this section, our goal is to provide a fine analysis of the order of vanishing of solutions to equations of the type (
1.4
) which vanish on
\Gamma
in the simplest setting: when the boundary
\Gamma
is affine, and
\cal{A}=I
is the identity matrix. That is, we are interested in solutions to (
2.1
), which we rewrite here

\displaystyle-\divv(\delta^{-n+d+1}(X)\nabla u(X))=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad\forall X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle u(X_{0})=

\displaystyle 0,\qquad\forall X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}.

As mentioned above, homogeneous solutions to these equations model the infinitesimal behavior of solutions to (
1.4
) in general, at least when
\Gamma
is smooth enough. We will make more precise statements in later sections (see Lemma
5.7
).

In Theorem
3.3
we provide a complete description of global
homogeneous
solutions
u_{\Lambda}
in this affine setting. Of particular importance is the fact that the singular sets
\mathcal{S}(u_{\Lambda})
of these homogeneous solutions satisfy Minkowski estimates depending only on
\Lambda
and the dimension, which is the content of Theorem
3.10
.

Here is where our methods begin to depart from the more classical case of harmonic functions, where Hausdorff measure estimates for the nodal and singular set of harmonic polynomials follow rather quickly from classical algebraic-geometric facts (see for example,
[
HS89
, Theorem 2.1]
). These Minkowski estimates we obtain here will later be combined with the basic geometric argument of Lemma
5.11
to deduce that the singular sets of
homogeneous
solutions are contained in cones (of bounded aperture) about
(n-2)
planes, which can further be passed to more general solutions via an approximation argument. These cone conditions, will be the most important part of our new covering argument in the proof of the main Theorem.

Before we begin the classification, we make two remarks which may be helpful to the reader:

Remark 3.1
.

We shall also frequently use the fact that for any
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\Omega
, any solution
u
of (
2.1
) is also smooth in
\Omega^{\prime}
since the coefficients
\delta(X)^{-n+d+1}\equiv\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}
are smooth there. As a consequence, we see that
u
satisfies the equation (
2.1
) pointwise in the usual sense in
\Omega^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.2
.

It is worthwhile to point out the rotational symmetry of the equation (
2.1
), which in turns gives us a correspondence between harmonic functions and rotationally-invariant solutions of (
2.1
). In particular, a straight-forward computation shows that if
u(x,t)
is rotationally-invariant in
t
in the sense that

\displaystyle u(x,t)=v(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)

for some other function
v
, then
u
solves (
2.1
) if and only if
v
is harmonic.

3.1.
Characterization of homogeneous solutions and their properties

In this section, we provide a complete (explicit) description of all homogeneous solutions to the equation (
2.1
) in the flat space
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
that vanish on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Such solutions play a role analogous to the role homogeneous harmonic polynomials play with respect to harmonic functions. Our argument is adapted from
[
CSS08
, Lemma 2.7]
, though the main tool for our Liouville argument comes from Proposition
A.2
instead of Hölder continuity estimates. We shall see shortly by a separation of variables argument that Laplace eigenfunctions on the sphere (i.e., homogeneous harmonic polynomials) still play an important role in this setting, so for concreteness, let us denote by
\lambda_{j}\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
the eigenvalues associated to
-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}
:

\displaystyle\lambda_{j}\coloneqq j(j+n-d-2).

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
u\in W_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a non-trivial solution to (
2.1
) which vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and which is homogeneous of degree
\Lambda\geq 0
. Then
\Lambda
lives in the discrete set

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}\coloneqq\mathbb{N}\cup\{\Lambda^{\prime}\;:\;(\Lambda^{\prime}-k)(\Lambda^{\prime}-k-1)=\lambda_{j}\text{ for some }k,j\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}\}\subset[1,\infty)

and
u
admits the following expansion:

\displaystyle u(x,t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{N(\Lambda)}a_{j}(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)\phi_{j}(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert),

where the
\phi_{j}
are Laplace eigenfunctions on
\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}
, in particular
-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}\phi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}
. Furthermore the
a_{j}
have the following properties:

(1)

Each
a_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})\cap C(\overline{\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}})
is homogeneous of degree
\Lambda
, and satisfies

(3.1)

\begin{split}\Delta_{x,r}a_{j}(x,r)-\dfrac{\lambda_{j}}{r^{2}}a_{j}(x,r)&=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{d+1},\\
a_{j}(x,0)&=0.\end{split}

In particular, each
a_{j}(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)\phi_{j}(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
is a
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution of (
2.1
) vanishing continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

(2)

When
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
then
a_{j}(x,r)
is a polynomial, and otherwise
r^{\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil-\Lambda}a_{j}(x,r)
is a polynomial in the variables
x
and
r
. In either case this polynomial is either even or odd. Moreover,
a_{0}
is a homogeneous harmonic polynomial in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
vanishing on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
.

(3)

For
j>0
, the smallest power of
r
appearing in the expansion
a_{j}(x,r)
is
r^{\gamma}
for some
\gamma\geq\gamma^{*}=(1+\sqrt{5})/2>3/2
. If
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, then in fact
\gamma\geq 2
.

(4)

The sum can be taken so that
N(\Lambda)\in\mathbb{N}
is the smallest integer with
\lambda_{N(\Lambda)}>\Lambda^{2}
.

Proof.

We know by standard elliptic theory that
u
is smooth in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
and satisfies
Lu=0
in the classical sense away from
\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
It is useful to use the cylindrical coordinates
(x,r,\omega)
, where
r=\left\lvert t\right\rvert
and
\omega=t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert
. In these coordinates, for every
x,r
fixed, we may expand
u(x,r,\cdot)
in spherical harmonics (since the direct sum of spherical harmonics of differing degrees is
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1},\sigma)
):

(3.2)

\displaystyle u(x,r,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}(x,r)\phi_{j}(w),

where as in the statement of the Theorem,
\phi_{j}
is a spherical harmonic of degree
j
associated to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}=j(j+n-d-2)
:

\displaystyle-\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}\phi_{j}=\lambda_{j}\phi_{j}.

Of course the coefficients
a_{j}
are explicit:

\displaystyle a_{j}(x,r)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}u(x,r,\omega)\phi_{j}(\omega)\;d\sigma(\omega),

so that since
u
is homogeneous of degree
\Lambda
and smooth in the variables
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r
for
r>0
, the same holds true for
a_{j}(x,r)
. Hence
a_{j}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})
and vanish continuously on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
.

A priori
, the convergence in (
3.2
) is in the
L^{2}
sense, but by the smoothness of
u
, we actually know more: inside compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
a_{j}(x,r)
decay faster than any polynomial in
j
(see Theorem 5 in
[
See66
]
). Because of this, the expansion (
3.2
) converges uniformly, and similarly for all derivatives of the expansion (computed term by term). Hence one may differentiate term by term in
x
,
r
, and
\omega
in the expansion (
3.2
) to compute the derivatives of
u
.

Now we re-write the operator
L=-\divv(r^{-n+d+1}\nabla\cdot)
in cylindrical coordinates, so that outside
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have

\displaystyle 0=Lu

\displaystyle=-\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\nabla u)

\displaystyle=(n-d-1)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d-1}t\cdot\nabla u-\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\Delta_{x,t}u

\displaystyle=(n-d-1)r^{-n+d-1}t\cdot\nabla u-r^{-n+d+1}\left(\Delta_{x}u+\partial_{r}^{2}u+\dfrac{n-d-1}{r}\partial_{r}u+\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}u\right)

(3.3)

\displaystyle=-r^{-n+d+1}\left(\Delta_{x}u+\partial_{r}^{2}u+\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}u\right),

where
\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}
is the spherical Laplacian in the
\omega
coordinate. Here we have used the fact that
t\cdot\nabla_{t}u=r\partial_{r}u
. In particular,
u
solves (classically) in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
the equation

\displaystyle\Delta_{x}u+\partial_{r}^{2}u+\dfrac{1}{r^{2}}\Delta_{\mathbb{S}^{n-d-1}}u=0.

Applying this operator to the expansion (
3.2
) term by term, and using the fact that
\phi_{j}
are orthogonal, we see readily that the
a_{j}(x,r)
satisfy the linear elliptic equation (
3.1
).

Along with the homogeneity requirement of
a_{j}
, we can classify all such solutions of (
3.1
). The simplest case is when
j=0
, since then
\lambda_{j}=0
so
a_{0}(x,r)
is a homogeneous harmonic function in
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
vanishing on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
, and so we can extend
a_{0}(x,r)
to be a homogeneous harmonic polynomial in all of
\mathbb{R}^{d+1}
.

Next we move on to the other
\lambda_{j}
. Let
\ell=\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil+\tfrac{d+1}{2}
. Then Proposition
A.2
applied to
u
in the ball
B_{R}=B_{R}(0)
implies that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R/2}}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}^{\ell+1}u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{-2(\ell+1)+2}\int_{B_{R}}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{-2(\ell+1)}\int_{B_{2R}}u^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{-2+2(\Lambda-\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil)-d-1}m(B_{2R})\sup_{B_{1}}u^{2}.

where the second inequality follows from Caccioppoli’s inequality
[
DFM21
, Lemma 8.6]
, and the third follows from the fact that
u
is homogeneous of degree
\Lambda
. It is easy to see that
m(B_{R})=\int_{B_{R}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\;dX
is homogeneous of degree
d+1
, so
m(B_{2R})\leq CR^{d+1}
, and we have thus shown

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R/2}}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}^{\ell+1}u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq R^{-2}\sup_{B_{1}}u^{2}.

Letting
R\rightarrow\infty
we obtain
\nabla_{x}^{m+1}u\equiv 0
, and thus this implies for
j\geq 0
and
r>0
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}^{\ell+1}a_{j}(x,r)\equiv 0,

or in other words, for each
r
fixed,
a_{j}(x,r)
is a polynomial of degree at most
\ell
. Thus we may expand even further,

\displaystyle a_{j}(x,r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell}b^{j}_{k}(x)r^{\Lambda-k}

where
b_{k}(x)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
k
. Here we have used the fact that
a_{j}
is homogeneous of degree
\Lambda
, so the coefficients in front of
b_{k}^{j}(x)
(which a priori are just
functions
of
r
) must be homogeneous in
r
. Moreover, notice that since
a_{j}(x,r)
are homogeneous of degree
\Lambda
and vanish on
\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
, it must be that
b_{k}^{j}(x)\equiv 0
for
k>\Lambda
, and thus we may write

\displaystyle a_{j}(x,r)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{m}b^{j}_{k}(x)r^{\Lambda-k},

where
m=\left\lfloor\Lambda\right\rfloor
.

To recap we have proven that the
a_{j}
satisfy properties (i) and (ii) in the theorem (except we have not shown that each
a_{j}
must either be even or odd). We now turn towards proving each
a_{j}
is either even or odd, properties (iii) and (iv) and the claim on the structure of the frequency set,
\mathcal{F}
. At this stage, let us consider the case that
\Lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, so that computing derivatives in
r
is simpler (since
\partial_{r}^{2}(r^{\Lambda-k})\neq 0
). The case when
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
can be considered similarly, though one needs some care in dealing with the terms
k=m
and
k=m-1
.

Let
k_{j}
be the largest
k\leq m
so that
b_{k}^{j}(x)\not\equiv 0
. Since
\Delta_{x,r}a_{j}-(\lambda_{j}/r^{2})a_{j}=0
classically, by comparing like powers of
r
using the expansion for
a_{j}
, we obtain the relations

(3.4)

\displaystyle\left((\Lambda-k_{j})(\Lambda-k_{j}-1)-\lambda_{j}\right)b^{j}_{k_{j}}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

(3.5)

\displaystyle\left((\Lambda-k_{j}-1)(\Lambda-k_{j}-2)-\lambda_{j}\right)b_{k_{j}-1}^{j}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

and similarly, for
0\leq k\leq k_{j}
,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\Delta b_{k}^{j}(x)+\left((\Lambda-k-2)(\Lambda-k-3)-\lambda_{j}\right)b^{j}_{k-2}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

with the understanding that by definition,
b_{-1}^{j}=b_{-2}^{j}\equiv 0
.
Since
b_{k_{j}}^{j}\not\equiv 0
, then
(\Lambda-k_{j})(\Lambda-k_{j}-1)=\lambda_{j}
and we have
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
as in the theorem statement. Furthermore we note that

\displaystyle(\Lambda-k_{j}-\ell)(\Lambda-k_{j}-\ell-1)\neq\lambda_{j},

for any
1\leq\ell\leq\Lambda-k_{j}
. Thus
b_{k_{j}-1}^{j}\equiv 0
from (
3.5
) and, using the recursive relation (
3.6
), this gives
b_{k_{j}-\ell}^{j}\equiv 0
for
\ell
odd,
1\leq\ell\leq k_{j}
. Finally, remark that since
\lambda_{j}\geq 1
, then the positive solution
\gamma
of
\gamma(\gamma-1)=\lambda_{j}
satisfies
\gamma\geq\gamma^{*}=(1+\sqrt{5})/2
, and so in particular,
\Lambda-k_{j}\geq\gamma^{*}
. This shows that the lowest power of
r
appearing in the expansion of
a_{j}
is at least as large as
\gamma^{*}
. It further implies that
\mathcal{F}\subset[1,\infty)
as claimed.

Moreover, we know that

\displaystyle\Lambda^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(\Lambda-k_{j})(\Lambda-k_{j}-1)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{j},

which implies that the sum in (
3.2
) is actually finite: whenever
j
is such that
\lambda_{j}>\Lambda^{2}
,
a_{j}(x,r)\equiv 0
. This completes the proof of the theorem when
\Lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}
.

When
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
(so that
m=\Lambda
), then remark that again we may compare like powers of
r
for the equation (
3.1
) for
a_{j}
. In this case, when comparing the lowest powers of
r
(
r^{-2}
and
r^{-1}
) we obtain the relations

\displaystyle-\lambda_{j}b_{m}^{j}

\displaystyle\equiv 0

\displaystyle-\lambda_{j}b_{m-1}^{j}

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

from which we see that
k_{j}
(defined as above) must satisfy
k_{j}\leq m-2
. The analysis from (
3.4
) onwards then applies, and in this case,
a_{j}(x,r)
is a polynomial in
x
and
r
, which completes the proof of the Theorem (notice that in this case, in fact, the lowest power of
r
appearing in the expansion is at least
2
).
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Slightly more analysis gives finer information on the expansion of
u
in Theorem
3.3
whenever
\Lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}
or
n-d-2
is even (so in particular the critical co-dimension 2 case).

If
\Lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}
, then the representation of
u
consists of a single term,
u=a_{j}\phi_{j}
. This can be shown as follows. If there are two solutions of the equation

(3.7)

\displaystyle(\Lambda-k_{i})(\Lambda-k_{i}-1)=j_{i}(j_{i}+n-d-2)

for
k_{i},j_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
, then from the explicit formula

\displaystyle\Lambda-k_{i}=\dfrac{1}{2}\left(1+\sqrt{1+4(j_{i}^{2}+j_{i}(n-d-2))},\right)

we see that
2(\Lambda-k_{1})-2(\Lambda-k_{2})
is an integer, which happens only when
1+4(j_{i}^{2}+j_{i}(n-d-2))
are perfect squares. Then in this case, we would see that
2\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
. Now since we assume
\Lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\Lambda=p/2
with
p\in\mathbb{N}
odd. Of course though, we readily see that
(p/2-k)(p/2-k-1)
is not an integer for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, from which we obtain a contradiction to the fact that
(\Lambda-k_{i})(\Lambda-k_{i}-1)
are integers. Altogether this proves the claim that when
\Lambda\not\in\mathbb{N}
, then there do not exist two solutions to (
3.7
), and from the proof of Theorem
3.3
we see that only one of the
a_{j}
can be nonzero, proving the claim.

On the other hand, when
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
and
n-d-2
is even, then we can show that the expansion of
u=\sum_{j}a_{j}\phi_{j}
only includes
j
even. This follows from examination of the equation (
3.7
): when
n-d-2
is even and
\Lambda\in\mathbb{N}
, then the left-hand side is an even integer always, while the right-hand side is even only when
j_{i}
is even. In particular, solutions only exist for
j_{i}
even, and thus again the proof of Theorem
3.3
shows that
a_{j}=0
for all
j
odd.

Remark 3.5
.

Since
\cal{F}
is a discrete set, we can write

(3.8)

\displaystyle\cal{F}=\{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\Lambda_{3},\dotsc,\}

where
\Lambda_{k}<\Lambda_{k+1}
, and by courtesy of Theorem
3.3
,
\Lambda_{1}=1<3/2<\Lambda_{2}
. We shall use this notation later in Section
5
.

3.2.
Volume estimates on singular set of homogeneous solutions

With all homogeneous solutions characterized, we can now provide explicit volume estimates on the tubular neighborhoods of homogeneous solutions to equations closely related to (
2.1
). This will be key in our blowup analysis. In the case of homogeneous harmonic polynomials, the quantitative estimates follow from algebraic geometric considerations (see Theorem 2.1 in
[
HS89
]
). Here we use similar ingredients but with additional complications due to the fact that our solutions are not necessarily polynomials.

First, let us introduce the class of operators we consider, which shall appear later as blowups of equations of the type (
1.4
) for matrices
A
satisfying a certain smoothness condition (see Definition
4.1
).

Definition 3.6
.

We say that a matrix
\mathcal{A}
separates
x
and
t
if it takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\begin{pmatrix}J&0\\
0&hI_{n-d}\end{pmatrix}

where
J
is a
d\times d
matrix,
h\in\mathbb{R}
, and
I_{n-d}
is the identity.

Notice that the following space
H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
depends only on parameters
\Lambda
and
\lambda
, and not the particular matrix
\mathcal{A}_{0}
.

Definition 3.7
.

For each
\Lambda>0
and
\lambda>0
, denote by
H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
the linear space of
\Lambda
-homogeneous solutions
u\in W_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
which vanish continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and satisfy

(3.9)

\displaystyle-\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}_{0}\nabla u)=0\text{ in }\mathbb{R}^{n},

for some constant matrix
\mathcal{A}_{0}
which is uniformly elliptic with constant
\lambda>0
and separates
x
and
t
.

Remark 3.8
.

Notice that for each
\lambda,\Lambda>0
,
H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}\subset C(B_{1})
is a compact subset. Indeed we shall shortly see in the proof of Theorem
3.10
that each
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution of (
3.9
) (after a constant change of variables through the matrix
\mathcal{A}_{0}
in (
3.9
)) coincides with a
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution of (
2.1
), which by Theorem
3.3
, are contained in a finite dimensional subspace of
C(B_{1})
. Since the space of such matrices
\mathcal{A}_{0}
which are uniformly elliptic with constant
\lambda>0
is also compact, one deduces that
H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
is compact. Of course one could also deduce such compactness using Lemma
4.13
later.

Recall that for
u\in L^{2}(dm)
and
X_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{r\rightarrow 0}\fint_{B_{r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;dm=0,

we define the vanishing order of
u
at
X_{0}
,
p
, to be the quantity

\displaystyle p\coloneqq\sup\{k\geq 0\;:\;\limsup_{r\downarrow 0}r^{-k}\left(\fint_{B_{r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;dm\right)^{1/2}=0\}.

Remark that when
u
is smooth in a neighborhood of
X_{0}
, then Taylor’s Theorem implies that the order vanishing of
u
at
X_{0}
is simply the smallest
k\in\mathbb{N}
so so that
\nabla^{k}u(X_{0})\neq 0
.

With this notation, let us define for any
u\in W_{r}(\Omega)
the strata
\cal{O}^{p}(u)
and the singular set of
u
,
\mathcal{S}(u)
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}^{p}(u)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;:\;\text{ $u$ vanishes to order at least $p$ at }X\},\qquad\mathcal{S}(u)\coloneqq\mathcal{O}^{3/2}(u).

Away from the boundary solutions to (
3.9
) are smooth so we can define the usual singular set

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\Omega=\{X\in\Omega\;:\;u(X)=\nabla u(X)=0\}

for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

With our notation explained, we now prove volume estimates on the singular set of homogeneous solutions of (
3.9
) with explicit and optimal dependence on the degree of homogeneity. First we need an estimate on the volume of tubular neighborhoods of real semi-algebraic varieties. The following can be seen as a Corollary of the proof of the main Theorem in
[
Won93
]
. In what follows, whenever
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we write

\displaystyle B_{r}(A)\coloneqq\{X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;:\;\dist(x,A)<r\}

for the
r
-neighborhood of
A
, and
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}
denotes Hausdorff dimension.

Theorem 3.9

(
[
Won93
]
, see also Theorem 5.9 in
[
YC04
]
)
.

There is a dimensional constant
C_{n}>0
depending only on
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that that following holds. Suppose that
p_{1},\dotsc,p_{m}
are real polynomials in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
d=\deg p_{1}+\cdots+\deg p_{m}
, and that the set

\displaystyle V\coloneqq\{p_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dotsc,p_{m}(x)\geq 0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

is such that
\dim_{\mathrm{H}}V=k\in\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}
. Then, for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}\left(B_{\varepsilon}(V)\cap B_{2}(0)\right)\leq C_{n}(\varepsilon d)^{n-k}.

To be clear, Wongkew proves an estimate for real algebraic varieties, though the proof really depends only on Milnor’s estimate on the Betti numbers of a real algebraic variety, which bounds the number of its connected components. However, this result also holds for semi-algebraic varieties: if
V=\{p_{1}(x)\geq 0,\dotsc,p_{m}(x)\geq 0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a real semi-algebraic variety and
d=\deg p_{1}+\dotsc+\deg p_{m}
, then the sum of the Betti numbers of
V
(and hence the number of connected components of
V
) is at most
C_{n}d^{n}

[
Mil64
, Theorem 3]
. One can also see a formulation similar to Theorem
3.9
in Section 5 of
[
YC04
]
.

Theorem 3.10
.

Let
u\in H_{\lambda}^{\Lambda}\setminus\{0\}
, so that
u
is a non-trivial
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution to (
3.9
) that vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
\Lambda\neq 0
. Then
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
, and there exists a universal constant
C>0
depending only on the dimensions
n
and
d
and the ellipticity
\lambda
so that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{1})+\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{S}(u))\cap\partial B_{1})\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\Lambda^{2},

and thus
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}\left(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{1}(0)\right)\leq C\Lambda^{2}.

Proof.

It suffices to assume that
u\in H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
solves (
2.1
); the more general case follows via a standard change of variables. To give a bit more detail, if a homogeneous
u
solves an equation of type (
3.9
) then
v(X)\coloneqq u(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)
satisfies (
2.1
) and vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the same homogeneity, since
\mathcal{A}_{0}
separates
x
and
t
. Furthermore the change of coordinates
x\mapsto\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X
is bi-Lipschitz with non-vanishing gradient, so it maps the singular set to the singular set and increases measure by at most a multiplicative constant (depending on
\lambda
).

Let
u
be a non-trivial
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution to (
2.1
), so that by Theorem
3.3
,
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
. Notice that since
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
is nonzero, then
\Lambda\geq 1
. Our main estimate shall be on singular points outside
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so define for any
v
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(v)\coloneqq\mathcal{S}(v)\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}).

As in the proof of Theorem
3.3
, let us adopt the notation
r=\left\lvert t\right\rvert
and
\omega=t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert
. Then for any
\gamma>0
, notice that we have the set equality

(3.12)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(u)=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(r^{\gamma}u),

simply because for
r\neq 0
,
u
and
r^{\gamma}u
have the same order of vanishing at any fixed point
X\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Define the transformation on functions,
T
, by
Tv(x,r,\omega)\coloneqq r^{2(N(\Lambda)+\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil-\Lambda)}v(x,r^{2},\omega)
. This transform will be important for homogeneous solutions because it allows us to relate their singular sets to those of polynomials. One easily verifies that for
v
smooth in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, (
3.10
) and (
3.12
) imply that

(3.13)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(v)

\displaystyle=F(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tv)),

where
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by
F(x,r,\omega)=(x,r^{2},\omega)
. Notice that
F
is locally bi-Lipschitz in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and in fact,
\left\lVert\nabla F\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1}(0))}\lesssim 1
. Along with (
3.13
), such properties of
F
guarantee that for any
v
smooth in
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(v))\cap B_{1}(0))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(F(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tv)))\cap B_{1}(0))

\displaystyle\leq C\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{C\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tv))\cap B_{2}(0)))

for some dimensional
C>0
.
In view of this estimate, to show the first inequality in (
3.11
) it suffices now to show that for our solution
u
,

(3.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu))\cap B_{2}(0))\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\Lambda^{2},

simply because
\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap B_{1}(0))\leq\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap B_{1}(0))\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\Lambda^{2}
. We now turn to the proof of (
3.14
).

By Theorem
3.3
, we know that
u
admits the expansion

\displaystyle u(x,r,\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{N(\Lambda)}a_{j}(x,r)\phi_{j}(\omega).

Decompose
u=u_{e}+u_{o}
where

\displaystyle u_{e}(x,r,\omega)\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0,\\
j\text{ even }\end{subarray}}^{N(\Lambda)}a_{j}(x,r)\phi_{j}(\omega),\qquad u_{0}(x,r,\omega)\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1,\\
j\text{ odd }\end{subarray}}^{N(\Lambda)}a_{j}(x,r)\phi_{j}(\omega),

and notice that
u_{e}
and
u_{0}
are
\Lambda
-homogeneous solutions of equation (
2.1
), and so is
u_{e}-u_{0}
. Since
T
is linear, we have that
Tu=Tu_{e}+Tu_{o}
. Now we further decompose

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu)=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{2},

where
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu)\cap\{Tu_{e}=0\}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{2}=\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu)\setminus\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
.

To deal with
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
, recall that
\mathcal{O}^{p}
denotes the points with vanishing order at least
p
and observe

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}

\displaystyle\subset\mathcal{O}^{3}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2}))

\displaystyle\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu_{e}+Tu_{o})\cup\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu_{e}-Tu_{o}).

Indeed, if
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
, then by definition
Tu=Tu_{e}+Tu_{o}
vanishes to order at least
2
at
X
, and if
X\in\{Te_{e}=0\}
then
Tu_{e}(X)-Tu_{0}(X)=0
. This proves the first inclusion. As for the second, if
(Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2}
vanishes to order
3
at
X\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then at least one of
Tu_{e}+Tu_{o}
or
Tu_{e}-Tu_{o}
must vanish to order at least
2
at
X
.

Now since
u
is a solution to (
2.1
), a second order elliptic equation with smooth coefficients away from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the fact that
T
is Lipschitz gives that
\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu)))=\dim_{\mathcal{H}}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(u))\leq n-2
(see for example, Chapter 4 of
[
HL
]
). In particular, we have from the inclusion above,

(3.15)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{O}^{3}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2})\leq n-2.

In addition, notice that

\displaystyle\left(Tu_{e}(x,r,\omega)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ even }\end{subarray}}^{N(\Lambda)}r^{2(N(\Lambda)+\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil-\Lambda)}a_{j}(x,r^{2})\phi_{j}(\omega)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=0\\
j\text{ even }\end{subarray}}^{N(\Lambda)}r^{2N(\Lambda)-j}r^{2\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil-2\Lambda}a_{j}(x,r^{2})r^{j}\phi_{j}(\omega)\right)^{2}

is an honest polynomial in
X=(x,t)
, since by Theorem
3.3
,
r^{2\left\lceil\Lambda\right\rceil-2\Lambda}a_{j}(x,r^{2})
is a polynomial in
x,r^{2}=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}
, and
r^{j}\phi_{j}(\omega)
is a homogeneous harmonic polynomial of degree
j
in
t
since
\phi_{j}
is a spherical harmonic in
\omega=t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert
. It is also easy to verify on the condition of
N(\Lambda)
from Theorem
3.3
that the degree of this polynomial is
\lesssim\Lambda
. Similarly,
(Tu_{o})^{2}
is a polynomial in
X
of degree
\lesssim\Lambda
. Thus along with (
3.15
), Theorem
3.9
applies to
\mathcal{O}^{3}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2})
, which is the nodal set of a polynomial of degree
\lesssim\Lambda
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to give that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1})\cap B_{2}(0))

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{O}^{3}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{0})^{2}))\cap B_{2}(0))

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\Lambda^{2}.

This concludes the measure estimate for
B_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1})\cap B_{2}(0)
.

The set
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
is slightly trickier, but we still have the inclusions

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{2}

\displaystyle\subset\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\left[\mathcal{S}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2})\cap\{(Tu_{e})^{2}\geq m^{-1}\}\right]

\displaystyle\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\cup\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu_{e}+Tu_{o})\cup\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu_{e}-Tu_{o}).

Indeed for the first inclusion, if
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{2}
, then the same argument as for
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
shows
(Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2}
vanishes to order at least
2
at
X
. Since
X\not\in Z(Tu_{e})
(i.e. the nodal set of
Tu_{e}
), then
X
belongs to the union above. As for the second inclusion, suppose that
X\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has

\displaystyle X\in\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\left[\mathcal{S}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2})\cap\{(Tu_{e})^{2}\geq m^{-1}\}\right].

Then we have
Tu_{e}(X)\pm Tu_{o}(X)=0
, and thus

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\nabla((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2})(X)

\displaystyle=2Tu_{e}(X)(\nabla Tu_{e}(X)\pm\nabla Tu_{o}(X)).

Since
Tu_{e}(X)\neq 0
, then
X\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(Tu_{e}\pm Tu_{o})
, showing the inclusion.

Now for any fixed
m
, just as for the argument for
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{S}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2})\cap\{(Tu_{e})^{2}\geq m^{-1}\}\leq n-2.

Using that
(Tu_{e})^{2}
,
(Tu_{0})^{2}
are polynomials of degree
\lesssim\Lambda
, then we apply Theorem
3.9
this time along with the increasing set theorem to conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{2})\cap B_{2}(0))

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{m\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{S}((Tu_{e})^{2}-(Tu_{o})^{2})\cap\{(Tu_{e})^{2}\geq m^{-1}\})\cap B_{2}(0))

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\Lambda^{2}.

Combining our estimates on
B_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}}_{1})\cap B_{2}(0)
and
B_{\varepsilon}(\tilde{\mathcal{S}_{2}})\cap B_{2}(0)
gives (
3.14
), completing the proof of the first estimate in (
3.11
). The second estimate on
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(B_{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{S}(u))\cap\partial B_{1})
is a quick consequence of the first estimate, the coarea formula, and the fact that
\mathcal{S}(u)
is scale-invariant since
u
is
\Lambda
-homogeneous. We omit the details.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

The proof of Theorem
3.10
becomes simpler when we have finer information on the expansion of the homogeneous solution
u=\sum_{j}a_{j}\phi_{j}
, such as the first two situations outlined in Remark
3.4
. Indeed in either of the two cases outlined there, it is much easier to find a polynomial
P
for which
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(u)\subset\tilde{\mathcal{S}}(P)
without having to deal with squaring some part of the solution
u
: in fact
Tu
or
rTu
is a polynomial in
X
in either of these cases. In general, when
n-d-2
is odd, though, the terms
a_{j}
in the expansion of
u
can be all nonzero, and such simplifications do not hold, and we are forced to consider the separation of
u=u_{e}+u_{o}
as above.

4.
Solutions to the “Lipschitz coefficient” equations

In this section, we discuss monotonicity formulae for solutions to “variable” coefficient degenerate elliptic equations in the flat space
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In strong analogy with the case of uniformly elliptic equations, we require that the coefficients satisfy an appropriate Lipschitz condition as detailed in Definition
4.1
. In the end, the conjugated elliptic operator
\tilde{L}_{\beta}
obtained from the operator
L_{\beta}:=-\divv(D_{\beta}^{-n+d+1}\nabla\,\cdot)
defined outside of a
C^{1,1}
,
d
-dimensional graph
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
will satisfy this condition, where
\rho
is an appropriate change of variables flattening
\Gamma
(see Theorem
B.5
)
3
3

3

Similarly if
L_{\infty}
is conjugated by
\rho
the resulting
\tilde{L}_{\infty}
will satisfy these conditions as long as
\Gamma
is
C^{2}
. One of the key pieces of this notion of regularity is the asymptotic block-type structure of
A
near
\mathbb{R}^{d}
; when
A
satisfies the
C^{0,1}
condition we introduce, then its values can be made sense pointwise on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and these matrices separate the variables
x
and
t
(recall Definition
3.6
).

Definition 4.1
.

We say that the matrix
A
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{R}(X_{0})
(with constants
\lambda,C_{0}>0
) provided that
A\in C^{0,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B_{R}(X_{0})\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is symmetric,
\delta^{n-d-1}A
is uniformly elliptic with constant
\lambda
, the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla A(X)\right\rvert\leq C_{0}\delta(X)^{-1},\;\;X\in B_{R}(X_{0})\cap\Omega

holds, and there is a
d\times d
uniformly elliptic symmetric matrix
J(x,t)
with constant
\lambda
and a scalar function
\lambda^{-1}\leq h(x,t)\leq\lambda
so that for
\mathcal{A}=\delta^{n-d-1}A
and
\mathcal{B}(X)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(x,t)\coloneqq\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J(x,t)&0\\
0&h(x,t)I_{n-d}\end{array}\right),

we have
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}\in C^{0,1}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
with the following estimates in
B_{R}(X_{0})
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla\mathcal{A}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\nabla J\right\rvert+\left\lvert\nabla h\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{0}\delta.

Note that in the co-dimension one setting, in the domain
\{(x^{\prime},x_{n})\mid x_{n}>\phi(x^{\prime})\}
for
\phi\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{n-1})
, if the Laplacian is conjugated by the standard change of variables
(x^{\prime},x_{n})\mapsto(x^{\prime},x_{n}-\phi(x^{\prime}))
, the resulting operator will have the regularity and block structure detailed above.

Remark 4.2
.

Notice that the estimates in Definition
4.1
imply that we can make sense of the values of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{R}(X_{0})
in the sense that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(Y_{0})\coloneqq\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\fint_{B_{r}(Y_{0})}\mathcal{A}\;dX\equiv\lim_{r\downarrow 0}\fint_{B_{r}(Y_{0})}\mathcal{B}\;dX\eqqcolon\mathcal{B}(Y_{0})

exists for
Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{R}(X_{0})
. In addition, the appearance of the matrix
\mathcal{B}
is in some sense cosmetic, since if
A
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition with respect to a matrix
\mathcal{B}
, then
A
also satisfies the same condition with
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}(x,t)\coloneqq\mathcal{A}(x,0)
in place of
\mathcal{B}
. However, when proving a matrix satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition, it is still sometimes useful to opt for the definition we choose above.

In the remainder of this section, we make the standing assumption that
u\in W_{r}(B_{10})\cap C(B_{10})
is a solution of

(4.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\divv(A\nabla u)&=0,&&\text{in }B_{10},\\
u&=0,&&\text{on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{10},\end{aligned}\right.

and in addition that

(4.4)

\text{$A$ satisfies the higher co-dimension $C^{0,1}$ condition in $B_{10}$ with constants $\lambda,C_{0}>1$}.

At some points, it will be convenient to assume that one has better control on the constants
\lambda
,
C_{0}
, (which will automatically be true locally at least after a linear change of variables) in the sense that the matrix
\mathcal{A}=\delta^{n-d-1}A
satisfies for
\eta\in(0,1/2)
sufficiently small,

(4.5)

\displaystyle B_{(1-\eta)}\subset\mathcal{A}^{1/2}(X)B_{1}\subset B_{(1+\eta)}\text{ and }\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(X)-\mathcal{A}(Y)\right\rvert\leq\eta\left\lvert X-Y\right\rvert\text{ for all }X,Y\in B_{10}.

Of course the two conditions above automatically imply that in addition,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(X)^{1/2}-\mathcal{A}(Y)^{1/2}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(X)^{-1/2}-\mathcal{A}(Y)^{-1/2}\right\rvert\leq C\eta

for some universal constant
C>0
depending only on the dimension
n
. From Remark
4.2
, it is easy to check that (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) then imply that

(4.7)

\text{$A$ satisfies the higher co-dimension $C^{0,1}$ condition in $B_{10}$ with constants $1+C\eta,\;\eta$},

which in particular, are bounded constants, since
\eta<1/2
.

4.1.
Monotonicity on the boundary

For solutions of (
4.3
) we define the frequency function (inspired by
[
Tao02
,
TZ08
,
GSVG14
,
Yu17
]
, and the earlier work of
[
GL86
]
). Notice that
\mathcal{A}(X_{0})
is a positive, symmetric matrix taking the block form as in Definition
4.1
, so that
\mathcal{A}(X_{0})^{1/2}
and
\mathcal{A}(X_{0})^{-1/2}
are well-defined, positive symmetric matrices.

Definition 4.3
.

Suppose that
X_{0}\in\Gamma\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
is a solution to (
4.3
) in
B_{R}(X_{0})
that vanishes continuously on
\Gamma\cap B_{R}(X_{0})
. Suppose in addition that
A
satisfies the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{R}(X_{0})
, so that
\mathcal{A}_{0}:=\mathcal{A}(Y_{0})
exists for any
Y_{0}\in B_{R}(X_{0})
by Remark
4.2
. With the ellipsoid
E_{r}^{\mathcal{A}_{0}}(Y_{0})=E_{r}(Y_{0})
defined by

E_{r}(Y_{0})\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}B_{r}+Y_{0}\equiv\left\{\left\langle\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(X-Y_{0}),(X-Y_{0})\right\rangle<r^{2}\right\}\equiv\left\{\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}(X-Y_{0})\right\rvert<r\right\},

we define the modified Almgren frequency
N(r)=N_{u}^{A}(Y_{0},r)
for
u
and
0<r<R
small enough so that
E_{r}(Y_{0})\subset B_{R}(X_{0})
by

(4.8)

\displaystyle N(r)\coloneqq\dfrac{r\int_{E_{r}(Y_{0})}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\,dY}{\int_{\partial E_{r}(Y_{0})}\mu_{Y_{0}}u^{2}\tfrac{r}{\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(Y-Y_{0})\right\rvert}\,d\sigma(Y)}\eqqcolon\dfrac{rD_{u}^{A}(r)}{H_{u}^{A}(r)}.

Here
\mu_{Y_{0}}:=\mu
is the scalar function defined by

\displaystyle\mu(Y)\coloneqq\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}(Y-Y_{0})\right\rvert^{-2}\left\langle A(Y)\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(Y-Y_{0}),\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}(Y-Y_{0})\right\rangle.

Remark 4.4
.

Notice in Definition
4.3
that when
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{A}(Y_{0})=I
, is the identity matrix, we have the simpler formula

(4.9)

\displaystyle N(r)=\dfrac{r\int_{B_{r}(Y_{0})}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\,dY}{\int_{\partial B_{r}(Y_{0})}\mu u^{2}\,d\sigma(Y)},

where
B_{r}(Y_{0})
is the usual round Euclidean ball. Since this formula is easier to work with, we shall often assume (after a suitable change of variables) that
\mathcal{A}_{0}=I
.

Before proving the almost monotonicity of
N
when
Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we provide several useful estimates on matrices satisfying the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition. To ease notation in what follows, we frequently write
A(X)X\equiv AX
when the argument of
A
is unambiguous.

Lemma 4.5
.

Suppose that
A
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{10}(0)
with constants
\lambda,C_{0}>1
and that in addition
\mathcal{A}(0)=I
. Then
\mu_{0}(X)=\mu(X)=\left\langle AX/\left\lvert X\right\rvert,X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert\right\rangle
and we define

\displaystyle\beta(X)\coloneqq AX/\mu.

The following estimates hold in
B_{10}(0)
:

(4.10)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\delta^{n-d-1}\mu(X)-1\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lvert X\right\rvert

(4.11)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla(\delta^{n-d-1}\mu(X))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C

(4.12)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla(\delta^{n-d-1}\mu(X))^{-1}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C

(4.13)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(X)-\mathcal{A}(\pi(X))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\delta(X)

(4.14)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle\beta(X),\nabla\delta(X)\right\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\delta(X)

(4.15)

\displaystyle\left\lvert D\beta(X)-I\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lvert X\right\rvert,

(4.16)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle\beta(X)-X,\nabla\delta\right\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lvert X\right\rvert\delta(X)

with
C=C(\lambda,C_{0})
. Recall that
\pi(X)
denotes the closest point on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
X
.

Proof.

Each of these is a straight-forward computation. For the first, for example, we note that

\displaystyle\delta^{n-d-1}\mu(X)-1

\displaystyle=\left\langle\mathcal{A}(X)X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert,X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert\right\rangle-1

\displaystyle=\left\langle(\mathcal{A}(X)-\mathcal{A}(0))X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert,X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert\right\rangle,

and use the Lipschitz nature of
\mathcal{A}
. Notice from the ellipticity assumption on
\mathcal{A}
, that
\delta^{n-d-1}\mu\simeq 1
uniformly on
B_{10}(0)
. The estimate (
4.11
) then follows from our observation above that

\displaystyle\delta^{n-d-1}\mu(X)=1+\left\langle(\mathcal{A}(X)-\mathcal{A}(0))X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert,X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert\right\rangle,

so differentiating and once again using that
\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(X)-\mathcal{A}(0)\right\rvert\leq C_{0}\left\lvert X\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert\nabla\mathcal{A}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}
gives (
4.11
). Notice that (
4.12
) follows immediately as well since
\delta^{n-d-1}\mu\simeq 1
. The fourth inequality (
4.13
) is an immediate consequence of the Lipschitz nature of
\mathcal{A}
. As for the fifth, we write

\displaystyle\beta(X)=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}(X)X}{\delta(X)^{n-d-1}\mu(X)}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\mathcal{A}(\pi(X))X}{\delta(X)^{n-d-1}\mu(X)}+\dfrac{(\mathcal{A}(X)-\mathcal{A}(\pi(X))X}{\delta(X)^{n-d-1}\mu(X)},

and use (
4.13
) to estimate the inner product of the second term with
\nabla\delta
since
\left\lvert\nabla\delta\right\rvert\leq 1
. In the first term, we use the fact that
\mathcal{A}(\pi(X))=\mathcal{B}(\pi(X))
has the structure as in (
4.1
), and thus
\left\langle\mathcal{A}(\pi(X)),\nabla\delta(X))\right\rangle\lesssim\delta(X)
. This brings us to the last estimates, (
4.15
) and (
4.16
).

To obtain these inequalities, we write

\displaystyle\beta(X)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}\left(\dfrac{1}{\delta^{n-d-1}\mu}-1\right)X+\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\right)X+\mathcal{B}X,

and differentiate term by term to get the estimates

\displaystyle\left\lvert D\left((\mathcal{A}\left(\dfrac{1}{\delta^{n-d-1}\mu}-1\right)X+\left(\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\right)X\right)\right\rvert\lesssim\left\lvert X\right\rvert,

by virtue of the fact that
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
are Lipschitz, estimates (
4.10
) and (
4.12
), and that
\left\lvert\mathcal{A}-\mathcal{B}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}\delta\leq C_{0}\left\lvert X\right\rvert
. As for the last term appearing in our decomposition of
\beta(X)
, we get the same estimate as above whenever
\partial_{X_{i}}
hits a coefficient of
\mathcal{B}(X)
, and thus we see the the main contribution of
\partial_{X_{i}}\beta_{k}(X)
comes from
\sum_{j}b_{jk}(X_{j})_{X_{i}}=b_{ik}
, where
\mathcal{B}=(\overline{b}_{ij})
. Altogether this gives us that

\displaystyle D\beta(X)=\mathcal{B}(X)+O(\left\lvert X\right\rvert)=I+O(\left\lvert X\right\rvert),

since
\mathcal{B}(0)=\mathcal{A}(0)=I
.

Finally, the last inequality (
4.16
) is an improvement of (
4.14
) obtained from from integrating (
4.15
). Indeed, we can write

(4.17)

\displaystyle\left\langle\beta(X)-X,\nabla\delta(X)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\left(\beta(X)-\beta(\pi(X))\right)-(X-\pi(X)),\nabla\delta(X)\right\rangle

since
\left\langle\beta(\pi(X))-\pi(X),\nabla\delta(X)\right\rangle=0
from the assumption on the structure of the matrix
\mathcal{A}(\pi(X))=\mathcal{B}(\pi(X))
as in (
4.1
). Next, we simply estimate crudely the right-hand side of (
4.17
) using the fundamental theorem of calculus along the segment
[\pi(X),X]
and (
4.15
) to obtain (
4.16
).
∎

With these estimates, we prove almost monotonicity of the frequency
N
.

Lemma 4.6

(Almgren monotonicity, variable coefficients)
.

Assume (
4.3
), (
4.4
) and in addition that
\mathcal{A}(0)=I
. Then for
0<r<1
the frequency
N(r)
as defined in (
4.8
) is almost monotone in the sense that there is a constant
C=C(\lambda,C_{0})
for which
N(r)e^{Cr}
is monotone increasing on
(0,1)
. In particular, the limit
N(0^{+})\coloneqq\lim_{r\downarrow 0}N(r)
exists.

Proof.

By definition of
N
, we compute

\displaystyle N^{\prime}(r)=\dfrac{rD(r)}{H(r)}\left(\dfrac{1}{r}+\dfrac{D^{\prime}(r)}{D(r)}-\dfrac{H^{\prime}(r)}{H(r)}\right),

and estimate
D^{\prime}(r)
and
H^{\prime}(r)
separately. Introduce the vector field
\beta
as in Lemma
4.5
,

\displaystyle\beta(X)\coloneqq A(X)X/\mu(X),

and notice that since the unit outer normal on
\partial B_{r}
is
n(X)=X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert
, then the definition of
\beta
and
\mu
give the identities

(4.18)

\displaystyle\beta\equiv rAn/\mu,\,\,\left\langle\beta,n\right\rangle\equiv r,\text{ on }\partial B_{r}.

Let us perform all our computations first without justifying certain integration by parts (which involve singular weights such as
\delta^{-n+d}
). At the end of the proof we justify such integration by parts steps.

First, for
D^{\prime}(r)
, recall that
A=\delta^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}
, use (
4.18
) and compute for almost every
r
,

(4.19)

\begin{split}D^{\prime}(r)&=\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\;d\sigma\\
&=r^{-1}\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\left\langle\beta,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma\\
&=r^{-1}\int_{B_{r}}\divv\left(\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\beta\right)\;dX.\end{split}

Writing
\mathcal{A}=\left(\overline{a}_{ij}\right)
for the entries of
\mathcal{A}
, we have from the symmetry of
\mathcal{A}
that

(4.20)

\begin{split}\int_{B_{r}}\divv\left(\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\beta\right)\;dx&=\int_{B_{r}}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\divv(\beta)\;dX\\
&+\int_{B_{r}}\left\langle\nabla(\delta^{-n+d+1}),\beta\right\rangle\left\langle\mathcal{A}\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\;dX\\
&+\int_{B_{r}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\sum_{ijk}(\overline{a}_{ij})_{X_{k}}u_{X_{i}}u_{X_{j}}\beta_{k}\;dX\\
&+2\int_{B_{r}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\sum_{ijk}\overline{a}_{ij}u_{X_{i}}u_{X_{j}X_{k}}\beta_{k}\;dX.\end{split}

By (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) we see that
\divv(\beta)=n+O(r)
and
\left\langle\nabla\delta,\beta\right\rangle=\delta+\delta\,O(r)
since
\left\langle X,\nabla\delta(X)\right\rangle=\delta(X)
. Using also the fact that
\overline{a}_{ij}
are Lipschitz and
\left\langle\nabla(\delta^{-n+d+1}),\beta\right\rangle=((-n+d+1)+O(r))\delta^{-n+d+1}
, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}}\divv(\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\beta)\;dX=(d+1)D(r)+2\int_{B_{r}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\sum_{ijk}u_{X_{i}}u_{X_{j}X_{k}}\beta_{k}\;dX+O(rD(r)).

Integrating by parts the second term in
X_{j}
and using that
u
is a solution gives us

(4.21)

\begin{split}2\int_{B_{r}}\delta^{-n+d+1}&\sum_{ijk}\overline{a}_{ij}u_{X_{i}}u_{X_{j}X_{k}}\beta_{k}\;dX\\
&=-2\int_{B_{r}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\sum_{ijk}\overline{a}_{ij}u_{X_{i}}u_{X_{k}}(\beta_{k})_{X_{j}}\;dX+2\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla u,\beta\right\rangle\;d\sigma\\
&=2\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla u,\beta\right\rangle\;d\sigma-2D(r)+O(rD(r)),\end{split}

where the second inequality follows from (
4.15
). Putting together our computations with that of
D^{\prime}(r)
, applying (
4.18
), and using that
A
is symmetric yields

(4.22)

\displaystyle D^{\prime}(r)=(d-1)D(r)/r+2\int_{\partial B_{r}}\mu^{-1}\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle^{2}\;d\sigma+O(D(r)).

Moreover, since
\divv(Au\nabla u)=\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle
, we may write

(4.23)

\displaystyle D(r)=\int_{B_{r}}\divv(Au\nabla u)\;dX

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial B_{r}}u\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma.

Next, we move to
H^{\prime}(r)
. Setting
\overline{\mu}=\delta^{n-d-1}\mu
, we have

\displaystyle H(r)

\displaystyle=r^{n-1}\int_{\partial B_{10}}\delta(rX)^{-n+d+1}\overline{\mu}(rX)u(rX)^{2}\;d\sigma

\displaystyle=r^{d}\int_{\partial B_{10}}\delta(X)^{-n+d+1}\overline{\mu}(rX)u(rX)^{2}\;d\sigma.

Notice that
\overline{\mu}
is Lipschitz by (
4.11
), and thus

(4.24)

\begin{split}H^{\prime}(r)&=dH(r)/r+2\int_{\partial B_{r}}\mu u\left\langle\nabla u,n\right\rangle+\delta^{-n+d+1}u^{2}\left\langle\nabla\overline{\mu},n\right\rangle\;d\sigma\\
&=dH(r)/r+2\int_{\partial B_{r}}\mu u\left\langle\nabla u,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma+O(H(r)),\end{split}

since
X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert=n
on
\partial B_{r}
. If one considers the vector field
An-\mu n
, then direct computation shows (by definition of
\mu
) that
\left\langle An-\mu n,n\right\rangle=0
on
\partial B_{r}
, and thus
An-\mu n
is a tangent vector field to
\partial B_{r}
. Moreover, we have the uniform bound

(4.25)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\divv_{\partial B_{r}}(An-\mu n)\right\rvert\leq C\mu.

Indeed using (
4.18
) we can write on
\partial B_{r}
,

\displaystyle An-\mu n=r^{-1}\mu\left(\beta(X)-X\right),

and remark that
\divv(\beta(X)-X)=O(r)
by (
4.15
), while

\displaystyle\nabla\mu=\nabla(\delta^{-n+d+1}\overline{\mu}),

and
\overline{\mu}
is Lipschitz, so that

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\mu,\beta(X)-X\right\rangle=O(r)\mu

by virtue of (
4.15
) and (
4.16
) and the fact that
\mu\simeq\delta^{-n+d+1}
again. Collectively, these prove (
4.25
).

In particular the divergence theorem on
\partial B_{r}
shows

(4.26)

\begin{split}\left\lvert\int_{\partial B_{r}}2\mu u\left\langle\nabla u,n\right\rangle-2u\left\langle\nabla u,An\right\rangle\;d\sigma\right\rvert&=\left\lvert\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left\langle\nabla(u^{2}),\mu n-An\right\rangle\;d\sigma\right\rvert\\
&=\left\lvert\int_{\partial B_{r}}u^{2}\divv_{\partial B_{r}}(\mu n-An)\;d\sigma\right\rvert=O(H(r)).\end{split}

Substituting this in (
4.24
) gives

(4.27)

\displaystyle H^{\prime}(r)=dH(r)/r+2\int_{\partial B_{r}}u\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma+O(H(r))

since
A
is symmetric.

In view of (
4.22
), (
4.23
), and (
4.27
), we have shown that

\displaystyle N^{\prime}(r)
