\displaystyle=2N(r)\left\{\dfrac{\int_{\partial B_{r}}\mu^{-1}(A\nabla u\cdot n)^{2}\;d\sigma}{\int_{\partial B_{r}}u(A\nabla u\cdot n)\;d\sigma}-\dfrac{\int_{\partial B_{r}}u(A\nabla u\cdot n)\;d\sigma}{\int_{\partial B_{r}}\mu u^{2}\;d\sigma}\right\}+O(N(r)),

which proves the claims since the first term above is nonnegative by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Thus to complete the proof, we need only justify our applications of the divergence theorem on
B_{r}
(and
\partial B_{r}
) where they appear. Indeed, our arguments thus far have not used that
u
vanishes continuously on
B_{10}
which is an important part of the analysis. In what follows, we sketch the necessary steps to make such applications (and related integration by parts) rigorous.

To this end, denote for
0<\varepsilon\ll r
,

\displaystyle\Omega_{r,\varepsilon}\coloneqq B_{r}\cap\{\delta>\varepsilon\},\,\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon}\coloneqq\partial\Omega_{r,\varepsilon}\setminus\partial B_{r}\equiv\{\delta=\varepsilon\}\cap B_{r}.

Our first point to justify is the application of the divergence theorem in (
4.19
), and in the end our justification comes from performing all our analysis on
\Omega_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}
for a suitable sequence
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
and then taking limits. Using the Lipschitz nature of
u
(Lemma
A.3
) we can invoke the dominated convergence theorem, to write

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r}}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\left\langle\beta,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0}\int_{\partial B_{r}\,\cap\,\{\delta>\varepsilon_{m}\}}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\left\langle\beta,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma

(4.28)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0}\int_{\partial\Omega_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\left\langle\beta,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma.

Indeed, the second inequality holds for an appropriate choice of
\varepsilon_{m}
because
n\equiv\nabla\delta
on
\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}
, and so (
4.14
) gives us that

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\left\lvert\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\left\langle\beta,n\right\rangle\right\rvert\;d\sigma\leq C\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\delta\;d\sigma\equiv\varepsilon_{m}C\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;d\sigma.

Now if we set
\psi(t)\coloneqq t\int_{\Gamma_{r,t}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;d\sigma
, then we readily check from the co-area formula,

\displaystyle\fint_{\varepsilon/2}^{\varepsilon}\psi(t)\;dt\simeq\int_{\varepsilon/2}^{\varepsilon}\psi(t)\;\dfrac{dt}{t}\lesssim\int_{B_{r}\cap\{\varepsilon/2<\delta<\varepsilon\}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dX\rightarrow 0

as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
because
u\in W_{r}(B_{10})
. Hence there is a sequence
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
for which
\psi(\varepsilon_{m})\rightarrow 0
, which shows we may write such a limit as in (
4.28
) for an appropriate choice of
\varepsilon_{m}
. Alternatively, we could instead apply Lemma
A.3
to see that
\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert
is bounded in a small neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and thus for any
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
, the same holds true by the estimate above.

Now with the limit (
4.28
), we proceed with the computations as in (
4.20
), but with the domain
B_{r}
replaced by
\Omega_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}
for
\varepsilon_{m}
fixed and then pass to the limit. The estimates here remain exactly the same, since all integrals converge absolutely except for the one appearing in (
4.21
) where we integrate by parts again and need further justification. To deal with this term, we notice that in the corresponding computation (
4.21
) with
\Omega_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}
replacing
B_{r}
, integrating by parts yields a new boundary term, which we must justify vanishes in the limit as
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
:

\displaystyle\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla u,\beta\right\rangle\;d\sigma.

Here, we brutally estimate
\left\lvert\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\right\rvert\lesssim\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert
, and split the contributions

\displaystyle\left\lvert\left\langle\nabla u,\beta\right\rangle\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\nabla_{x}u\right\rvert\left\lvert X\right\rvert+\left\lvert\nabla_{t}u\right\rvert\left\lvert\left\langle\beta,\nabla\delta\right\rangle\right\rvert,

where we use that
\nabla\delta\equiv t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert
, to estimate again with, (
4.14
)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\left\langle\nabla u,\beta\right\rangle\;d\sigma\right\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert\left\lvert\nabla_{x}u\right\rvert\left\lvert X\right\rvert\;d\sigma+\varepsilon_{m}\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;d\sigma

\displaystyle\lesssim r\left(\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;d\sigma\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}u\right\rvert^{2}\;d\sigma\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\varepsilon_{m}\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;d\sigma\eqqcolon\phi(\varepsilon_{m})+\psi(\varepsilon_{m}).

We see as in the previous paragraph that
\psi(\varepsilon_{m})\downarrow 0
as
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
. On the other hand Lemma
A.3
says that
\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert
is bounded in a neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and thus we have the estimate

(4.29)

\displaystyle\phi(\varepsilon_{m})\leq C_{r,u}\left(\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}u\right\rvert^{2}\;d\sigma\right)^{1/2}.

Finally, we recall from Proposition
A.1
that
\nabla_{x}u\in W_{r}(B_{10})
with
\mathrm{Tr}(\nabla_{x}u)=0
on
\Gamma\cap B_{10}
, by the regularity assumptions on
A
, and the estimate (
A.1
) implies that for an appropriate choice of
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
, the right-hand side of (
4.29
) vanishes as
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
. Altogether, these estimates allow us to pass to the limit in the domain
\Omega_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}
and obtain the same estimate for
D^{\prime}(r)
as in (
4.22
).

To justify the equality (
4.23
), we use the same domain approximation, and write

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}}\divv(uA\nabla u)\;dX

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0}\int_{\Omega_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}\divv(uA\nabla u)\;dX

\displaystyle=\lim_{\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0}\int_{\partial B_{r}\cap\{\delta>\varepsilon_{m}\}}u\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma+\int_{\Gamma_{r,\varepsilon_{m}}}u\left\langle A\nabla u,n\right\rangle\;d\sigma.

The second integral above vanishes in the limit since Lemma
A.3
implies the decay of
u
away from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:
\left\lvert u\right\rvert\leq C_{u}\delta
, while
\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert
is bounded in a neighborhood of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

This brings us to the justification of our computation of
H^{\prime}(r)
, which consists of many similar ideas. In (
4.24
), one can argue that the differentiation in the integrand is allowed by the dominated convergence theorem, the Lipschitz nature of
\overline{\mu}
, and the estimates

(4.30)

\displaystyle\left\lvert u\right\rvert+\delta\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert\leq C_{u}\delta

from Lemma
A.3
. Indeed this estimate above gives that the integral
\int_{\partial B_{r}}\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert u\right\rvert\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert\;d\sigma
converges absolutely by Cauchy-Schwarz. What is left is again to justify the estimate (
4.26
), which one can argue again by truncating to the manifold with boundary
\partial B_{r}\cap\{\delta>\varepsilon_{m}\}
and passing to the limit as
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
. Here one need to take care of the error obtained from the boundary contribution, which is bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r}\cap\{\delta=\varepsilon_{m}\}}u^{2}\delta^{-n+d+1}d\mathcal{H}^{n-2}\leq C_{u}\varepsilon_{m}^{2-n+d+1}\varepsilon_{m}^{n-2}=C_{u}\varepsilon_{m}^{d+1},

by (
4.30
) and the fact that
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\partial B_{r}\cap\{\delta=\varepsilon_{m}\})\simeq\varepsilon_{m}^{n-2}
for
\varepsilon_{m}
small. The above estimate implies that these boundary integrals vanish as
\varepsilon_{m}\downarrow 0
, which concludes our sketch of justifications and thus the proof.
∎

In the case that
\mathcal{A}(0)\neq I
, then one can perform a straight-forward change of variables to reduce to the case of Lemma
4.6
, and so almost-monotonicity still holds in that case. Since we shall use this change of variables again in the future, let us set it aside as a remark.

Remark 4.7
.

Assume (
4.3
) and (
4.4
). Writing
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{A}(0)
, and
v(X)\coloneqq u(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)
, then
v
solves

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\divv(B\nabla v)&=0\text{ in }\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10},\\
v&=0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10},\end{aligned}\right.

with
B(X)\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}A(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}
. Moreover
B(X)
satisfies the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{10/\sqrt{\lambda}}
with constants
C\lambda,CC_{0}
for some dimensional constant
C>1
.

Proof.

The fact that
v
is a solution of the associated equation is a straight-forward computation using the definition of weak solution, so we omit the details. On the other hand, the asymptotic structure of
\mathcal{A}
from Definition
4.1
implies that
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2},\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}
map
\mathbb{R}^{d}
bijectively to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so that indeed
v
vanishes on
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
since
u
vanishes on
B_{10}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Finally, we may write
B(X)=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}\mathcal{A}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}
and use the structure of
\mathcal{A}
at the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{d}
again as in Definition
4.1
to conclude that
B
satisfies the higher co-dimension condition in
B_{10/\sqrt{\lambda}}\subset\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10}
, since
\mathcal{A}
is Lipschitz.
∎

At this stage, it will make life easier to instead assume (
4.5
) with
\eta
sufficiently small instead of requiring ourselves to shrink to a smaller scale, though the two are essentially equivalent.

Corollary 4.8
.

Assume (
4.3
) and (
4.5
). Then as long as
\eta
sufficiently small, there is a constant
C=C(n,d)>0
so that
N(r)e^{Cr}
is monotone increasing where it is defined (so in particular, on the interval
(0,10(1-\eta))
).

Proof.

By Remark
4.7
, we know for
v(X)\coloneqq u(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)
that

\left\{\begin{aligned} -\divv(B\nabla v)&=0\text{ in }\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10},\\
v&=0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10},\end{aligned}\right.

where
B
is the matrix defined by
B(X)\coloneqq\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}A(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}
. Since
A
satisfies the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{10}
with constants bounded constants by (
4.6
),
B(X)=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{B}(X)
satisfies the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{10(1-\eta)}
with bounded constants. Moreover, by construction we have that
\mathcal{B}(0)=I
, and so Lemma
4.6
applies to
v
to give almost monotonicity of the function

\displaystyle r\mapsto\dfrac{r\int_{B_{r}}\left\langle B\nabla v,\nabla v\right\rangle\;dX}{\int_{\partial B_{r}}\mu_{B}v^{2}\;d\sigma},\qquad\mu_{B}(X)\coloneqq\left\langle B\dfrac{X}{\left\lvert X\right\rvert},\dfrac{X}{\left\lvert X\right\rvert}\right\rangle.

Changing variables through the map
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}
(using the coarea formula to compute the Jacobian for the change of variables over
\partial B_{r}
), we for any
f\in C(B_{r})
that

(4.31)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r}}f(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)\;d\sigma(X)

\displaystyle=\det\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}\int_{\partial E_{r}}f(Y)\dfrac{r}{\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}Y\right\rvert}\;d\sigma(Y).

In particular, we have the equalities
compute that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}}\left\langle B\nabla v,\nabla v\right\rangle\,dX

\displaystyle=\det\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}\int_{E_{r}}\left\langle A\nabla u,\nabla u\right\rangle\,dX,

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r}}\mu_{B}v^{2}\;d\sigma

\displaystyle=\det\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}\int_{\partial E_{r}}\mu_{B}(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}X)u(X)^{2}\dfrac{r}{\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}X\right\rvert}\;d\sigma(X),

which concludes the proof since

\displaystyle B(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}X)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}A(X)\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}.

∎

Remark 4.9
.

From (
4.27
) and (
4.23
) in the proof of Lemma
4.6
we see that the following identity holds:

\displaystyle\dfrac{H^{\prime}(r)}{H(r)}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{d}{r}+\dfrac{2D(r)}{H(r)}+O(1)

(4.32)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{d}{r}+\dfrac{2N(r)}{r}+O(1).

In particular, this implies

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{dr}\log(H(r)r^{-d})=\dfrac{2N(r)}{r}+O(1).

Along with the almost-monotonicity of
N(r)
from Lemma
4.6
, we see from a straight-forward estimate integrating
\tfrac{d}{dr}\left(\log(H(r)r^{-d})\right)
that whenever
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<10(1-\eta)
,

\displaystyle r_{2}^{-d}H(r_{2})\leq Cr_{1}^{-d}H(r_{1})\left(\dfrac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\right)^{CN(r_{2})}

with constant
C
depending only on the dimensions
n
and
d
.

In view of the preceding Remark, standard arguments as in
[
HL
, Theorem 3.1.3]
,
[
NV17b
, Theorem 4.6]
(along with the fact that
\mu(X)\simeq_{\lambda}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}
and
r/\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1}Y\right\rvert\simeq_{\lambda}1
on
\partial E_{r}(X_{0})
) give the following corollaries.

Corollary 4.10

(Doubling inequalities on the boundary)
.

Assume
\eta
is sufficiently small. Then there is some constant
C>1
for which we have the following doubling inequalities whenever
E_{2r}\subset B_{10}
(and in particular, whenever
0<2r<10(1-2\eta)
)

\displaystyle C^{N(r)-1}\fint_{\partial E_{r}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}u^{2}d\sigma\leq\fint_{\partial E_{2r}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}u^{2}\;d\sigma

\displaystyle\leq C^{N(2r)+1}\fint_{\partial E_{r}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}u^{2}\;d\sigma,

\displaystyle C^{N(r)-1}\fint_{E_{r}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}u^{2}dX\leq\fint_{E_{2r}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}u^{2}\;dX

\displaystyle\leq C^{N(2r)+1}\fint_{E_{r}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}u^{2}\;dX.

Moreover, by taking
\eta
smaller, we can also assume that same inequalities hold with
B_{2r}
and
B_{r}
in place of
E_{2r}
and
E_{r}
.

Corollary 4.11

(Uniform control on the boundary)
.

Assume
\eta
is sufficiently small. Then there is a
C=C(n,d)>0
so that for all
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{2}
, and all
0<r<2
, one has

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,r)\leq CN_{u}(0,10(1-\eta)).

In addition, for each
\varepsilon>0
, there is
\delta>0
so that if
\eta
is small enough, then

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,r)\leq(1+\varepsilon)N_{u}(0,10(1-\eta)),\;Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{\delta},\;\;0<r<2.

Just as in the case of harmonic functions, we have stronger rigidity results for the frequency function when considering solutions of (
2.1
). A careful evaluation of the proof of Lemma
4.6
shows that if
\mathcal{A}(X)=I
(i.e., in the setting of the equation (
2.1
)), then there are no error terms involving
\nabla\mathcal{A}
, and we have true monotonicity,
\tfrac{d}{dr}N(r)\geq 0
. We summarize these findings in the following Lemma, since we shall later use these more precise estimates for homogeneous solutions.

Lemma 4.12

(Almgren monotonicity, flat space)
.

Suppose that
u\in W_{r}(B_{R})
is a solution to (
2.1
) in
B_{R}
, and that u vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{R}
.
Then the following hold:

(1)

N(r)
is non-decreasing for
r\in(0,R)
,

(2)

\dfrac{d}{dr}\log(r^{-d}H(r))=\dfrac{2N(r)}{r}
, so that for
0<r_{1}<r_{2}<R
,

\displaystyle\left(\dfrac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\right)^{2N(r_{1})}\leq\dfrac{r_{2}^{-d}H(r_{2})}{r_{1}^{-d}H(r_{1})}\leq\left(\dfrac{r_{2}}{r_{1}}\right)^{2N(r_{2})},

(3)

N(r)\equiv\Lambda
on a non-trivial interval
I\subset(0,R)
if and only if
u
is
\Lambda
-homogeneous.

In particular,
N(0^{+})=\lim_{r\downarrow 0}N(r)
exists.

Finally, since we shall frequently use compactness arguments to shorten our exposition, let us introduce the following Lemma which says that solutions to equations of the type (
4.3
) with bounded frequency are a compact class. Since the arguments involved in the proof of this Lemma are straight-forward (once one recalls the Lipschitz estimates for solutions of (
4.3
) guaranteed by Lemma
A.3
), we omit the details.

Lemma 4.13

(Compactness of solutions with bounded frequency)
.

Assume
\eta
is sufficiently small and fix
\Lambda_{0}>0
. Suppose that
A_{m}
are a sequence of matrices satisfying the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{8R_{m}}
with constants
1+\eta,\eta
, and that the
R_{m}
are non-decreasing. Assume also that
u_{m}\in W_{r}(B_{8R_{m}})\cap C(B_{8R_{m}})
are a sequence of functions such that

(1)

-\divv(A_{m}\nabla u_{m})=0
in
B_{8R_{m}}
,

(2)

u_{m}
vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{8R_{m}}
,

(3)

N_{u_{m}}^{A_{m}}(0,r)\leq\Lambda_{0}
for
0<r\leq 5R_{m}
, and
H_{u_{m}}^{A_{m}}(0,R_{1})=1
,

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Then with
R_{\infty}=\liminf_{m\rightarrow\infty}R_{m}\in[R_{1},\infty]
, there is a subsequence
m_{k}\rightarrow\infty
, a matrix
A_{\infty}
satisfying the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition with constants
1+\eta,\eta
in
B_{5R_{\infty}}
, and a solution
u_{\infty}\in W_{r}(B_{5R_{\infty}})
so that

(1)

\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d-1}A_{m}\rightarrow\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d-1}A
uniformly on compact subsets of
B_{5R_{\infty}}
,

(2)

u_{m_{k}}\rightarrow u_{\infty}
uniformly on compact subsets of
B_{5R_{\infty}}
,

(3)

-\divv(A_{\infty}u_{\infty})=0
in
B_{5R_{\infty}}
,

(4)

u_{\infty}
vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{5R_{\infty}}
,

(5)

N_{u_{m}}^{A_{m}}(0,s)\rightarrow N_{u_{\infty}}^{A_{\infty}}(0,s)
as
m\rightarrow\infty
for
s\in(0,5R_{\infty})
, and

(6)

H_{u_{\infty}}^{A_{\infty}}(0,R_{1})=1
, and
N_{u_{\infty}}^{A_{\infty}}(0,r)\leq C\Lambda_{0}
for all
r\leq 5R_{\infty}
.

As a first consequence of Lemma
4.13
, let us demonstrate how one can deduce doubling inequalities for solutions
u

off
of the boundary at sufficiently small scales, assuming only a bound on
N_{u}
in a ball centered on the boundary. Importantly, this estimate is
not
contained in the conclusion of Corollaries
4.10
,
4.11
since these two results only give doubling information about the solution
u
on ellipses centered on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma 4.14
.

Assume
\eta
is sufficiently small. For each
\Lambda_{0}>1
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
, there is a
C_{1}=C_{1}(\Lambda_{0},\gamma)>1
so that the following holds. Whenever
u
is a solution of (
4.3
) with (
4.5
) and
N_{u}(0,10(1-\eta))\leq\Lambda_{0}
, then for any
Z\in B_{2}
, we have

(4.33)

\displaystyle\sup_{E_{\gamma}(Z)}\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{2}\geq C_{1}^{-1}\fint_{\partial E_{1}}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w},

and the doubling inequalities

(4.34)

\displaystyle\fint_{\partial E_{2\gamma}(Z)}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\leq C_{1}\fint_{\partial E_{\gamma}(Z)}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w},\qquad\fint_{E_{2\gamma}(Z)}u^{2}\;dm\leq C_{1}\fint_{E_{\gamma}(Z)}u^{2}\;dm.

Moreover, each of the following quantities are comparable (with constant
C_{1}
) to
\fint_{\partial E_{1}}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}
:

(4.35)

\displaystyle\sup_{E_{\gamma}(Z)}\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{2},\;\fint_{\partial E_{\gamma}(Z)}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w},\;\fint_{E_{\gamma}(Z)}u^{2}\;dm.

Proof.

We argue by contradiction and compactness. If the Lemma does not hold, then there is a choice of
\Lambda_{0}>1
, and
\gamma\in(0,1)
, for which one can find a contradicting sequence of matrices
A_{m}
, solutions
u_{m}
, and points
Z_{m}\in B_{2}
satisfying the hypothesis of the Lemma but which fail the inequality (
4.33
). Hence, after normalizing by a multiplicative constant so that
H_{u_{m}}^{A_{m}}(0,1)=1
, we have

(4.36)

\displaystyle\sup_{E_{\gamma}(Z_{m})}\left\lvert u_{m}\right\rvert^{2}\leq Cm^{-1}\fint_{\partial E_{1}}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}u_{m}^{2}\;d\sigma=Cm^{-1}.

By Corollary
4.8
, we have for each
m
that
N_{u_{m}}^{A_{m}}(0,r)\leq C\Lambda_{0}
for
r\leq 10(1-\eta)
, and thus we are in the context to apply Lemma
4.13
with
R_{m}\equiv 1
for all
m
as long as
\eta
is chosen small enough. Hence we may extract a subsequence (which we still label
u_{m}
for convenience) for which
u_{m}\rightarrow u
locally uniformly in
B_{5}
to some non-trivial solution
u
of an equation of the type (
4.3
) in
B_{5}
with corresponding matrix
A
which satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
in this same ball. In view of the inequality (
4.36
), we see that the solution
u
must vanish identically on some open subset of
B_{4}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d})
. However, since
u
satisfies a uniformly elliptic equation with Lipschitz coefficients locally in
B_{5}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d})
, this contradicts the strong unique continuation principle for such solutions
[
GL86
]
, so that the first claim is proved.

The doubling claim (
4.34
) and the comparability of the quantities in (
4.35
) follows from essentially the same compactness argument, so we omit the details.
∎

The following Corollary is proved in a similar way, using the fact that for solutions
u
of (
2.1
),
N_{u}(r)
is scale-invariant in the sense that whenever
u_{r}\coloneqq u(r\,\cdot\,)
, then
N_{u_{r}}(s)=N_{u}(sr)
. While it’s true that we can prove a much stronger statement (namely, that the limit of the following Corollary is unique), for our current purposes, and for the sake of brevity, we only need the existence of tangent maps in the following sense.

Corollary 4.15
.

Let
u\in W_{r}(B_{R})
be a solution to (
2.1
) which vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{R}
. Then for each sequence of scales
r_{m}\downarrow 0
, there is a subsequence
r_{m_{k}}\downarrow 0
so that for
\{u_{m_{k}}\}
defined by

\displaystyle u_{m_{k}}\coloneqq\dfrac{v_{m_{k}}}{\left(\int_{\partial B_{1}}v_{m_{k}}^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\right)^{1/2}},\;\;v_{m_{k}}\coloneqq u(r_{m_{k}}\,\cdot\,),

one has
\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}u_{m_{k}}=v
for some
(N_{u}(0^{+}))
-homogeneous solution
v
of (
2.1
) which vanishes continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here the limit holds in uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and thus also in
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\sigma_{w})
.

4.2.
Monotonicity formulae off the boundary

One important aspect of the definition of the frequency function
N_{u}(Y,r)
as in (
4.8
) is that when
Y\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
r\ll\delta(Y)
, it coincides with the generalized frequency function introduced by Garofalo and Lin in
[
GL86
]
(and later by Naber and Valtorta in
[
NV17b
]
) to study unique continuation properties of solutions to elliptic, divergence form equations with Lipschitz coefficients. In particular, we may use the following rescaling trick to see that inside Whitney regions for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we may always fall back on this now well-understood theory.

Remark 4.16
.

Assume (
4.3
) and (
4.5
) with
\eta
sufficiently small. Then for
Y\in B_{2}
and
\rho\coloneqq\delta(Y)/4
, we have by linearity that
v(X)\coloneqq u(Y+\rho X)
solves the equation

\displaystyle-\divv(\rho^{n-d-1}\tilde{A}\nabla v)=0\text{ in }B_{2},

where
\tilde{A}(X)=A(Y+\rho X)
(note that
X\in B_{2}
implies that
Y+\rho X\in B_{\delta(Y)/2}(Y)
). Moreover, it is straight-forward to verify that
\rho^{n-d-1}\tilde{A}
is uniformly elliptic with constant at most
C\eta
and Lipschitz with constant at most
C\eta
. Applying the results of
[
GL86
]
and
[
NV17b
]
to the solution
v
then gives us the following estimates in Whitney scales.

Lemma 4.17

(Almost monotonicity for Whitney scales)
.

As long as
\eta
is sufficiently small, there exists
C=C(n,d)>0
so that
e^{Cr}N_{u}(Y,r)
is monotone increasing in
r
for
r\in(0,\delta(Y)/4)
and
Y\in B_{2}
.

Theorem 4.18

(Whitney covering estimates)
.

Let
Y\in B_{2}
and suppose that
B_{2r}(Y)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then if
\eta
is sufficiently small, then there exists a constant
C=C(n,d)
so that
N_{u}(Z,t)\leq\Lambda_{0}
for
Z\in B_{r}(Y)
and
0<t\leq r
implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}\left(B_{s}(\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{r/2}(Y)\right)\leq C^{\Lambda_{0}^{2}}(s/r)^{2},\;\;s<r/2.

4.3.
The behavior of
N
through the boundary

With the almost-monotonicity properties of
N_{u}(X_{0},r)
established for
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
or
X_{0}\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
but
r\ll\delta(X_{0})
, we now investigate the behavior of
N_{u}(X_{0},r)
“through” the boundary, i.e., the behavior of
N_{u}(X_{0},r)
for when
X_{0}\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
yet
B_{r}(X_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\neq\varnothing
. Our first result below says that assuming a bound on the frequency of the solution
u
at some top scale, the values of
N_{u}(X_{0},r)
and
N_{u}(\pi(X_{0}),r)
are sufficiently close for
r\gg\delta(X_{0})
.

Lemma 4.19

(Boundary hopping)
.

Assume (
4.3
) and (
4.5
) with
\eta
sufficiently small. Suppose in addition that
N_{u}(0,10(1-\eta))\leq\Lambda_{0}
for some
\Lambda_{0}>1
. Then there exists
C_{1}=C_{1}(\Lambda_{0})>0
so that whenever
X_{0}\in B_{10^{-1}}
, and
r\in(3\delta(X_{0}),10^{-1})

(4.37)

\displaystyle e^{-C_{1}\delta(X_{0})/r}\leq\dfrac{H_{u}(X_{0},r)}{H_{u}(\pi(X_{0}),r+2\delta(X_{0}))},\dfrac{H_{u}(X_{0},r)}{H_{u}(\pi(X_{0}),r-2\delta(X_{0}))}\leq e^{C_{1}\delta(X_{0})/r}.

In particular, if
r=\gamma\delta(X_{0})<10^{-1}
for
\gamma>3
, then

(4.38)

\displaystyle e^{-C_{1}/\gamma}N_{u}(\pi(X_{0}),(\gamma-2)\delta(X_{0}))\leq N_{u}(X_{0},\gamma\delta(X_{0}))\leq e^{C_{1}/\gamma}N_{u}(\pi(X_{0}),(\gamma+2)\delta(X_{0})).

Proof.

First, let us fix
X_{0}\in B_{10^{-1}}
,
r\in(3\delta(X_{0}),10^{-1})
, and normalize
u
so that
\sup_{E_{2({r+\delta(X_{0})})}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert=1
. Define for
X\in[\pi(X_{0}),X_{0}]
(the segment connecting
\pi(X_{0})
to
X_{0}
), the radii
r(X)=r+2\left\lvert X-X_{0}\right\rvert
. Our goal is to explicitly estimate

(4.39)

\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\log(H_{u}(X,r(X)))\right\rvert\leq Cr(X)^{-1},\;\;X\in[\pi(X_{0}),X_{0}]

in order to obtain the first claimed inequality. This is largely computational, so let us sketch the main steps.

By the choice of the normalization of
u
and Lemma
4.14
, we see that if
\eta
is sufficiently small, then there is a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\Lambda_{0})>1
for which
\fint_{\partial E_{r(X)}(X)}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\simeq_{C_{1}}1
, or in other words,

(4.40)

\displaystyle C_{1}^{-1}\leq r(X)^{-d}\int_{\partial E_{r(X)}(X)}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\leq C_{1},\;X\in[\pi(X_{0}),X_{0}].

Next, we remark as in the change of variables from (
4.31
) that we may rewrite

\displaystyle H_{u}(X,r)\equiv\det\mathcal{A}(X)^{1/2}r^{n-1}\int_{\partial B_{1}}u(\tilde{Y})^{2}\delta(\tilde{Y})^{-n+d+1}\tilde{\mu}(Y)\;d\sigma(Y)

where we define
\tilde{Y}\coloneqq X+r\mathcal{A}(X)^{1/2}Y\in\partial E_{r}(X)
whenever
Y\in\partial B_{1}
and
\tilde{\mu}(Y)\simeq 1
is the Lipschitz function defined by

(4.41)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mu}(Y)\coloneqq\left\langle\mathcal{A}(\tilde{Y})\mathcal{A}(X)^{-1/2}Y,\mathcal{A}(X)^{-1/2}Y\right\rangle.

Indeed
\tilde{\mu}(Y)
is Lipschitz since
\mathcal{A}
and
\tilde{Y}
are. In fact, for the latter, we have the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{X}\tilde{Y}-I\right\rvert\leq Cr\left\lvert Y\right\rvert\leq Cr.

With this change of variables, we then may rewrite

\displaystyle H_{u}(X,r(X))\equiv\det\mathcal{A}(X)^{1/2}r(X)^{n-1}\int_{\partial B_{1}}u(\tilde{Y})^{2}\delta(\tilde{Y})^{-n+d+1}\tilde{\mu}(Y)\;d\sigma(Y),

and so computing
\nabla_{X}H_{u}(X,r(X))
becomes a routine application of the product rule. It is easy to see that the contribution coming from the terms where
\nabla_{X}
is applied to
\det\mathcal{A}(X)^{1/2}
,
r(X)^{n-1}
, or
\tilde{\mu}(Y)
are bounded in absolute value by

\displaystyle CH_{u}(X,r(X)),CH_{u}(X,r(X))/r(X),CH_{u}(X,r(X)),

respectively, and so since
r(X)<C
, to prove (
4.39
) we need only to consider the terms where
\nabla_{X}
is applied to
u(\tilde{Y})^{2}
or
\delta(\tilde{Y})^{-n+d+1}
.

As for the first term involving
\nabla_{X}(u(\tilde{Y})^{2})
, we estimate using the Lipschitz nature of
\tilde{Y}
and the gradient estimate Lemma
A.3
to see

\displaystyle\left|\det\mathcal{A}(X)^{1/2}r(X)^{n-1}\int_{\partial B_{1}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.2u(\tilde{Y})\nabla u(\tilde{Y})\nabla\tilde{Y}\delta(\tilde{Y})^{-n+d+1}\tilde{\mu}(Y)\;d\sigma(Y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Cr(X)^{n-1}\int_{\partial B_{1}}\delta{(\tilde{Y})}^{-n+d+1}\;d\sigma(Y)\leq Cr(X)^{d}\leq CC_{1}H_{u}(X,r(X))

by virtue of (
4.40
). As for the second term involving
\nabla_{X}(\delta(\tilde{Y})^{-n+d+1})
, one argues essentially in the same way with Lemma
A.3
and the fact that
\left\lvert u(Y)\right\rvert\leq C\delta(Y)
for
Y\in E_{2(r+\delta(X))}(X)
to conclude that

\displaystyle\left|\det\mathcal{A}(X)^{1/2}r(X)^{n-1}\int_{\partial B_{1}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.u(\tilde{Y})^{2}\nabla(\delta(\tilde{Y})^{-n+d+1})\tilde{\mu}(Y)\;d\sigma(Y)\right|

\displaystyle\leq Cr(X)^{n-1}\int_{\partial B_{1}}u^{2}\delta{(\tilde{Y})}^{-n+d}\;d\sigma(Y)\leq Cr(X)^{d}\leq CC_{1}H_{u}(X,r(X))

as well, which altogether completes our proof of the estimate (
4.39
). Integrating this inequality over the segment
[\pi(X_{0}),X_{0})]
, one readily obtains

\displaystyle\left\lvert\log\left(\dfrac{H_{u}(X_{0},r(X_{0}))}{H_{u}(\pi(X_{0}),r(\pi(X_{0}))}\right)\right\rvert\leq CC_{1}\delta(X_{0})/r,

which is the first desired estimate in (
4.37
), while the other is obtained in essentially the same manner where we instead define
r(X)\coloneqq r-2\left\lvert X-X_{0}\right\rvert
.

In order to obtain the estimate (
4.38
), we simply recall the definition of
N_{u}
as in (
4.8
), apply (
4.37
) and the containment

\displaystyle E_{r+2\delta(X_{0})}(\pi(X_{0}))\supset E_{r}(X_{0}),\;\;E_{r-2\pi(X_{0})}(\pi(X_{0}))\subset E_{r}(X_{0})

which holds if
\eta
is sufficiently small.
Indeed if
Y\in E_{r}(X_{0})
, then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(\pi(X_{0}))^{-1/2}(Y-\pi(X_{0}))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(x_{0})^{-1/2}(Y-X_{0})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\left(\mathcal{A}(x_{0})^{-1/2}-\mathcal{A}(\pi(X_{0}))^{-1/2}\right)(Y-X_{0})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(\pi(X_{0}))^{-1/2}(X_{0}-\pi(X_{0}))\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq r+C\eta\delta(X_{0})+(1+C\eta)\delta(X_{0})\leq r+2\delta(X_{0}),

by courtesy of (
4.5
), while the other containment is proved in essentially the same manner.
∎

Combining the previous Lemma with the doubling inequalities in Lemma
4.14
as well as Corollary
4.11
, we may now conclude uniform boundedness of the frequency
N_{u}
in all small balls near the origin assuming only a bound on
N_{u}(0,10(1-\eta))
.

Corollary 4.20
.

Suppose (
4.3
) and (
4.5
) with
\eta
is sufficiently small, and fix
\Lambda_{0}>1
. Then there is a
C_{1}=C_{1}(\Lambda_{0})>0
so that
N_{u}(0,10(1-\eta))\leq\Lambda_{0}
implies
N_{u}(Y,r)\leq C_{1}
for all
Y\in B_{10^{-2}}
and
0<r<10^{-2}
.

Proof.

Let
Y\in B_{10^{-2}}
, and notice that for all
r>0
of the form
r=\gamma\delta(Y)\in(3\delta(Y),10^{-1})
with
\gamma>3
, we have from Lemma
4.19
and Corollary
4.11

(4.42)

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,r)\leq e^{C_{1}}N_{u}(\pi(Y),(\gamma+2)\delta(Y))\leq e^{C_{1}}C\Lambda_{0},

for some constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(\Lambda_{0})
. As for the range
r\in(4^{-1}\delta(Y),4\delta(Y))
, we use Lemma
4.14
and
E_{r}(Y)\subset E_{16r}(Y)
to see that

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,r)\leq\dfrac{Cr\int_{E_{r}(Y)}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm}{\int_{\partial E_{r}(Y)}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}}\leq\dfrac{CC_{1}r\int_{E_{16r}(Y)}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm}{\int_{\partial E_{16r}(Y)}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}}\leq CC_{1}N_{u}(Y,16r)\leq C(\Lambda_{0}),

since
16r>3\delta(Y)
, so that (
4.42
) applies. Finally, when
r\in(0,\delta(Y)/4)
, we apply the monotonicity Lemma
4.17
in Whitney regions to conclude

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,r)\leq CN_{u}(Y,\delta(Y)/4),

which concludes the proof.
∎

In view of the previous Lemma, in our efforts towards proving Theorem
1.1
we now may frequently use in addition to (
4.3
) and (
4.5
) that
u
is a solution for which

(4.43)

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,r)\leq\Lambda_{0},\;\text{ whenever }E_{r}(Y)\subset B_{10}.

The important piece of (
4.43
) is that we have a uniform estimate on
N_{u}(Y,r)
for
Y\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is not guaranteed initially by Corollary
4.11
.

5.
Frequency pinching, cone conditions, and quantitative approximations

In this section, we compile a list of results which say that when
u
is a solution whose frequency
N_{u}(X_{0},r)
is almost constant on a sufficiently large interval, and
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the singular set of
u
lives in conical neighborhood of an
(n-2)
-plane. This containment in a cone is an essential part of our main covering argument Lemma
6.3
in the next section. Before our results, let us introduce two useful definitions.

Definition 5.1
.

Given a non-constant solution
u
of (
4.3
), we say that its frequency is
\varepsilon
-pinched about
\Lambda
at
X_{0}\in B_{1}
in the scales
[r_{1},r_{2}]
if for all
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lvert N_{u}(X_{0},r)-\Lambda\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon.

In what follows, whenever
0\not\equiv f\in L^{\infty}(B_{r}(X_{0}))
, define

f_{r}^{X_{0}}(Y)\coloneqq f(X_{0}+rY)\left\lVert f(X_{0}+r\;\cdot)\right\rVert^{-1}_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})},

so that
f_{r}^{X_{0}}\in L^{\infty}(B_{1})
with
\left\lVert f_{r}^{X_{0}}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}=1
. With this notation we introduce a normalized distance to
H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
as follows.

Definition 5.2
.

When
0\not\equiv f\in L^{\infty}(B_{r}(X_{0}))
, we define the normalized
L^{\infty}
distance of
f
to
H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
in
B_{r}(X_{0})
by

\displaystyle\dist_{r}^{X_{0}}(f,H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda})\coloneqq\inf_{u\in H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}}\left\lVert f_{r}^{X_{0}}-u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})},

and the normalized
L^{\infty}
distance of
f
to
H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}
over the interval
[r_{1},r_{2}]
by

\displaystyle\dist^{X_{0}}_{r_{1},r_{2}}(f,H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda})\coloneqq\inf_{u\in H_{\Lambda}^{\lambda}}\sup_{s\in[r_{1},r_{2}]}\left\lVert f_{s}^{X_{0}}-u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}.

The expectation is that if a solution is pinched around
\Lambda
in an interval then it should grow approximately like a
\Lambda
-homogeneous function for scales in that interval. Our next Lemma confirms this as long as the solution is pinched around a point in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Lemma 5.3

(Pinching specifies growth)
.

Assume that
u
is a solution of (
4.3
), and assume in addition (
4.5
) and (
4.43
). Then there exists a constant
C=C(\lambda,C_{0})>1
so that if
u
is
\varepsilon
-pinched about
\Lambda
at
X_{0}\in B_{5}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the scales
[r_{1},r_{2}]
with
r_{2}<1
, then for
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
,

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left(\dfrac{r}{r_{2}}\right)^{2(\Lambda+\varepsilon)}H_{u}(X_{0},r_{2})r_{2}^{-d}\leq H_{u}(X_{0},r)r^{-d}\leq C\left(\dfrac{r}{r_{2}}\right)^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}H_{u}(X_{0},r_{2})r_{2}^{-d}.

In addition, for all
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
so that
2r<r_{2}
, we have the gradient estimate

\displaystyle r^{2}\fint_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq C\left(H_{u}(X_{0},r_{2})r_{2}^{-d}\right)\left(\dfrac{2r}{r_{2}}\right)^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}

Proof.

To ease notation, we write
H(r)=H_{u}(X_{0},r)
and
N(r)=N_{u}(X_{0},r)
. By Remark
4.9
we see that

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{dr}\log(H(r)r^{-d})=\dfrac{2N(r)}{r}+O(1),

with implicit constant depending only on
C_{0}
,
\lambda
, and the dimension. Integrating this inequality from
r
to
r_{2}
then gives for any
r_{1}<r<r_{2}
,

\displaystyle 2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)\log(r_{2}/r)-C\leq\log\left(\dfrac{H(r_{2})(r_{2})^{-d}}{H(r)r^{-d}}\right)\leq 2(\Lambda+\varepsilon)\log(r_{2}/r)+C

from which one easily deduces the first pair of desired inequalities.

As for the second estimate, notice that since
\tfrac{d}{dr}\log(H(r)r^{-d})\geq-C
, by Remark
4.9
, the same computation as above yields the estimate

\displaystyle H(t)\leq C(t/r)^{d}H(r),\;\;0<t<r<r_{2}.

In particular, given
r\in[r_{1},r_{2}]
with
2r<r_{2}
, we can estimate using the fact that
\int_{\partial B_{t}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\simeq H(t)
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2r}\int_{\partial B_{t}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}dt\leq C\int_{0}^{2r}\int_{\partial B_{2r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}dt\leq CrH(2r),

or in other words,

\displaystyle\fint_{B_{2r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;dm\leq CH(2r)(2r)^{-d},

since
m(B_{t}(X_{0}))\simeq t^{d+1}
and
\sigma_{w}(\partial B_{t}(X_{0}))\simeq t^{d}
for all
t>0
. Caccioppoli’s inequality along with the first estimate of the Lemma then yields the second:

\displaystyle r^{2}\fint_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq C\fint_{B_{2r}(X_{0})}u^{2}\;dm\leq CH(2r)(2r)^{-d}\leq Cr^{-2}\left(H(r_{2})r_{2}^{-d}\right)(2r/r_{2})^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}.

∎

For points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
we can say a little bit more, namely that if the frequency of
u
is pinched between scales, then
u
is close to some homogeneous solution of the “constant coefficient equation” in a slightly smaller range of scales. This is an analogue of
[
CNV15
, Theorem 3.12]
, though the richness of the space of homogeneous solutions in our setting adds additional complexity to the proof. Note that it will be more convenient for us to measure closeness in the
L^{\infty}
-sense. We do this below in Lemma
5.5
.

Lemma 5.4

(Approximation by homogeneous solutions)
.

For each
\tau\in(0,1)
small enough depending on
\lambda,n,d
and
\Lambda_{0}>1
, there exists
\gamma=\gamma(\tau,\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\Lambda,\tau)\in(0,1)
small enough so that the following holds.

Assume (
4.3
), (
4.5
), (
4.43
) and that
u
is
\varepsilon
-pinched about
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
,
\Lambda\leq\Lambda_{0}
at
X_{0}\in B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the scales
[r_{1},1]
for
r_{1}<100^{-1}
. Assume in addition that
\mathcal{A}(X_{0})=I
. Then for
\eta
chosen small enough, depending on
\tau
and
\Lambda_{0}
, and
M
chosen large enough (depending on
\tau
) there exists a
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution
w_{0}
of the “constant-coefficient equation” (
2.1
) for which

\displaystyle\fint_{\partial B_{r}}(u-w_{0})^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\leq\tau^{1/2}\fint_{\partial B_{r}}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w},\;\;r\in[Mr_{1},\gamma].

Proof.

To ease notation, we assume
X_{0}=0
and write
H(s)=H_{u}(0,s)
. In addition, since the conclusion of the Lemma is invariant under multiplication of
u
by constants, we normalize
u
so that
H(1)=H_{u}(0,1)=1
. Since
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
, choose
i\in\mathbb{N}
so that
\Lambda=\Lambda_{i}
. As per Remark
A.8
, we may write
u(r,\theta)
in polar coordinates as

\displaystyle u(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(r)\phi_{j}^{k}(\theta),

where the
\phi_{j}^{k}
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\sigma_{w})
and
a_{j}^{k}(r)\in\mathbb{R}
. With this notation, we easily see that

\displaystyle s^{-d}H(s)\simeq_{\lambda}\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s)^{2},\;\;s\in[r_{1},1],

since the conformal factor
\mu
defining
H(r)
(recall Definition
4.3
) is bounded above and below by a constant depending only on the ellipticity constant of
\mathcal{A}=\delta^{n-d-1}A
.

First, we fix
r_{1}<s_{0}<1/2
, and let
v
be the solution to the boundary value problem

(5.1)

\displaystyle-\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\nabla v)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ in }B_{s_{0}}\cap\Omega,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=u

\displaystyle\text{ on }\partial(B_{s_{0}}\setminus\Gamma).

Assuming the representation of
u
as in the statement of the Lemma, a straightforward computation gives us the explicit representation for
v
:

(5.2)

\displaystyle v(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s_{0})(r/s_{0})^{\Lambda_{k}}\phi_{j}^{k}(\theta),

simply because
v
has boundary data
u
on
\partial B_{s_{0}}
, and each term in the sum above is a (homogeneous) solution of (
5.1
). Our goal now is to show that
s\mapsto a_{j}^{k}(s)
grows like
(s/s_{0})^{\Lambda_{k}}
. This will quickly imply, given a good choice of
s_{0}
, that a solution like
v
above will satisfy the conclusion of Lemma
5.4
.

To this end, we estimate the error
u-v
using Lemma
A.4
followed by Lemma
5.3
to obtain

(5.3)

\displaystyle\fint_{B_{s_{0}}}\left\lvert\nabla(u-v)\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq C\eta s_{0}\fint_{B_{s_{0}}}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq C\eta s_{0}^{-1}(2s_{0})^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}.

On the other hand, we can estimate this difference from below using the orthogonality of the basis
\phi_{j}^{k}
in
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\sigma_{w})
. Indeed, letting
\partial_{r}
denote the radial derivative, fixing
\gamma\in(0,1)
, and writing
b_{j}^{k}(r)=a_{j}^{k}(s_{0})(r/s_{0})^{\Lambda_{k}}
, we have

(5.4)

\begin{split}\fint_{B_{s_{0}}}\left\lvert\nabla(u-v)\right\rvert^{2}\;dm&\geq\fint_{B_{s_{0}}}\left(\partial_{r}(u-v)\right)^{2}\;dm\\
&\geq C^{-1}s_{0}^{-d-1}\int_{\gamma s_{0}}^{s_{0}}\int_{\partial B_{t}}\left(\partial_{r}(u-v)\right)^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}dt\\
&\geq C^{-1}s_{0}^{-d-1}\int_{\gamma s_{0}}^{s_{0}}t^{d}\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\left((a_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)-(b_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)\right)^{2}\;dt\\
&\geq C_{\gamma}^{-1}s_{0}^{-1}\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\int_{\gamma s_{0}}^{s_{0}}\left((a_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)-(b_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)\right)^{2}\;dt,\end{split}

where
C_{\gamma}
now is a constant depending on
\gamma
.

Combining both of our estimates and using that
b_{j}^{k}(s_{0})=a_{j}^{k}(s_{0})
, we can estimate

\displaystyle\left\lvert a_{j}^{k}(\gamma s_{0})-b_{j}^{k}(\gamma s_{0})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\gamma s_{0}}^{s_{0}}\left\lvert(a_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)-(b_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)\right\rvert\;dt\leq Cs_{0}^{1/2}\left(\int_{\gamma s_{0}}^{s_{0}}\left((a_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)-(b_{j}^{k})^{\prime}(t)\right)^{2}\;dt\right)^{1/2}.

Squaring the above inequality, summing over all indices, and using (
5.4
), (
5.3
), and the definition of
b_{j}^{k}
gives us that

(5.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\left(a_{j}^{k}(\gamma s_{0})-\gamma^{\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s_{0})\right)^{2}\leq C_{\gamma}\eta s_{0}(2s_{0})^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)},

whenever
r_{1}<s_{0}<1/2
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Crudely estimating a single term, recalling the definition of
b_{j}^{k}
, and multiplying by
\gamma^{-\Lambda_{k}}
gives us the term-wise bound

(5.6)

\displaystyle\left\lvert a_{j}^{k}(s_{0})-\gamma^{-\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(\gamma s_{0})\right\rvert\leq 2^{\Lambda+\Lambda_{k}}C\,\eta^{1/2}s_{0}^{\Lambda-\varepsilon+1/2},

for any
j,k
and any
r_{1}<s_{0}<1/2
and
\gamma\in(1/2,1)
.

Using our previous computations, we first estimate the growth of
a_{j}^{k}(s)
for
k<i
; we claim that for any
1\leq k<i
,
1\leq j\leq N_{k}
, and
s\in[r_{1},1/2]
, we have

(5.7)

\left\lvert a_{j}^{k}(s)-(s/r_{1})^{\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(r_{1})\right\rvert\leq C_{\Lambda}\eta^{1/2}(H(s)s^{-d})^{1/2}

where
C_{\Lambda}
depends only on
\Lambda
. To prove (
5.7
), let us assume for simplicity that
r_{1}=2^{-\ell}
and
r_{1}<s=2^{-\ell+m}\leq 1/2
for some integers
\ell,m
, though it is easy to prove the general case of (
5.7
) from the flexibility of (
5.6
) with the parameter
\gamma
. Now fix any such triple
k
,
j
,
s
. We apply the inequality (
5.6
) with
\gamma=1/2
to the telescoping series

\displaystyle\left\lvert a_{j}^{k}(s)-(s/r_{1})^{\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(r_{1})\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\left\lvert a_{j}^{k}(2^{-\ell+m})-2^{m\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(2^{-\ell})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}2^{i\Lambda_{k}}\left\lvert a_{j}^{k}(2^{-\ell+m-i})-2^{\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(2^{-\ell+m-i-1})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2^{\Lambda+\Lambda_{k}}C\eta^{1/2}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}2^{i(\Lambda+\varepsilon)}(2^{-\ell+m-i})^{\Lambda-\varepsilon+1/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Lambda}\eta^{1/2}2^{(-\ell+m)(\Lambda-\varepsilon+1/2)}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}2^{i(2\varepsilon-1/2)}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Lambda}\eta^{1/2}s^{\Lambda+1/3},

since
\varepsilon<1/10
, which gives (
5.7
) after a simple application of Lemma
5.3
.

Next we move on to the estimate
a_{j}^{k}(s)
for
k>i
. Fix
\gamma\in(0,1)
small and to be determined. We show that for each
s\in[r_{1},\gamma]
, we have the estimate

(5.8)

\sum_{k>i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s)^{2}\leq\tau H(s)s^{-d}

provided that
\varepsilon
and
\eta
are chosen small enough depending on
\Lambda
and
\tau
. Indeed, by virtue of (
5.5
), the representation
u
in polar coordinates, and Lemma
5.3
, we may estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{k>i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k>i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\left(a_{j}^{k}(s)-\gamma^{\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(\gamma^{-1}s)\right)^{2}+\gamma^{2{\Lambda_{k}}}(a_{j}^{k}(\gamma^{-1}s))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Lambda,\gamma}\eta s^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)+1}+C\gamma^{2\Lambda_{i+1}}\sum_{i>k}(a_{j}^{k}(\gamma^{-1}s))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Lambda,\gamma}\eta s^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)+1}+C\gamma^{2\Lambda_{i+1}}H(\gamma^{-1}s)(\gamma^{-1}s)^{-d}

\displaystyle\leq H(s)s^{-d}\left(C_{\Lambda,\gamma}\eta+C\gamma^{2(\Lambda_{i+1}-\Lambda-\varepsilon)}\right).

Setting
\delta_{0}\coloneqq\Lambda_{i+1}-\Lambda>0
, then provided that
\varepsilon<\delta_{0}/2
, we first choose
\gamma
small enough so that
C\gamma^{\delta_{0}}<\tau/2
, and then
\eta
small enough so that
C_{\Lambda,\gamma}\leq\tau/2
, which proves (
5.8
).

With our estimates thus far we conclude. We define explicitly our approximation: set

\displaystyle\tilde{w}(s,\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{1\leq k\leq i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(r_{1})(s/r_{1})^{\Lambda_{k}}\phi_{j}^{k}(\theta),

so that by Corollary
A.10
, we know that
w
is a solution of (
2.1
) (and in fact, is a finite sum of homogeneous solutions). Using the orthogonality of
\phi_{j}^{k}
in
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\sigma_{w})
, we readily estimate using (
5.7
) and (
5.8
),

\displaystyle s^{-d}\int_{\partial B_{s}}(u-\tilde{w})^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k\leq i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k}(s)-(s/r_{1})^{\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(r_{1}))^{2}+\sum_{k>i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\Lambda}\eta\left(\sum_{1\leq k\leq i}N_{k}\right)H(s)s^{-d}+\tau H(s)s^{-d}=H(s)s^{-d}(\tilde{C}_{\Lambda}\eta+\tau)

for any
s\in[r_{1},\gamma]
. Taking
\eta
small enough depending on
\tau
and
\Lambda
then proves the estimate:

(5.9)

s^{-d}\int_{\partial B_{s}}(u-\tilde{w})^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\leq\tau H(s)s^{-d}

We now turn to showing that the lower order terms are not so significant, that is:

(5.10)

\sum_{k<i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s)^{2}\leq\tau^{1/2}H(s)s^{-d}

for
s\in[Mr_{1},\gamma]
(recall again that the frequency around which we are pinched,
\Lambda=\Lambda_{i}
), as long as
\tau
is chosen small enough (depending only on the ellipticity and the ambient dimensions) and
M
is large enough depending on
\tau
.

Assume that (
5.10
) does not hold for some
s\in[Mr_{1},\gamma]
, and use the triangle inequality to get

C\left(s^{-d}\int_{\partial B_{s}}(u-\tilde{w})^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}+\sum_{k<i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k}(r_{1}))^{2}(s/r_{1})^{2\Lambda_{k}}\right)\geq\sum_{k<i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s)^{2}\gtrsim\tau^{1/2}H(s)s^{-d}.

Using (
5.9
) this implies that

\sum_{k<i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k}(r_{1}))^{2}(s/r_{1})^{2\Lambda_{k}}\gtrsim\tau^{1/2}H(s)s^{-d}

as long as
\tau
is chosen small enough.

From here, we can use Lemma
5.3
(but on the interval
[r_{1},s]
) to get that

\begin{aligned} \left(\frac{s}{r_{1}}\right)^{2\Lambda_{i-1}}\sum_{1\leq k<i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(r_{1})^{2}&\gtrsim\tau^{1/2}H(s)s^{-d}\\
&\gtrsim\tau^{1/2}\left(\frac{s}{r_{1}}\right)^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}H(r_{1})r_{1}^{-d}\\
&\gtrsim\tau^{1/2}\left(\frac{s}{r_{1}}\right)^{2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}\sum_{1\leq k<i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(r_{1})^{2}.\end{aligned}.

Since
\varepsilon>0
is small enough we have that
2(\Lambda-\varepsilon)>2\Lambda_{i-1}
(see Theorem
3.3
) which is a contradiction since
s/r_{1}\geq M
which is large, depending on
\tau
. Thus we have shown (
5.10
).

To finish set

w_{0}(s,\theta)\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}}a_{j}^{i}(r_{1})(s/r_{1})^{\Lambda_{i}}\phi_{j}^{i}(\theta).

Then using the orthogonality of the
\phi_{j}
’s we estimate that

s^{-d}\int_{\partial B_{s}}(u-w_{0})^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}\leq\sum_{k<i}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}(s)^{2}+\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}}(a_{j}^{i}(s)-(s/r_{1})^{\Lambda_{i}}a_{j}^{i}(r_{1}))^{2}.

The first term is small by (
5.10
) and the second term we have already shown is small when proving (
5.9
). So we are done.

∎

To transfer the smallness from
L^{2}
to
L^{\infty}
requires just a bit of PDE. We separate the argument into this lemma for the reader’s convenience.

Lemma 5.5
.

For each
\tau\in(0,1)
small enough and
\Lambda_{0}>1
, there exists
\gamma=\gamma(\tau,\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
,
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\Lambda_{0},\tau)\in(0,1)
, and
\eta=\eta(\tau,\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
small enough so that the following holds.

Suppose that
u
solves (
4.3
), and in addition we have (
4.5
) and the bound (
4.43
). If
u
is
\varepsilon
-pinched about
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
at
X_{0}\in B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the scales
[r_{1},1]
for
r_{1}<100^{-1}
(with
\Lambda\leq\Lambda_{0}
), then
\dist_{[\gamma^{-1}r_{1},\gamma]}^{X_{0}}(u,H_{\Lambda}^{2})<\tau
.

Proof.

We can assume for notational simplicity that
X_{0}=0
. What remains is to transfer the
L^{2}(\partial B_{s},d\sigma_{w})
estimate in Lemma
5.4
to an
L^{\infty}
estimate (note we are abusing the notation
\tau
here a little bit, but it does not matter what power
\tau
comes with). We do this by first passing to an
L^{2}
estimate on the bulk
B_{s}
, and then appealing to the Moser-type inequality in Lemma
A.5
for the difference
u-w_{0}
.

We note that
v:=(u-w_{0})
is a strong solution to

(5.11)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\divv(A\nabla v)&=-\mathrm{div}(\delta(X)^{-n+d+1}f)&\text{ in }&B_{1}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
v&=0&\text{ on }&B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{aligned}

with
f=(I-\cal A)\nabla w_{0}
and
0<r<1
. At this stage, it is convenient to make the normalizing assumption that
H_{u}(\gamma)\gamma^{-d}=1
, so that
H_{w_{0}}(\gamma)\gamma^{-d}\simeq 1
. Using Moser inequality and perhaps shrinking
\gamma
we have that for any
\rho\leq\gamma

\sup_{B_{\rho}}|v|\leq\left\{\left(\fint_{B_{2\rho}}v^{2}\;dm\right)^{1/2}+\rho\left(\fint_{B_{2\rho}}\left\lvert f\right\rvert^{q_{0}}\;dm\right)^{1/q_{0}}\right\}

for some
q_{0}>1
. Using the homogeneity of
w_{0}
we have that

|f|(X)\leq C\eta|X|^{\Lambda-1}\rho^{-\Lambda}|\nabla w_{0}(\rho\frac{X}{|X|})|.

Using that
H^{\lambda}_{\Lambda_{0}}
is compact and
w_{0}
is homogeneous we have

(5.12)

\left(\fint_{B_{2\rho}}\left\lvert f\right\rvert^{q_{0}}\;dm\right)^{1/q_{0}}\leq C\eta\rho^{-1}\left(H_{w_{0}}(\rho)\rho^{-d}\right)^{1/2}.

As long as
\rho\in[Mr_{1},\gamma]
we can use the comparability of
H_{w_{0}}(\rho)
and
H_{u}(\rho)
(which follows from Lemma
5.4
) and then apply Lemma
4.14
to get

(5.13)

\left(\fint_{B_{2\rho}}\left\lvert f\right\rvert^{q_{0}}\;dm\right)^{1/q_{0}}\leq C\eta\rho^{-1}\sup_{B_{\rho}}|u|,\qquad\forall\rho\in[Mr_{1},\gamma].

Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
to be determined, and, assuming
\rho>\gamma^{-1}r_{1}
we may assume (again possibly shrinking
\gamma
) that
2^{-k}\rho,\rho\in[Mr_{1},\gamma]
(where
M
is given by Lemma
5.4
). First, remark that Corollary
4.10
and homogeneity tells us that there is a constant
C_{2}>1
for which

(5.14)

\displaystyle\fint_{B_{\rho/2^{k}}}u^{2}\;dm\leq CC_{2}^{-k(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}\fint_{B_{\rho}}u^{2}\;dm,\qquad\fint_{B_{\rho/2^{k}}}w_{0}^{2}\;dm\leq CC_{2}^{-k(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}\fint_{B_{\rho}}w_{0}^{2}\;dm.

On the other hand, integrating the conclusion of Lemma
5.4
for
s\in[\rho/2^{k},\rho]
and applying Lemma
4.14
tells us that if
\eta
is chosen sufficiently small, then there is a constant
C_{1}>1
(depending on our upper bound of the frequency,
\Lambda_{0}
) for which

(5.15)

\displaystyle\fint_{B_{\rho}\setminus B_{\rho/2^{k}}}(u-w_{0})^{2}\;dm\leq C\tau^{1/2}\fint_{B_{\rho}}u^{2}\;dm\leq CC_{1}\tau\sup_{B_{\rho/2}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{2}.

Choosing
k
large enough depending on
\tau
and
\Lambda
so that
C_{2}^{-k(\Lambda-\varepsilon)}<\tau^{1/2}
, one combines the previous two inequalities to deduce

(5.16)

\displaystyle\fint_{B_{\rho}}(u-w_{0})^{2}\;dm\leq CC_{1}\tau^{1/2}\sup_{B_{\rho/2}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert^{2},\;\;\rho\in[\gamma^{-1}r_{1},\gamma].

Note that
\gamma
ultimately depends on
M,k
which in turn depend on
\tau,\Lambda
so there is no circularity here.

Plugging this estimate, and (
5.13
) into our Moser inequality gives that

\sup_{B_{\rho}}|u-w_{0}|\leq C\tau^{1/4}\sup_{B_{\rho}}|u|+C\eta\sup_{B_{\rho}}|u|.

Recalling the normalization in Definition
5.2
and letting
\eta
be small enough, we are done.
∎

One consequence of our pinching estimate is that if the frequency is close enough to 1 in a ball, there can be no singular points in a smaller interior ball. Before we present this corollary, we remind the reader that by Theorem
3.3
, we know that
\Lambda_{1}=1
and
\Lambda_{2}\geq(1+\sqrt{5})/2>3/2
. Since the only non-trivial
\Lambda_{1}
-homogeneous solution of (
2.1
) vanishing on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
\left\lvert t\right\rvert
(up to a multiplicative constant), and since
\left\lvert\nabla(\left\lvert t\right\rvert)\right\rvert
is non-vanishing in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we can deduce the following.

Lemma 5.6

(Lack of singular points)
.

Assume
u
is a solution of (
4.3
), with (
4.5
) and
N_{u}(0,1)\leq 1+\varepsilon
with
\varepsilon
and
\eta
sufficiently small. Then there is some
r_{0}=r_{0}(\eta,\varepsilon)\in(0,1)
so that
Z(u)\cap B_{r_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
and moreover
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}}=\varnothing
. Recall that
Z(u)
denotes the nodal set of
u
.

Proof.

First, we apply Corollary
4.11
to see that if
\eta
is sufficiently small, then in fact we have for some
\delta>0
small,

(5.17)

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,r)\leq 1+2\varepsilon,\;\;0<r<\delta,\;Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{\delta}.

Notice that this automatically gives us that
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{\delta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, since
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
coincides with the set of points
Y
for which
N_{u}(Y,0^{+})\geq\Lambda_{2}
. To prove the Lemma, it suffices to show the restricted cone containment,

(5.18)

\displaystyle\{u=0\}\cap B_{\delta}(Y)\cap\{\delta(X)\geq\left\lvert X-Y\right\rvert/2\}=\{0\},

since then one may union the above over all
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{\delta}
to obtain
Z(u)\cap B_{\delta}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. After a harmless change of variables, we may prove (
5.18
) for
Y=0
and also assume that
\mathcal{A}(0)=I
.

By the almost-monotonicity of
N_{u}
as in Lemma
4.6
, we have

\displaystyle N_{u}(0,r)\geq N_{u}(0,s)e^{-C(r-s)}\geq N_{u}(0,s)e^{-C\delta}

for
0<s<r<\delta
. It follows that if there is
0<r<\delta
with
N_{u}(0,r)\leq(1-2\varepsilon)
, then
N_{u}(0,0^{+})\leq(1-2\varepsilon)(1+C\delta)
. By choosing
\delta
even smaller if necessary (which still preserves (
5.17
)) we see that
N_{u}(0,0^{+})\leq 1-\varepsilon
, which is a clear contradiction of the fact that
N_{u}(0,0^{+})
agrees with the order of vanishing of
u
at
Y
, which must be greater than or equal to
1
. We thus have shown that the frequency of
u
is
(2\varepsilon)
-pinched about
1=\Lambda_{1}\in\cal{F}
at
0\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the scales
[0,\delta]
. If we fix
0<\tau<1/10
and choose
\varepsilon
small enough depending on
\tau
, then we may apply Lemma
5.5
to see that there is a
\gamma\in(0,1)
and a
1
-homogeneous solution
w
of the constant coefficient equation (
2.1
) so that

(5.19)

\displaystyle\sup_{B_{r}}\left\lvert u-w\right\rvert\leq\tau\sup_{B_{r}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert,\;\;0<r<\gamma\delta.

It is easy to see that the above implies

(5.20)

\displaystyle\sup_{B_{r}}\left\lvert w\right\rvert\leq(1+\tau)\sup_{B_{r}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert,\;\;\sup_{B_{r}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert\leq(1-\tau)^{-1}\sup_{B_{r}}\left\lvert w\right\rvert,\;\;0<r<\gamma\delta.

This implies that
w
is a non-trivial
1
-homogeneous solution of (
2.1
) since
u
is non-trivial. As per Theorem
3.3
, it follows that
w(X)=c\delta(X)=c\left\lvert t\right\rvert
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. Notice that by renormalizing
u
, we may assume that
c=1
, and thus from (
5.19
) we see that if
X\in B_{\gamma\delta}
satisfies
\delta(X)\geq\left\lvert X\right\rvert/2
, then

\displaystyle\left\lvert u(X)-\delta(X)\right\rvert\leq\tau(1-\tau)^{-1}\sup_{B_{\left\lvert X\right\rvert}}\left\lvert w\right\rvert\leq\tau(1-\tau)^{-1}\sup_{B_{2\delta(X)}}\left\lvert w\right\rvert\leq 2\tau(1-\tau)^{-1}\delta(X).

Since
\tau<1/10
, we see that
2^{-1}\leq u(X)/\delta(X)\leq 2
for
X\in B_{\gamma\delta}
satisfying
\delta(X)\geq\left\lvert X\right\rvert/2
, which proves our claim (
5.18
) and thus the Lemma.
∎

With the previous Lemma in hand, we can prove by a contradiction-compactness argument the following.

Lemma 5.7

(Upper semi-continuity of the singular set)
.

Fix
\Lambda_{0}>0
,
\tau\in(0,1)
, and
v\in H_{\Lambda}^{2}
, and assume that
u
is a solution of (
4.3
) with (
4.5
), (
4.43
), and
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}=1
. If
\varepsilon
and
\eta
are sufficiently small, depending only on
\tau
,
\Lambda_{0}
, and
v
, then
\left\lVert u-v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}<\varepsilon
implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{1}\subset B_{\tau}(\mathcal{S}(v)).

Proof.

We argue by contradiction and compactness. If the conclusion of the Lemma is not valid, then there exists
\tau_{0}
,
\Lambda_{0}
, and
v\in H_{\Lambda}^{2}
with
\Lambda\leq\Lambda_{0}
and a sequence of functions
u_{m}\in W_{r}(B_{10})
solving

\displaystyle-\divv(A_{m}\nabla u_{m})

\displaystyle=0\text{ in }B_{10},

\displaystyle u_{m}

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{10},

where
A_{m}
satisfies the condition (
4.5
) with
\eta_{m}=1/m
,
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}=1
,
\left\lVert u_{m}-v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}<1/m
, and
u_{m}
is such that

\displaystyle N_{u_{m}}(Y,r)\leq\Lambda_{0},\;\;\text{ whenever }E_{r}(Y)\subset B_{10}.

However, for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a point
X_{m}\in\mathcal{S}(u_{m})\cap B_{1}
for which
\dist(X_{m},\mathcal{S}(v))\geq\tau_{0}
.

Applying Lemma
4.13
(along with Lemma
4.14
to guarantee non-degeneracy), we see that up to taking a subsequence (still labeled
u_{m}
for convenience), the
u_{m}
converge uniformly on compact subsets of
B_{5}
to some solution
u_{\infty}
of (
2.1
) vanishing continuously on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Moreover, we may assume
X_{m}\rightarrow X
for some point
X\in\overline{B_{1}}\setminus\mathcal{S}(v)
. Of course since
\left\lVert u_{m}-v\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}<m^{-1}
, it must be that
u_{\infty}=v
. Since the coefficients
\mathcal{A}_{m}=A_{m}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d-1}
are uniformly Lipschitz, we can apply standard elliptic regularity theory and assume also that
\nabla u_{m}\rightarrow\nabla v
uniformly on compact subsets of
B_{5}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In the case that
X\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then the uniform
C^{1}
convergence of
u_{m}
to
v
away from
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
readily gives that
v(X)=\nabla v(X)=0
, and thus
X\in\mathcal{S}(v)
, which contradicts our choice of the points
X_{m}
.

Hence it must be the case that
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\overline{B_{1}}
, with
X\not\in\mathcal{S}(v)
. As such, we know that
N_{v}(X,0^{+})=1
, and so since
N_{v}(X,r)
is monotone in
r
by Lemma
4.12
, given any
\varepsilon>0
, there is
s_{0}>0
for which
N_{v}(X,r)\leq 1+\varepsilon
for all
0<r<s_{0}
. The conclusion of Lemma
4.13
shows that

\displaystyle N_{u_{m}}^{A_{m}}(X,s_{0})\leq 1+2\varepsilon

for all
m
sufficiently large. However on the other hand, applying Lemma
5.6
in
B_{s_{0}}(X)
to the solution
u_{m}
for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small, we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u_{m})\cap B_{r_{0}s_{0}}(X)=\varnothing,

which is a clear contradiction to the fact that
X_{m}\in\mathcal{S}(u_{m})\rightarrow X
. This finishes the proof of the Lemma. ∎

Before stating one of the main results of this section, we need to introduce some definitions. Whenever
V\in G(n,d)
,
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
0<\alpha<1
, we use the notation

\displaystyle X(Y,V,\alpha)\coloneqq\{Z\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\;:\;\dist(Z-Y,V)<\alpha\left\lvert Z-Y\right\rvert\},

to denote the cone about
V
centered at
Y
with aperture
\alpha
. We also denote
X(0,V,\alpha)\equiv X(V,\alpha)
for the cone centered at the origin, so that in this notation,
X(Y,V,\alpha)\equiv Y+X(V,\alpha)
. We shall also need two geometric estimates (Lemmas
5.10
and
5.11
) which are independent of the solutions of our PDE at hand, and only concern Hausdorff measure estimates and conical properties of sets. As such, we leave them for the end of the Section, but shall soon apply them in our covering arguments, such the following Corollary (see Figure
1
for a schematic picture).

\mathbb{R}^{d}
V_{j}
B_{\gamma}(X_{0})
B_{\gamma^{-1}r_{1}}(X_{0})
\mathcal{S}(u)

Figure 1.
The relative position of the singular set of
u
(blue) inside an annulus, guaranteed by Corollary
5.8
.

Corollary 5.8
.

For each
\Lambda_{0}>0
, there exists
\alpha=\alpha(\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
(potentially close to
1
),
\gamma=\gamma(\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
, and finitely many
(n-2)
-planes
\{V_{j}\}_{j=1}^{K}\subset G(n,n-2)
with
K=K(\Lambda_{0})\in\mathbb{N}
so that the following holds.

Suppose that
u
is a solution of (
4.3
) with (
4.5
) and (
4.43
). Assume in addition that the frequency of
u
is
\varepsilon
-pinched about
\Lambda
at
X_{0}\in B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
in the scales
[r_{1},1]
for
r_{1}<100^{-1}
. Then if
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are small enough, depending on
\Lambda_{0}
, then there exists an index
1\leq j\leq K
for which

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u_{s}^{X_{0}})\cap\partial B_{1}\subset X(V_{j},\alpha)

for all
s\in[\gamma^{-1}r_{1},\gamma]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\left(B_{\gamma}(X_{0})\setminus B_{\gamma^{-1}r_{1}}(X_{0})\right)\subset X(X_{0},V_{j},\alpha).

Proof.

Fix
\tau_{1}\in(0,1)
small and to be determined. Recall from Remark
3.8
that
H_{\Lambda}^{2}\subset C(B_{1})
is compact, and thus we may choose
K(\tau_{1})\in\mathbb{N}
elements,
\{v_{1},\dotsc,v_{K}\}\subset H_{\Lambda}^{2}
which are
\tau_{1}
-dense in
H_{\Lambda}^{2}
. That is, for each
v\in H_{\Lambda}^{2}
, there is some
1\leq j\leq K
for which
\left\lVert v-v_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}<\tau_{1}
. Combining Lemma
5.11
for
F=\mathcal{S}(v_{j})
with the measure estimates from Theorem
3.10
, we see that there is some small
\tau_{2}=\tau_{2}(n,\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
so that for each
1\leq j\leq K
, one can find a
2
-plane,
W_{j}\in G(n,2)
for which

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(v_{j})\cap\partial B_{1}\cap B_{\tau_{2}}(W_{j})=\varnothing.

In other words, with
V_{j}=W_{j}^{\perp}\in G(n,n-2)
, we have

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(v_{j})\cap\partial B_{1}\subset B_{\sqrt{1-\tau^{2}_{2}}}(V_{j}).

Now let
u
be such a solution as in the statement of the Corollary. By Lemma
5.5
and the choice of the elements
\{v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}
, we know that if
\eta
and
\varepsilon
are chosen small enough depending on
\Lambda_{0}
and
\tau_{1}
, then there exists some index
1\leq j\leq K
for which

\displaystyle\left\lVert u_{s}^{X_{0}}-v_{j}\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})}<2\tau_{1}

for all
s\in[\gamma^{-1}r_{1},\gamma]
where
\gamma\in(0,1)
is a small parameter depending on
\Lambda_{0}
and
\tau_{1}
. By taking
\tau_{1}
and
\eta
small enough depending on
\tau_{2}
and
\Lambda_{0}
, Lemma
5.7
then says that for all such
s
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u_{s}^{X_{0}})\cap B_{1}\subset B_{\tau^{2}_{2}/8}(\mathcal{S}(v_{j})).

The scale invariance of
\mathcal{S}(v_{j})
(since
v_{j}
is
\Lambda
-homogeneous) then implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u_{s}^{X_{0}})\cap\partial B_{1}\subset B_{\tau^{2}_{2}/4}(\mathcal{S}(v_{j})\cap\partial B_{1})

as long as
\tau_{2}
is chosen sufficiently small. Combining this containment with (
5.21
), we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u_{s}^{X_{0}})\cap\partial B_{1}\subset B_{1-\tau^{2}_{2}/4}(V_{j}),

which completes the proof of the first claim with
\alpha=1-\tau^{2}_{2}/4
. Of course, the second claim follows from the first one, once one realizes that
\mathcal{S}(u_{s}^{X_{0}})\cap\partial B_{1}=\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\partial B_{s}(X_{0})
.
∎

We end our discussion on frequency pinching with a lemma on the frequency drop. Essentially,
N_{u}
must drop in frequency a definite amount once it dips below one of the frequencies
\Lambda\in\mathcal{F}
. For solutions to the constant coefficient equation (
2.1
), this follows from some straightforward computations using the Fourier decomposition (see Lemma
A.11
). For solutions to more variable coefficient equation, one can reduce to the case of “constant coefficients” using contradiction-compactness techniques as above. Since these results are well know to experts, we omit the proof.

Lemma 5.9

(Frequency drop)
.

Fix
\Lambda_{0}>0
and
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small (depending on
\Lambda_{0}
), and assume (
4.3
), (
4.5
), and (
4.43
). Suppose that
\Lambda_{k+1}\in\mathcal{F}
, and that the solution
u
satisfies

\displaystyle N_{u}(0,1)\leq\Lambda_{k+1}-\varepsilon.

Then if
\eta=\eta(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{0})
is chosen small enough, there is some
\rho_{1}=\rho_{1}(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
small for which
N_{u}(0,s)\leq\Lambda_{k}+\varepsilon
for all
0\leq s\leq\rho_{1}
.

Finally, for completeness, we provide the proofs of the following geometric lemmas.

Lemma 5.10
.

Fix
L>2
, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
. Let
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{N}\in B_{1}
be any finite number of points, and suppose that
\{B_{i}=B_{t_{i}}(Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{K}
is a finite family of pairwise disjoint balls with radii
t_{i}\coloneqq L^{-1}\min_{1\leq j\leq N}\left\lvert Y_{i}-X_{j}\right\rvert
. If there is a
d
-plane
V\in G(n,d)
so that for each
1\leq i\leq K
and
1\leq j\leq N
,

\displaystyle B_{i}\subset B_{1}(0)\cap X(X_{j},V,\alpha),

then there is a constant
C=C(n,d,\alpha,L)>0
so that
\sum_{i=1}^{K}t_{i}^{d}\leq C
, with
C
independent of
N
and
K
.

Proof.

Notice that it suffices to show that for any
X\in V
,

(5.22)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\{1\leq i\leq K\;:\;\pi_{V}^{-1}(X)\cap B_{i}\neq\varnothing\}\right\rvert\leq C

for some constant
C>0
depending only on
n,d,\alpha
and
L
. Indeed (
5.22
) is equivalent to
\sum_{1\leq i\leq K}\chi_{\pi_{V}(B_{i})}\leq C
, which along with
B_{i}\subset B_{1}(0)
implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{K}t_{i}^{d}

\displaystyle=C\sum_{i=1}^{K}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\pi_{V}(B_{i}))=C\int_{V}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\chi_{\pi_{V}(B_{i})}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}\leq C\int_{B_{1}(0)\cap V}d\mathcal{H}^{d}\leq C.

To show (
5.22
), we will show first that the balls
B_{i}
with overlapping projections have comparable radii. To this end, let
Y,Z\in B_{1}
be points for which
\pi_{V}(Y)=\pi_{V}(Z)
and
Y,Z\in X(X_{j},V,\alpha)
for each
1\leq j\leq N
. Let
1\leq i\leq N
be an index minimizing
\{\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert\;:\;1\leq j\leq N\}
. First, we claim that the cone condition on
Z
implies that

(5.23)

\displaystyle\min\{\left\lvert Z-X_{j}\right\rvert\;:\;1\leq j\leq N\}\simeq\left\lvert Z-X_{i}\right\rvert,

with implicit constant depending only on
\alpha
. Indeed, for any index
1\leq j\leq N
, notice that
Y\in X(X_{j},V,\alpha)
implies that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\pi_{V}(Y-X_{j})\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\pi_{V}(Y-X_{j})\right\rvert+\left\lvert\pi_{V^{\perp}}(Y-X_{j})\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert\pi_{V}(Y-X_{j})\right\rvert+\alpha\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert,

from which we conclude
\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert\simeq_{\alpha}\left\lvert\pi_{V}(Y-X_{j})\right\rvert
. Since the same holds for
Z
, the fact that
\pi_{V}(Z)=\pi_{V}(Y)
readily gives (
5.23
).

Since
L>2
, then the definition of
t_{i}
readily implies for any
1\leq i\leq K
and
1\leq j\leq N
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert Z-X_{j}\right\rvert\simeq\left\lvert Y_{i}-X_{j}\right\rvert

for any choice of indices
1\leq i\leq K
,
1\leq j\leq N
and any
Z\in B_{i}
. In particular, if
Y\in B_{i}
and
Z\in B_{i^{\prime}}
where
1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq K
are such that
\pi_{V}(Y)=\pi_{V}(Z)
, then (
5.23
) and the previous observation imply that

t_{i}\simeq\min_{1\leq j\leq N}\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert\simeq\min_{1\leq j\leq N}\left\lvert Z-X_{j}\right\rvert\simeq t_{i^{\prime}},

so that
B_{i}
and
B_{i^{\prime}}
have comparable radii. Finally we conclude: fix
1\leq i\leq K
and choose
1\leq j\leq N
so that

\displaystyle Lt_{i}=\left\lvert Y_{i}-X_{j}\right\rvert=\min_{1\leq k\leq N}\left\lvert Y_{i}-X_{k}\right\rvert.

If we denote by
I\coloneqq\{1\leq i^{\prime}\leq K\;:\;\pi_{V}(B_{i^{\prime}})\cap\pi_{V}(B_{i})\neq\varnothing\}
, then there is a constant
C>1
depending on
n
,
d
,
\alpha
, and
L
for which
C^{-1}t_{i}\leq\left\lvert Y_{i^{\prime}}-X_{j}\right\rvert\leq Ct_{i}
and
C^{-1}t_{i}\leq t_{i^{\prime}}\leq Ct_{i}
for each
i^{\prime}\in I
. Using the disjointness of the
B_{i^{\prime}}
we then see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{Ct_{i}}(X_{j}))

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i^{\prime}\in I}\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{i^{\prime}})\geq C^{-1}\left\lvert B_{t_{i}}\right\rvert\left\lvert I\right\rvert,

so that
\left\lvert I\right\rvert\leq C
. Clearly (
5.22
) follows from this estimate, completing the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.11

(Volume estimates imply cones on spheres)
.

For each
\Lambda>0
, there is a constant
\delta=\delta(\Lambda,n)>0
so that the following holds. If
F\subset\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
is a subset for which

\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(B_{\varepsilon}(F)\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1})\leq\Lambda\varepsilon^{2},\qquad 0<\varepsilon<1,

then there is a
2
-plane
V\in G(n,2)
for which
F\cap B_{\delta}(V)=\varnothing
.

Proof.

Notice that we may as well assume that
F=-F
, since
F\cup(-F)\subset\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
also satisfies the main assumption of the Lemma with
2\Lambda
in place of
\Lambda
. First, let us show the existence of
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\Lambda,n)>0
and a point
Y\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
for which

(5.24)

\displaystyle\left(B_{\delta_{0}}(Y)\cup B_{\delta_{0}}(-Y)\right)\cap F=\varnothing.

Fix any direction
Z_{0}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and denote by
H=H(Z_{0})\subset\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
a spherical cap,
H\coloneqq\{X\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\;:\;X\cdot Z_{0}>1/2\}
. Assuming
0<\delta_{0}<10^{-1}
small to be determined below, we choose
m\geq c\delta_{0}^{-n+1}
points
X_{1},\dotsc,X_{m}\in H
so that
\{B_{\delta_{0}}(X_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}
is a collection of pairwise disjoint balls with centers in
H
. Denote by
I=\{1\leq i\leq m\;:\;B_{\delta_{0}}(X_{i})\cap F\neq\varnothing\}
, and notice by the disjointedness of the
B_{\delta_{0}}(X_{i})
and the fact that
B_{\delta_{0}}(X_{i})\subset B_{C\delta_{0}}(F)
for
i\in I
, we have

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left\lvert I\right\rvert\delta_{0}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i\in I}\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(B_{\delta_{0}}(X_{i})\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\left((\cup_{i\in I}B_{\delta_{0}}(X_{i}))\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\right)\leq\mathcal{H}^{n-1}\left(B_{C\delta_{0}}(X_{i})(F)\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\right)\leq C\Lambda\delta_{0}^{2},

and thus
\left\lvert I\right\rvert\leq C\Lambda\delta_{0}^{-n+3}
for some constant
C>0
depending only on
n
. Choosing
\delta_{0}
small enough so that
C\Lambda\delta_{0}^{-n+3}<c\delta_{0}^{-n+1}
(or in other words,
\delta_{0}^{2}<(C\Lambda)^{-1}
), we see that
\left\lvert I\right\rvert<m
. Thus there is some
1\leq j\leq m
with
j\not\in I
, so that (
5.24
) holds for
Y=X_{j}
since
F=-F
.

Fix such a
Y
and parametrize a family of
2
-planes containing
Y\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
as follows: let
E
denote the equator
E\coloneqq\{W\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\;:\;\left\langle Y,W\right\rangle=0\}
, so that
E\simeq\mathbb{S}^{n-2}
, and define
V(W)\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\{Y,W\}\in G(n,2)
for
W\in E
. Next, fix
0<\delta\ll\delta_{0}
small and to be determined, and choose
m\geq c\delta^{-n+2}
points
W_{1},\dotsc,W_{m}\in E
so that
\{B_{\delta}(W_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}
is a family of pairwise disjoint balls centered on
E
. Similar to before, denote by
I=\{1\leq i\leq m\;:\;B_{\delta}(V(W_{i}))\cap F\neq\varnothing\}
, and choose for each
i\in I
some
Z_{i}\in B_{\delta}(V(W_{i}))\cap F\setminus B_{\delta_{0}}(Y)
and
X_{i}\in V(W_{i})\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\setminus B_{\delta_{0}/2}(Y)
with
\left\lvert Z_{i}-X_{i}\right\rvert\leq C\delta
. Notice that such a choice of
X_{i}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
is possible provided
\delta
is much smaller than
\delta_{0}
. It is a straight-forward computation to see that
X_{i}\in V(W_{i})\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\setminus B_{\delta_{0}/2}(Y)
in fact implies
\left\lvert X_{i}-X_{j}\right\rvert\geq c\delta_{0}\left\lvert W_{i}-W_{j}\right\rvert\geq c\delta\delta_{0}
for some
c>0
. Hence we may conclude as before; for
r\coloneqq c\delta\delta_{0}/2
, we see that
\{B_{r}(X_{i})\}_{i\in I}
is a family of pairwise disjoint balls and contained in
B_{C\delta}(F)
, and thus we estimate

\displaystyle C^{-1}\left\lvert I\right\rvert r^{n-1}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(\cup_{i\in I}B_{r}(X_{i})\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1})\leq\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(B_{C\delta}(F)\cap\mathbb{S}^{n-1})\leq C\Lambda\delta^{2}.

Recalling the definition of
r
, one sees that
\left\lvert I\right\rvert\leq C\Lambda\delta^{-n+3}\delta_{0}^{-n+1}
, and so if
C\Lambda\delta^{-n+3}\delta_{0}^{-n+1}<c\delta^{-n+2}
, then there is some
1\leq j\leq m
for which
j\not\in I
, which completes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

6.
The main covering argument

In this section, we prove our main result regarding the size of
\mathcal{S}(u)
for solutions
u
of equations of the type (
4.3
), i.e., we prove Theorem
6.4
. The main tool used in the proof of this Theorem is the covering Lemma
6.3
, which provides Minkowski estimates on
B_{1}\cap(\mathcal{S}(u)\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d})
at a discrete scale, much like Proposition 3.36 in
[
NV17b
]
. In our setting however, the proof is (and necessarily must be) considerably different. Since this covering argument is one of the main difficulties presented by Theorem
1.1
, let us spend more time explaining this difficulty and contrasting it with the case of harmonic functions (or more generally, solutions to elliptic PDEs with Lipschitz coefficients).

Suppose that
v
is a harmonic function in
B_{1}
, and there are two distinct points
X_{1},X_{2}\in B_{1}
for which the Almgren frequency function
N_{v}(X_{1},r)
,
N_{v}(X_{2},r)
is constant on nontrivial intervals
I_{1},I_{2}
respectively. Then by a rigidity result for
N_{v}
, we know that
v(\,\cdot\,+X_{1}),v(\,\cdot\,+X_{2})
are homogeneous harmonic polynomials, and thus
v
is homogeneous with respect to the points
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
. As a consequence,
v
is
invariant
in the
X_{2}-X_{1}
direction:

\displaystyle v(Y+t(X_{2}-X_{1}))=v(Y),\;t\in\mathbb{R},

and thus the same holds true for
Z(v)
(the nodal set of
v
) and
\mathcal{S}(v)
. A quantification of the previous statement says that if there are many points
X_{i}\in B_{1}
for which
N_{v}(X_{i},r)
is approximately constant on a sufficiently large interval, then the
X_{i}
must be contained in a small neighborhood of an affine set (see Lemma 3.22 and Corollary 3.24 in
[
NV17b
]
). This quantitative cone-splitting is one of the key arguments used in
[
NV17b
]
to conclude
\mathcal{S}(v)\cap B_{1/2}
is contained in finitely many
(n-2)
-dimensional Lipschitz graphs, and thus has finite
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}
measure. The same sort of analysis can be done for solutions
v
of
-\divv(A\nabla v)=0
when
A
is Lipschitz, because at small scales,
A
is approximately constant and so solutions
v
are well-approximated by harmonic functions.

For solutions
u
to equations of the type (
4.3
) (or even (
3.9
)), we immediately have a problem at the level of the rigidity result for
N_{u}
. While it is true that
N_{u}(X,r)\equiv\Lambda
for a nontrivial interval
r\in I\subset[0,\infty)
implies that
u
is
\Lambda
-homogeneous when
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{1}
, (Lemma
4.12
), we do not have such rigidity for
X\in B_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Even worse, the coefficients
A
are not uniformly Lipschitz, and the computation in Remark
4.16
tells us that the best behavior we can expect of
u
in the Whitney ball
B_{\delta(X)/4}(X)
is that of a solution
v
to a divergence form equation
-\divv(\tilde{A}\nabla v)=0
in the ball
B_{2}
with
|\nabla\tilde{A}|\leq C
. In particular, this behavior
does not improve
as
\delta(X)\rightarrow 0
, and so
u
is only well-approximated by a harmonic function in
B_{\tau\delta(X)}(X)
for
\tau\in(0,1)
sufficiently small. Of course the balls,
B_{\tau\delta(X_{i})}(X_{i})
, can be far from overlapping, and so this harmonic approximation tells us very little about the global structure (i.e., Lipschitz parametrization) of the points
X_{i}
in
B_{1}
. As such, we must abandon this strategy of obtaining Lipschitz parametrizations and find another way to estimate the size of all of the balls centered at the points
X_{i}
where the frequency is pinched. Our approach is to show that such balls must be contained in many cones centered at
\mathbb{R}^{d}
pointing in roughly the same direction.

With the tools developed thus far, we can prove our main covering Lemma used in the proof of Theorem
1.1
, but first let us introduce some notation. In what follows, assume
u
is a solution of (
4.3
).

Definition 6.1
.

Fix
\varepsilon>0
. We say that a boundary ball
B_{t}(X_{0})
for
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda,\Lambda_{0})
-good if for each
Y\in B_{t}(X_{0})
and
0<s\leq t
, we have

(6.1)

\displaystyle N_{u}(Y,s)\leq\begin{cases}\Lambda+\varepsilon,&Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\Lambda_{0},&Y\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.\end{cases}

Definition 6.2
.

Fix
\bar{r}>0
. Whenever
B_{t}(X_{0})
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda,\Lambda_{0})
-good, define for
X\in B_{t}(X_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
r_{X}^{\prime}\coloneqq\sup\{s\geq 0\;:\;N_{u}(X,s)\leq\Lambda-\varepsilon\}
, and
r_{X}\coloneqq\max\{\bar{r},r_{X}^{\prime}\}
(here,
r_{X}^{\prime}=-\infty
if the set in its definition is empty).

Our main covering result, in analogy with Proposition 3.36 in
[
NV17b
]
, is the following.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
\Lambda_{0},\bar{r}>0
and suppose
\Lambda_{k}\in\mathcal{F}
is such that
\Lambda_{k}\leq\Lambda_{0}
and
k>1
. Then there exists
C=C(\Lambda_{0})>1
and a choice of
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\Lambda_{0}),\eta=\eta(\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
sufficiently small so that the following holds.

Suppose that
u
is solution of (
4.3
), with (
4.5
) and (
4.43
), and
B_{1}(0)
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k},\Lambda_{0})
-good. Then there is a finite collection of balls
\{B_{r_{i}}(X_{i})\}_{i\in I}
with
X_{i}\in(\mathcal{S}(u)\cup\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap B_{1}(0)
so that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}(u)\cup\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap B_{1}(0)\subset\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{r_{i}}(X_{i}),\;\;\;\sum_{i\in I}r_{i}^{n-2}\leq C.

Moreover, for each ball
B_{r_{i}}(X_{i})
, one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

C^{-1}\bar{r}\leq r_{i}\leq C\bar{r}
,

(2)

B_{2r_{i}}(X_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

(3)

X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
B_{r_{i}}(X_{i})
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k-1},\Lambda_{0})
-good.

Proof.

Since the proof is technical, let us provide an overview. It may be useful for the reader to refer back to the parameters introduced in this Lemma, as well as their uses, below:

•

\Lambda_{0}>1
: a uniform bound on the Almgren frequency
N_{u}

•

\Lambda_{k}
, (
\Lambda_{k-1}
) : the current (next) discrete frequency, as in the Lemma statement

•

\varepsilon\in(0,1)
: the small “pinching” parameter around the frequency, see Definitions
5.1
and
6.1

•

\eta\in(0,1)
: how close
\mathcal{A}
is to the identity matrix, see (
4.5
)

•

\rho_{1}\in(0,1)
: a small scale for which the frequency-dropping property, Lemma
5.9
, holds

•

\alpha,\gamma\in(0,1)
