: geometric parameters quantifying the containment of
\mathcal{S}(u)
in a cone provided that
u
has
\varepsilon
-pinched frequency (see Corollary
5.8
)

•

\tau\in(0,1)
,
M,L_{1},L_{2}>1
: fixed parameters that ensure certain geometric containments hold

The main idea of the proof is as follows. We want to find a cover of
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{1}(0)
consisting of smaller balls
B_{r_{i}}(X_{i})
satisfying the conclusions of the Lemma. Since
B_{1}(0)
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k},\Lambda_{0})
-good, then the frequency satisfies
N_{u}(X,s)\leq\Lambda_{k}+\varepsilon
for
X\in B_{1}(0)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
0<s\leq 1
, and thus the frequency of
u
is
\varepsilon
-pinched about
\Lambda_{k}
at
X
in the scales
[r_{X},1]
, by the definition of
r_{X}
. Suppose that
r_{X}=r_{X}^{\prime}
. First, notice that
N_{u}(X,r_{X})=\Lambda_{k}-\varepsilon
, and so the frequency-dropping principle, Lemma
5.9
, implies (for
\varepsilon
and
\eta
small) that
N_{u}(X,s)\leq\Lambda_{k-1}+\varepsilon
for
0<s\leq\rho_{1}r_{X}
. This is essentially (iii), our desired “goodness” condition for the next set of balls (though at the moment this only shows that bound for
N_{u}(X,s)
at
X
, and not near
X
). Moreover, Corollary
5.8
says that as long as
\varepsilon
and
r_{X}^{\prime}
are sufficiently small, then singular set
\mathcal{S}(u)

away
from the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{d}
is contained in a conical region about an
(n-2)
-hyperplane. For such singular points, we will choose a certain class of Whitney-type balls to cover them, so that these balls satisfy (ii). Finally, we will use Lemma
5.10
to obtain a Minkowski estimate on these Whitney-type balls away from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, while the balls centered on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
sum for free, as long as we can find an essentially disjoint collection. The arguments thus far only accounts for the types of points
X
for which
r_{X}=r_{X}^{\prime}
, but for the other points, we have that
r_{X}=\bar{r}
, which will give balls for which (i) holds. The biggest technicality in the proof comes from the fact that for the aforementioned balls for which (iii) holds, we only had that
N_{u}(X,s)\leq\Lambda_{k-1}+\varepsilon
for
0<s\leq\rho_{1}r_{X}
. In order to guarantee such bounds for all
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
nearby
X
, we will need to separate boundary points
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{1}(0)
into good points, where
r_{Y}\gtrsim r_{X}
near
B_{r_{X}}(X)
, and deal with the rest separately.

At this point, let us begin the rigorous proof in earnest.
With
\Lambda_{0}
given, we may choose some
\alpha=\alpha(\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
, a finite family of
(n-2)
-planes
\{V_{1},\dotsc,V_{K}\}\subset G(n,n-2)
with
K=K(\Lambda_{0})\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(\Lambda_{0}),\eta=\eta(\Lambda_{0})
, and
\gamma=\gamma(\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
small enough so that the conclusion of Corollary
5.8
holds. By choosing
\eta
smaller (now also depending on
\varepsilon
), we also may assume that conclusion of Lemma
5.9
holds with
\rho_{1}=\rho_{1}(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{0})\in(0,1)
. Finally, we fix parameters
M,L_{1},L_{2}>1
large which shall be specified later for geometric arguments, but in the end only
M
and
L_{2}
will depend on
\Lambda_{0}
(implicitly through
\varepsilon
,
\eta
, and
\gamma
as well).

Step one: covering the boundary.
First, we subdivide
B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
into a good piece
G
and a bad piece
F
for the covering:

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle\coloneqq\{X\in B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\;:\;r_{Y}\geq r_{X}/10\text{ for all }Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{10Mr_{X}}(X)\}

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle\coloneqq(B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d})\setminus G.

By Vitali’s covering lemma, we may choose a finite collection of points
X_{i}\in G
,
i\in I_{1}
so that with
r_{i}=r_{X_{i}}
, we have
B_{Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
are disjoint and
\cup_{i\in I_{1}}B_{5Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\supset G
. It is clear that since the
B_{Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
are disjoint and centered on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\leq n-2
, then

(6.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{1}}(Mr_{i})^{n-2}\leq C.

Moreover, for each
i\in I_{1}
, either
\bar{r}\leq r_{i}\leq 10\bar{r}
or otherwise
r_{i}>10\bar{r}
and the definition of
G
implies that
r_{Y}=r_{Y}^{\prime}\geq r_{i}/10
for
Y\in B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Thus in this latter case, we have that for all
Y\in B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
N_{u}(Y,r_{Y}^{\prime})\leq\Lambda_{k}-\varepsilon
by definition, and thus Lemma
5.9
implies that

(6.3)

N_{u}(Y,s)\leq\Lambda_{k-1}+\varepsilon\text{ for all }0\leq s\leq\rho_{1}10Mr_{i},\;Y\in B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Note that the factor of
\rho_{1}
in (
6.3
) means these balls do not yet satisfy the conditions of the Lemma, but we will modify them at the end of the proof to get our cover of
G
.

Next, we move to the covering of the bad part of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{1}
,
F
. Consider any point
Y\in F
. By the definition of a bad point, we can find some
Q_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{10Mr_{Y}}
with
r_{Q_{1}}<r_{Y}/10
. Then either
Q_{1}\in G
and we stop, or
Q_{1}\in F
and we repeat to find
Q_{2}\in B_{10Mr_{Q_{1}}}(Q_{1})
with
r_{Q_{2}}<r_{Q_{1}}/10<10^{-2}r_{Y}
. We repeat the process (which terminates, since the
r_{X}
are bounded from below) until we find some
Q\in G
for which

\displaystyle\left\lvert Y-Q\right\rvert\leq 20Mr_{Y},\;\;r_{Q}<r_{Y}/10.

Since
Q\in G
, there exists some index
i
(depending on
Y
) so that
Q\in B_{5Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
. By definition of
G
, we see that
r_{i}/10\leq r_{Y}
and thus
\left\lvert Y-X_{i}\right\rvert\leq 30Mr_{Y}
. Altogether this shows that

(6.4)

\displaystyle\min_{j\in I_{1}}\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert\leq 30Mr_{Y}\text{ for all }Y\in F.

For
Y\in F
, denote by
i(Y)
an index minimizing the distance above.

Now for
Y\in F\setminus\cup_{i\in I_{1}}B_{5Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
, let us set
t_{Y}\coloneqq\min_{j\in I_{1}}\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert/L_{1}<30Mr_{Y}/L_{1}
. For such
Y
and for
Z\in B_{10t_{Y}}(Y)
, then choosing
L_{1}>10
large enough, we have

\displaystyle\min_{j\in I_{1}}\left\lvert Z-X_{j}\right\rvert\geq\left\lvert Y-X_{j}\right\rvert-|Z-Y|\geq\dfrac{1}{2}\left\lvert Y-X_{i(Y)}\right\rvert=\dfrac{L_{1}t_{Y}}{2}.

In particular, with (
6.4
) applied to
Z\in F\cap B_{10t_{Y}}(Y)
combined with the above, we see that

\displaystyle 30Mr_{Z}\geq\min_{j\in I_{1}}\left\lvert Z-X_{j}\right\rvert\geq\dfrac{L_{1}t_{Y}}{2},

i.e.,
r_{Z}\geq(L_{1}t_{Y})/(60M)\geq t_{Y}/(60M)
. If
Z\in G\cap B_{10t_{Y}}(Y)
, then there is an
i\in I_{1}
for which
Z\in B_{5Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
, and so by definition of
G
, we have
r_{Z}\geq r_{i}/10
. On the other hand, since
Y\not\in B_{5Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
but
B_{5Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
meets
B_{10t_{Y}}(Y)
, we readily see that
L_{1}t_{Y}\leq\left\lvert Y-X_{i}\right\rvert\leq 10t_{Y}+5Mr_{i}
, so for
L_{1}>15
, we have
t_{Y}\leq Mr_{i}
. Hence we see
r_{Z}\geq r_{i}/10\geq t_{Y}/(10M)
in this case, and so in either case we have shown that

(6.5)

\displaystyle r_{Z}\geq t_{Y}/(60M)\text{ for any }Z\in B_{10t_{Y}}(Y)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Now we repeat essentially the same procedure as for
G
but with the radii
t_{Y}
instead. By Vitali’s covering Lemma, we may choose a finite number of points
Y_{i}\in F\setminus\cup_{j\in I_{1}}B_{5Mr_{j}}(X_{j})
,
i\in I_{2}
and radii
t_{i}=t_{Y_{i}}
so that the
B_{t_{i}}(Y_{i})
are pairwise disjoint and
\cup_{i\in I_{2}}B_{5t_{i}}(Y_{i})\supset F\setminus\cup_{j\in I_{1}}B_{5Mr_{j}}(X_{j})
. Similar to before, since the
B_{t_{i}}(Y_{i})
are disjoint and centered on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have the estimate

(6.6)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{2}}(10t_{i})^{n-2}\leq C.

First recall from above that for any such
Y_{i}
,
|Y-X_{j}|\geq 5Mr_{j}\geq 5M\bar{r}
. Using (
6.5
) just as before, we obtain that either for each
i\in I_{2}
, either
\frac{5M}{L_{1}}\bar{r}\leq t_{i}<60M\bar{r}
, or otherwise for all
Z\in B_{10t_{i}}(Y_{i})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have
r_{Z}\geq t_{i}/(60M)\geq\bar{r}
and so by definition
N_{u}(Z,r_{Z})\leq\Lambda_{k}-\varepsilon
and, by Lemma
5.9
,

(6.7)

\displaystyle N_{u}(Z,s)\leq\Lambda_{k-1}+\varepsilon\text{ for all }0\leq s\leq\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M),\;\;Z\in B_{10t_{i}}(Y_{i})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Just as for
G
, these balls do not quite satisfy the conditions of the Lemma (due to the
\rho_{1}
factor) but we will modify them later to cover
F
, and thus all of
B_{1}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Step two: the covering away from the boundary.
We move to our covering for the rest of
\mathcal{S}(u)
, which we write as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)\coloneqq(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{1})\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i\in I_{1}}B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\cup\bigcup_{i\in I_{2}}B_{10t_{i}}(Y_{i})\right).

For
Z\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)
we define similarly to before

\displaystyle s_{Z,1}\coloneqq\min_{i\in I_{1}}\left\lvert Z-X_{i}\right\rvert,\;s_{Z,2}\coloneqq\min_{i\in I_{2}}\left\lvert Z-Y_{i}\right\rvert,\;s_{Z}\coloneqq\min\{s_{Z,1},s_{Z,2}\}/L_{2}.

First we remark that with this choice of
s_{Z}
, we have

(6.8)

\displaystyle s_{Z}\leq 2\delta(Z)/L_{2}

for all
Z\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)
. Indeed given such a
Z
, we know that
\pi(Z)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is contained in some
B_{5Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
or
B_{5t_{i}}(Y_{i})
, so assume it is in the first set (the argument for the second case is exactly the same). Since
Z\not\in B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
, we have that

\displaystyle 10Mr_{i}<\left\lvert Z-X_{i}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert Z-\pi(Z)\right\rvert+\left\lvert\pi(Z)-X_{i}\right\rvert<\delta(Z)+5Mr_{i},

and thus
\delta(Z)>5Mr_{i}>\left\lvert\pi(Z)-X_{i}\right\rvert
. Finally, the triangle inequality, the previous inequality, and the definition of
s_{Z}
give (
6.8
). As before, we choose a finite number of points
Z_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)
,
i\in I_{3}
and radii
s_{i}=s_{Z_{i}}
so that the
B_{s_{i}}(Z_{i})
are disjoint and
\cup_{i\in I_{3}}B_{5s_{i}}(Z_{i})\supset\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)
. In view of (
6.8
) for
L_{2}
large, we see that the balls
B_{5s_{i}}(Z_{i})
are relatively far from the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(i.e. satisfy the second condition in the Lemma for balls in our cover), so we need only to estimate
\sum_{i\in I_{3}}s_{i}^{n-2}
, which we do now.

First, we claim that for every
Z\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)
, we have the estimate

(6.9)

\displaystyle\min_{i\in I_{1}}\left\lvert Z-X_{i}\right\rvert\leq 2L_{1}\min_{i\in I_{2}}\left\lvert Z-Y_{i}\right\rvert,

Indeed, suppose otherwise, and choose
j\in I_{2}
so that
\left\lvert Z-Y_{j}\right\rvert=s_{Z,2}
and
i\in I_{1}
so that
L_{1}t_{Y_{j}}=\left\lvert Y_{j}-X_{i}\right\rvert
. We immediately obtain

\displaystyle 2L_{1}\left\lvert Z-Y_{j}\right\rvert<\left\lvert Z-X_{i}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert Z-Y_{j}\right\rvert+\left\lvert Y_{j}-X_{i}\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert Z-Y_{j}\right\rvert+L_{1}t_{j},

which says that
\left\lvert Z-Y_{j}\right\rvert\leq 2t_{j}
, a clear contradiction to the fact that
Z\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)
. Notice that (
6.9
) and the definition of
s_{i}
implies for every
i\in I_{3}
,

(6.10)

\displaystyle s_{i}\geq\min_{j\in I_{1}}\left\lvert Z-X_{j}\right\rvert/(2L_{1}L_{2})=s_{Z_{i},1}/(2L_{1}L_{2}).

Define for
i\in I_{3}
,
j(i)\in I_{1}
an index for which
\left\lvert Z_{i}-X_{j(i)}\right\rvert\leq 2L_{1}L_{2}s_{i}
, which exists by (
6.10
). Now we separate the index set
I_{3}
into
K+1
families as follows.

First set
I_{1}^{\tau}=\{j\in I_{1}\;:\;r_{j}\geq\tau\}
,
I_{3}^{\tau}=\{i\in I_{3}\;\;:\;j(i)\in I_{1}^{\tau}\}
for some fixed
0<\tau<\gamma^{2}/2
, where
\gamma\in(0,1)
was chosen at the beginning of the proof. By definition of
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k},\Lambda_{0}
)-good and the radii
r_{X}^{\prime}
, we know that
u
is
\varepsilon
-pinched about
\Lambda_{k}
in the scales
[r_{X}^{\prime},1]
when
X\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{1}
. Hence by Corollary
5.8
, we know that for each
j\in I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\tau}
, there is an index
1\leq\ell(j)\leq K
so that

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\cap(B_{\gamma}(X_{j})\setminus B_{\gamma^{-1}r_{j}}(X_{j}))\subset X(X_{j},V_{\ell(j)},\alpha),

where the
\{V_{\ell}\}
are the planes chosen in the beginning of the proof. For convenience, write
\ell(i)=\ell(j(i))
, and introduce the partitions

\displaystyle I_{3}^{\ell}\coloneqq\{i\in I_{3}\setminus I_{3}^{\tau}\;:\;\ell(i)=\ell\},\;\;I_{1}^{\ell}\coloneqq\{j\in I_{1}\;:\;\ell(j)=\ell\}.

Now we provide an estimate for the sums
\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\ell}}s_{i}^{n-2}
and
\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\tau}}s_{i}^{n-2}
separately. The second sum is easy to estimate though, once we recall (
6.10
). Indeed we have that since
Z_{i}\not\in B_{10Mr_{j(i)}}
,

\displaystyle s_{i}\geq\left\lvert Z_{i}-X_{j(i)}\right\rvert/(2L_{1}L_{2})\geq(5M\tau)/(L_{1}L_{2}),

which is a constant. Hence using the disjointedness of the
B_{s_{i}}(Z_{i})
(and the fact that they are contained in
B_{2}
),

(6.12)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\tau}}s^{n-2}\leq C\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\tau}}s^{n}\leq C.

Moving to the other sums, we claim that as long as
M>\gamma^{-1}
, then for any pair
i\in I_{3}
and
j\in I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\tau}
for which
Z_{i}\in B_{\gamma}(X_{j})
, (
6.11
) implies
Z_{i}\in X(X_{j},V_{\ell(j)},\alpha)
. Indeed (
6.11
) applies to
Z_{i}
provided that the annulus there is non-degenerate, and
Z_{i}
is contained in it. This constitutes two conditions:
\gamma^{-1}r_{j}<\gamma
, which is guaranteed by the choice of
\tau
and the fact that
j\in I_{1}\setminus I_{1}^{\tau}
, and
\left\lvert Z_{i}-X_{j}\right\rvert\geq\gamma^{-1}r_{j}
, which is true provided we choose
M>\gamma^{-1}
(recall that
Z_{i}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(u)
, so
Z_{i}\not\in B_{10Mr_{j}}(X_{j})
). We have shown the claim, but in fact even more is true: if
L_{2}
is chosen small enough, depending on
\alpha
, then

(6.13)

\displaystyle B_{\left\lvert Z_{i}-X_{j}\right\rvert/L_{2}}(Z_{i})\subset X(X_{j},V_{\ell(j)},(1+\alpha)/2).

Indeed this is a simple geometric fact, which at the unit scale reads as follows: if
Z\in\partial B_{1}\cap X(V,\alpha)
, where
V\in G(n,n-2)
and
L=L(\alpha)>1
is sufficiently large, then
B_{L^{-1}}(Z)\subset X(V,(1+\alpha)/2)
, independently of the plane
V
. We omit this computation.

Finally, we are in the position to conclude. If we set for
\ell
fixed and
i\in I_{3}^{\ell}
,
\tilde{s}_{i,\ell}\coloneqq\min_{j\in I_{1}^{\ell}}\left\lvert Z_{i}-X_{j}\right\rvert/L_{2}\geq s_{i}
, then with the containment (
6.13
) we are able to apply Lemma
5.10
to the family of balls
\{B_{\tilde{s}_{i,\ell}}(Z_{i})\}_{i\in I_{3}^{\ell}}
, the boundary points
\{X_{j}\}_{j\in I_{3}^{\ell}}
and the fixed plane
V_{\ell}
to conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\ell}}s_{i}^{n-2}\leq C\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\ell}}\tilde{s}_{i,\ell}^{n-2}\leq C(\alpha).

To be clear, we only have the containment (
6.13
) for when
Z_{i}\in B_{\gamma}(X_{j})
, and so we must apply Lemma
5.10
several times (with balls centered on the boundary of radius
\gamma/2
, say), and use the fact that if
Z_{i}\not\in B_{\gamma}(X_{j})
for each
j\in I_{1}^{\ell}
, then
\tilde{s}_{i,\ell}\gtrsim_{\gamma}1
. Altogether with (
6.12
) then,

(6.14)

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in I_{3}}s_{i}^{n-2}\leq\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\tau}}s_{i}^{n-2}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\sum_{i\in I_{3}^{\ell}}s_{i}^{n-2}\leq C+C(\alpha)K,

is a constant depending only on
\Lambda_{0}
.

Step three: refining the cover.
Finally, we can produce our desired cover from our two main steps thus far. For the piece of the cover coming from the balls
\{B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\}_{i\in I_{1}}
, we choose for each
i\in I_{i}
boundedly many points
X_{i,1},\dotsc,X_{i,N(i)}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
,
X_{i,1}^{\prime},\dotsc,X_{i,N(i)}^{\prime}\in B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})
with
N(i)\leq C=C(M,\rho_{1})
so that

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j=1}^{N(i)}B_{\rho_{1}10Mr_{i}}(X_{i,j})\supset B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\cap\{Y\mid\delta(Y)\leq\rho_{1}5Mr_{i}\},

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j=1}^{N(i)}B_{\rho_{1}r_{i}}(X_{i,j}^{\prime})\supset B_{10Mr_{i}}(X_{i})\cap\{Y\mid\delta(Y)>\rho_{1}5Mr_{i}\},

and for each
1\leq j\leq N(i)
we have that
B_{2\rho_{1}r_{i}}(X_{i,j}^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Notice that for each
i\in I_{1}
, either
\bar{r}\leq r_{i}\leq 10\bar{r}
, or otherwise (
6.3
) implies that
B_{\rho_{1}10Mr_{i}}(X_{i,j})
are
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k-1},\Lambda_{0})
-good for
1\leq j\leq N(i)
. We perform essentially the same construction for the balls
\{B_{10t_{i}}(Y_{i})\}_{i\in I_{2}}
with (
6.7
) to find boundedly many balls
B_{\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M)}(Y_{i,j}),B_{\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M^{2})}(Y_{i,j}^{\prime})
with
1\leq j\leq N(i)\leq C(M,\rho_{1})
,
Y_{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{10t_{i}}(Y_{i})
, and so that

\displaystyle\bigcup_{j=1}^{N(i)}\left(B_{\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M)}(Y_{i,j})\cup B_{\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M^{2})}(Y_{i,j}^{\prime})\right)\supset B_{10t_{i}}(Y_{i}),

\displaystyle B_{2\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M^{2})}(Y_{i,j}^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Moreover, for each
i\in I_{2}
, either
\frac{5M}{L_{1}}\bar{r}\leq t_{i}\leq 60M\bar{r}
or otherwise
B_{\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M)}(Y_{i,j})
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k-1},\Lambda_{0})
-good. Finally, for each of the balls
B_{5s_{i}}(Z_{i})
,
i\in I_{3}
we recall (
6.8
) which tells us that for
L_{2}
sufficiently large that
B_{10s_{i}}(Z_{i})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In view of the estimates (
6.2
), (
6.6
), and (
6.14
) and the fact that
N(i)\leq C
, our Lemma is complete if we cover by the collection of balls

\displaystyle\{B_{5s_{i}}(Z_{i})\}_{i\in I_{3}},\;\;\{B_{\rho_{1}10Mr_{i}}(X_{i,j}),B_{\rho_{1}r_{i}}(X_{i,j}^{\prime})\}_{i\in I_{1},\,1\leq j\leq N(i)},

\displaystyle\{B_{\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M)}(Y_{i,j}),B_{\rho_{1}t_{i}/(60M^{2})}(Y_{i,j}^{\prime})\}_{i\in I_{2},\,1\leq j\leq N(i)}.

∎

With the help of Lemma
6.3
, we may now provide a proof of our main result.

Theorem 6.4
.

Suppose that
A
satisfies the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{10}
with constants
\lambda,C_{0}>1
, and that
w\in W_{r}(B_{10})\cap C(B_{10})
is a solution of (
4.3
). Then there exists
r_{0}=r_{0}(\lambda,C_{0})\in(0,10)
small enough and
C=C(\lambda,C_{0},N_{w}(0,100r_{0}))>1
so that for
0<s<r_{0}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}\left(B_{s}(\mathcal{S}(w))\cap B_{r_{0}}\right)\leq Cs^{2}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathcal{S}(w)\cap B_{r_{0}})\leq C(\lambda,C_{0},N_{w}(0,100r_{0})).

Moreover,
\mathcal{S}(w)\cap B_{r_{0}}
is countably
(n-2)
-rectifiable: there exists countably many
(n-2)
-dimensional Lipschitz graphs
\Sigma_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n-2}\left(\mathcal{S}(w)\cap B_{r_{0}}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{i}\right)=0.

Proof.

Let
w
be such a solution, and define the function
v(X)\coloneqq w(\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}X)
where
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{A}(0)
. By Remark
4.7
, we have that
v
is a solution of

\displaystyle-\divv(B\nabla v)

\displaystyle=0\text{ in }\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10},

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}B_{10}.

where
B
satisfies the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{10/\sqrt{\lambda}}
with constants
C\lambda,CC_{0}
and
\mathcal{B}(0)=I
. Moreover, since
\mathcal{A}_{0}^{1/2}
is bi-Lipschitz and the frequency function is constructed precisely so that
N_{w}^{A}(0,r)=N_{v}^{B}(0,r)
,
then it suffices to prove the result for
v
in place of
w
. Of course here we are also using the fact that
\mathcal{S}(v)=\mathcal{A}_{0}^{-1/2}\mathcal{S}(w)
.

To this end, notice that for
v_{r_{0}}(X)=v(r_{0}X)
with
r_{0}>0
sufficiently small,
v_{r_{0}}
solves an equation of the type (
4.3
) with matrix
B_{r_{0}}=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{B}(r_{0}\,\cdot)
, which satisfies the small-constant condition (
4.5
) with
\eta=r_{0}CC_{0}
. Along with Corollary
4.20
, we may thus assume by taking
r_{0}
sufficiently small that
u\equiv v_{r_{0}}
is a solution of (
4.3
) satisfying (
4.5
) for
\eta
as small as we wish, with the additional bound (
4.43
). Here
\Lambda_{0}>1
is a constant which depends on
\lambda,C_{0}
, and also
N_{w}^{A}(100r_{0})=N_{v}^{B}(100r_{0})=N_{u}^{B}(100)
.

Now we begin the covering argument. Fix
0<s<r_{0}
, and choose
\varepsilon(\Lambda_{0})>0
and
\eta^{\prime}(\Lambda_{0})>0
small enough so that we may apply Lemma
6.3
. Next, choose a finite number of points

\displaystyle Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{1}

with
N=N(\eta^{\prime})
so that

\displaystyle\dist(Y,\mathbb{R}^{d})\geq\eta^{\prime}/2,\text{ for }Y\in B_{1}\setminus\cup_{i=1}^{N}B_{\eta^{\prime}}(Y_{i}),

and let us first cover
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{\eta^{\prime}}(Y_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq N
fixed.

First, notice that if
\Lambda_{k}\in\mathcal{F}
is smallest frequency with
\Lambda_{k}>\Lambda_{0}
, then
B_{\eta^{\prime}}(Y_{i})
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k},\Lambda_{0})
-good, and so we may apply Lemma
6.3
to find a finite collection of balls
\{B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})\}_{j\in I_{k}}
for which

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}^{d}\cup\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{\eta^{\prime}}(Y_{i})\subset\bigcup_{j\in I_{k}}B_{r_{j}}(X_{j}),\;\sum_{j\in I_{k}}r_{j}^{n-2}\leq C(\eta^{\prime})^{n-2}.

Moreover, we also have for any index
j\in I_{k}
, either
C^{-1}s\leq r_{j}\leq Cs
or
2B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, (in which case we write
j\in I_{k}^{G}
) or
X_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k-1},\Lambda_{0})
-good (in which case we write
j\in I_{k}^{B}
). For each index
j\in I_{k}^{B}
we iterate; applying Lemma
6.3
again, we can find a collection of balls
\{B_{r_{\ell}}(X_{\ell})\}
indexed by
\ell\in I^{j}_{k-1}
for which

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}^{d}\cup\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})\subset\bigcup_{\ell\in I_{k-1}^{j}}B_{r_{\ell}}(X_{\ell}),\;\sum_{\ell\in I_{k-1}^{j}}r_{\ell}^{n-2}\leq C(r_{j})^{n-2}.

In addition, the same alternative holds for
B_{r_{\ell}}(X_{\ell})
; for each such ball, either
C^{-1}s\leq r_{\ell}\leq Cs
or
2B_{r_{\ell}}(X_{\ell})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or
B_{r_{\ell}}(X_{\ell})
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{k-2},\Lambda_{0})
-good. Notice that combining the two previous sums, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in I_{k}}\sum_{\ell\in I_{k-1}^{j}}r_{\ell}^{n-2}\leq C^{2}(\eta^{\prime})^{n-2}.

Repeating this finitely many times, this process must terminate until we find a finite union of balls
\{B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})\}_{j\in I}
(relabeled for convenience) for which

(6.15)

\displaystyle(\mathbb{R}^{d}\cup\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{\eta^{\prime}}(Y_{i})\subset\bigcup_{j\in I}B_{r_{j}}(X_{j}),\;\,\;\sum_{j\in I}r_{j}^{n-2}\leq C^{k}(\eta^{\prime})^{n-2},

and for each
j\in I
, one of the alternatives holds: either
C^{-1}s\leq r_{j}\leq Cs
,
B_{2r_{j}}(X_{j})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, or
B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})
is
(\varepsilon,\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{0})
-good. If
B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})
is a ball for which the last alternative holds, notice we can apply Lemma
5.6
to further decompose each such ball
B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})
into a finite family of sub-balls of comparable radii which cover
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})
and instead, only one of the first two alternatives hold. Finally, for each
j\in I
for which
B_{2r_{j}}(X_{j})\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we may apply the Theorem
4.18
of
[
NV17b
]
(using the fact that the frequency of
u
in
B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})
is uniformly bounded by
\Lambda_{0}
, and that
u
satisfies a uniformly elliptic equation with Lipschitz coefficients in
B_{r_{i}}(X_{j})
by Remark
4.16
) to obtain a further sub-cover of finitely many balls
\{B_{t_{\ell}}(Z_{\ell})\}
for which
C^{-1}s\leq t_{\ell}\leq Cs
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})\subset\bigcup_{\ell}B_{t_{\ell}}(Z_{\ell}),\;\,\;\sum_{\ell}t_{\ell}^{n-2}\leq Cr_{j}^{n-2}.

Replacing the
B_{r_{j}}(X_{j})
by this sub-cover and again relabeling for convenience, we have found a collection of balls
B_{r_{i}}(X_{i})
for which

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{\eta^{\prime}}(Y_{i})\subset\bigcup_{j\in I}B_{r_{j}}(X_{j}),\;\,\;\sum_{j\in I}r_{j}^{n-2}\leq C^{k}(\eta^{\prime})^{n-2},\;\;C^{-1}s\leq r_{j}\leq Cs.

Summing over the finitely many points
Y_{1},\dotsc,Y_{N}
, and taking a cover of
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\{\delta(Y)>\eta^{\prime}/2\}
using Whitney balls as we did previously with the main result of
[
NV17b
]
, we thus can cover

(6.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{1}\subset\bigcup_{j\in J}B_{r_{j}}(X_{j}),\;\,\;\sum_{j\in J}r_{j}^{n-2}\leq C,\;\;C^{-1}s\leq r_{j}\leq Cs,

which one readily sees implies the first main conclusion of the Theorem once recalling the definition of
u
.

To see that
\mathcal{S}(u)
is countably
(n-2)
rectifiable in
B_{1}
, we simply recall Remark
4.16
which implies that
u
solves a uniformly elliptic equation with Lipschitz coefficients in Whitney balls
B_{\delta(Y)/4}(Y)
away from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular, the main Theorem in
[
NV17b
]
implies that
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{\delta(Y)/4}(Y)
is
(n-2)
-rectifiable, whenever
Y\in B_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We then can cover
B_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
by countably many Whitney balls, and cover the remaining piece
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-2}
to conclude, since
d\leq n-2
.
∎

As a Corollary of the main result, Theorem
6.4
, we obtain singular set estimates for solutions to the degenerate elliptic equation outside of
C^{1,1}

d
-dimensional graphs. In what follows, as in Appendix
B
, when
\Gamma
is
d
-Ahlfors regular
4
4

4

any graph of a
d
-Lipschitz function will be
d
-Ahlfors regular
, we write

(6.17)

D_{\beta}(X)\coloneqq\left(\int_{\Gamma}\frac{1}{\left\lvert X-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\right)^{-1/\beta},\qquad X\not\in\Gamma

for the regularized distance, and
D_{\infty}(X)\coloneqq\dist(X,\Gamma)
for the euclidean distance to
\Gamma
.

Corollary 6.5

(The main result)
.

Let
\beta\in(1,\infty]
and assume the following:

(1)

0\in\Gamma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
d
-dimensional,
d
-Ahlfors regular
C^{1,1}
graph parametrized by
\phi\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{n-d})
with
\left\lVert\nabla\phi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq{C_{2}}
,

(2)

u\in W_{r}(B_{1})\cap C(B_{1})
is a weak solution of

-\mathrm{div}(a(X)D_{\beta}(X)^{-n+d+1}\nabla u(X))=0,\qquad X\in B_{1}

and
u|_{\Gamma\cap B_{1}}=0
for
a(X)\in C^{0,1}(B_{1})
and
\Lambda\geq a(x)\geq\lambda

Then there exists
r_{0}\in(0,1)
small enough and
C>1
(both depending on
n,{C_{2}},\beta,\|a\|_{\mathrm{Lip}},\lambda,\Lambda
and
C
depending also on
N_{u}(0,100r_{0})
) so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{s}(\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{r_{0}})\leq Cs^{2},\qquad\mathcal{H}^{n-2}(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}})\leq C.

Moreover,
\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}}
is countably
(n-2)
-rectifiable: there exists countably many
(n-2)
-dimensional Lipschitz graphs
\Sigma_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
i\in\mathbb{N}
so that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n-2}\left(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}}\setminus\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{i}\right)=0.

Proof.

This Corollary is a simple consequence of Theorems
6.4
and
B.5
. (For details regarding the case
\beta=\infty
, see Remark
B.6
). First, Theorem
B.5
implies that there is
r_{0}=r_{0}(n,d,C_{2})\in(0,1)
and a bi-Lipschitz change of variables (with constant at most
2
)

\displaystyle\rho:B_{r_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\rho(B_{r_{0}})

which maps
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{r_{0}}(0)
bijectively onto
\Gamma\cap\rho(B_{r_{0}})
. By
[
DFM19
, Section 4]
, we know that

-\mathrm{div}(a(X)D_{\beta}(X)^{-n+d+1}\nabla u(X))=0

in
B_{r_{0}}
implies that
w\coloneqq u\circ\rho
is a solution of

\displaystyle-\divv(a\circ\rho A_{\rho,\beta}\nabla w)

\displaystyle=0\text{ in }\rho(B_{r_{0}}),

\displaystyle w

\displaystyle=0\text{ on }\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap\rho(B_{r_{0}}),

where
A_{\rho,\beta}
is symmetric, degenerate elliptic matrix defined by
\rho
in (
B.5
). Moreover, Theorem
B.5
also guarantees that
A_{\rho,\beta}
satisfies the higher co-dimensional condition in
B_{r_{0}}
with constants
\lambda=\lambda(n,d,\beta)
and
C_{0}=C_{0}(n,d,\beta,C_{2})>0
. Furthermore, since
a
is Lipschitz and
a\simeq 1
and
\rho
is Bi-Lipschitz, it follows immediately that
a\circ\rho A_{\rho,\beta}
also satisfies the higher co-dimension condition with perhaps slightly larger constants that depend on the properties of
a
. Applying Theorem
6.4
to
w
gives us the estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{s}(\mathcal{S}(w))\cap B_{r_{0}^{2}})\leq C^{\prime}s^{2}

where
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(n,d,C_{2},N_{w}(0,100r_{0}^{2}))
. Since
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz with constant
\leq 2
, we have that
\mathcal{S}(u)=\rho^{-1}(\mathcal{S}(w))
and so the above implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{n}(B_{s}(\mathcal{S}(u))\cap B_{r_{0}^{2}/2})\leq CC^{\prime}s^{2},

which is almost our desired Minkowski estimate. Moreover, since
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz, then the
(n-2)
-rectifiability of
\mathcal{S}(w)
implies the same for
\mathcal{S}(u)
.

The only thing left to do is replace the constant
C^{\prime}
which depends on
N_{w}(0,100r_{0}^{2})
with the frequency for
N_{u}
. However, recalling the doubling inequalities from Corollary
4.10
, we can estimate the frequency
N_{w}(0,r)
by a doubling index, which transfers easily to
u
through the change of variables
\rho
:

(6.18)

\displaystyle N_{w}(0,r)\leq C\log\left(\dfrac{\int_{E_{2r}}w^{2}\;dm}{\int_{E_{r}}w^{2}\;dm}\right)+1,\,r<r_{0}^{2}.

It is easy to check that the change of variables
\rho
, which is centered about the point
0\in\Gamma
, asymptotically maps round balls to round balls near the origin, and so in particular,
\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}(0)=a\circ\rho(0)I
and thus
E_{r}=B_{cr}
for some
c>0
. Combining this with (
6.18
) and the fact that
\rho
is bi-Lipschitz, it is easy to check then that for
0<r<r_{0}^{2}
and
r_{0}
small enough,

\displaystyle N_{w}(0,r)\leq C\log\left(\dfrac{\int_{B_{4cr}}u^{2}\;dm}{\int_{B_{cr/2}}u^{2}\;dm}\right)+1=C\mathscr{N}_{u}(cr/2)+1,

and so the claim is proved.

∎

Remark 6.6

(The case
d<n-2
: a simpler proof)
.

There is a much simpler proof of the
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}
-measure bound from Theorem
1.1
when
d<n-2
, which simply uses the subcritical scaling of all of the quantities. The main point is that one may apply Naber-Valtorta in each Whitney ball
B_{\delta(Y)/4}(Y)
for
Y\not\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
(as per Remark
4.16
) to deduce that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n-2}\left(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{\delta(Y)/4}(Y)\right)\leq C({N}_{u}(0,100r_{0}))\delta(Y)^{n-2},

and then we sum over a countable collection of such balls covering
B_{r_{0}}\setminus\Gamma
. This sum converges when
d<n-2
, since in the ball there are
\approx 2^{dk}
such Whitney balls of radius
2^{-k}
each of which contains a singular set with
\mathcal{H}^{n-2}
-measure no larger than
C2^{-k(n-2)}
. Summing over all such Whitney balls, and all such scales gives us the estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n-2}\left(\mathcal{S}(u)\cap B_{r_{0}}\right)\leq C(N_{u}(0,100r_{0}))\sum_{k\geq 1}2^{dk-k(n-2)}\leq C(N_{u}(0,100r_{0})),

when
d<n-2
. Of course this argument still requires one to prove uniform boundedness of the frequency function outside of some ball (i.e., Corollary
4.20
).

Given the above,
d=n-2
is really the critical case of the conclusion of the main Theorem. In some sense this suggests that the exact statement as in Theorem
1.1
is maybe the wrong one to consider when
d<n-2
, but the discussion at the end of Section
2
also suggests that it is not so trivial to find an alternative statement.

Appendix A
Regularity theory for elliptic equations with boundaries of high co-dimension

In this section, we recall (and prove) several PDE estimates for solutions to equations of the form (
4.3
) for matrices
A
with enough regularity. As usual, we consider the domain
\Omega\coloneqq\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
and set
\Gamma\coloneqq\partial\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The next result comes from
[
DFM23a
]
, and although the results stated there are global, the arguments and conclusion can be made local. The main premise is that for matrices
A
which satisfy the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition, one has additional regularity of
\nabla_{x}u
for solutions
u
to (
4.3
). In fact, one really only needs to know boundedness of
\nabla_{x}\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathcal{A}
is the matrix coming from Definition
4.1
.

Lemma A.1

(Propositions 7.3, 7.5 in
[
DFM23a
]
)
.

Suppose that
X_{0}\in\Gamma
,
R>0
, and
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
solves (
4.3
) in
B_{R}(X_{0})
, and
u
vanishes continuously on
\Gamma\cap B_{R}(X_{0})
. If the matrix
A
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{R}(X_{0})
with constants
C_{0},\lambda>0
, then we have
\nabla_{x}u\in W_{r}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
(i.e.,
\nabla\nabla_{x}u\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(B_{R}(X_{0}),dm)
) with the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R/2}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla\nabla_{x}u\right\rvert^{2}dm\lesssim_{\lambda,C_{0}}R^{-2}\int_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm.

Moreover,
\mathrm{Tr}(\nabla_{x}u)=0
almost everywhere on
\Gamma\cap B_{R}(X_{0})
, and

(A.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{B_{R/2}(X_{0})\cap\{\varepsilon/2<\delta<\varepsilon\}}\left\lvert\nabla_{x}u\right\rvert^{2}\delta^{-n+d}\;dX=0.

As a consequence of the above, we conclude with some “soft” estimates on higher order derivatives of
u
near
\Gamma
for solutions of (
2.1
).

Proposition A.2
.

Suppose that
X_{0}\in\Gamma
,
R>0
,
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
solves (
2.1
) in
B_{R}(X_{0})
, and
u
vanishes continuously on
\Gamma\cap B_{R}(X_{0})
. For
r<R/2
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and
\alpha,\beta
multi-indices with
k=\left\lvert\beta\right\rvert
, define the function

\displaystyle v_{\alpha,\beta}(X)\coloneqq\delta(X)^{k}\sup_{Y\in W_{X}}\left\lvert\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{t}^{\beta}u(Y)\right\rvert

where
W_{X}=B(X,\delta(X)/8)
is a Whitney ball. Then one has the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}v_{\alpha,\beta}(X)^{2}\;dm\lesssim_{k}R^{-2\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+2}\int_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm.

Proof.

The argument is a simple consequence of Schauder theory, and the “classic elliptic theory” that extends to the degenerate elliptic operators we consider.
Fix
X\in B_{r}(X_{0})
, set
\rho=\delta(X)/4
, and re-scale
u
by

\displaystyle w(Y)\coloneqq(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u)(X+\rho Y).

We remark that since
u
is a solution to the equation (
2.1
), then so is
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u
since
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(\left\lvert t\right\rvert)=0
.
Moreover, Proposition
A.1
iterated
\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert-1
times gives that
\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u\in W_{r}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
with

(A.2)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\lesssim R^{-2\left\lvert\alpha\right\rvert+2}\int_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm.

Now, setting
\tilde{\delta}(Y)\coloneqq\rho^{-1}\delta(X+\rho Y)
, we see that
w
solves the equation

\displaystyle-\divv(\tilde{\delta}^{-n+d+1}\;\nabla w)=0\text{ in }B_{1}.

The classical Schauder Theory (see for example,
[
FRRO22
, Corollary 2.29]
implies that for any
\gamma\in(0,1)
,

(A.3)

\displaystyle\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{C^{k,\gamma}(B_{1/2})}\lesssim_{n,d,k}\left\lVert w\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{1})},

with constant depending only on
n,d,
and
k
since
\tilde{\delta}
is smooth in
B_{1}
, bounded above and below in
B_{1}
(with controlled constant), and since
\|\tilde{\delta}\|_{C^{m}(B_{1})}\leq C_{m}
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
. The inequality (
A.3
) clearly gives that

\displaystyle v_{\alpha,\beta}(X)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sup_{B_{\delta(X)/4}(X)}\left\lvert\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u\right\rvert,

so that since
(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u)^{2}\geq 0
has
-\divv(\delta^{-n+d+1}\nabla((\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u)^{2}))=-2\delta^{-n+d+1}\left\lvert\nabla\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u\right\rvert^{2}\leq 0
, the interior Moser estimate (
[
DFM21
, Lemma 8.7]
) gives that

\displaystyle v_{\alpha,\beta}(X)^{2}\lesssim\fint_{B_{\delta(X)/2}(X)}(\partial_{x}^{\alpha}u)^{2}\;dm.

Since on
B_{\delta(X)/2}(X)
,
\delta(Y)\simeq\delta(X)
, the conclusion of the Proposition holds after a suitable covering argument, by estimate (
A.2
).
∎

It turns out that in the proof of the monotonicity of the frequency function for solutions in Lemma
4.6
, we need
L^{\infty}
estimates on
\nabla u
. In the setting of the matrices
A
which satisfy the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition, we can indeed provide such an estimate.

Lemma A.3

(Boundedness of
\nabla u
)
.

Suppose that
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
solves (
4.3
) for
X_{0}\in\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
A
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{R}(X_{0})
with constants
C_{0},\lambda>0
. Then there is a constant
C
depending only on the constants
n
,
d
,
C_{0}
,
\lambda
, and the scale
R>0
for which

\displaystyle\left\lvert u\right\rvert+\delta\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert\leq C\delta\sup_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert u\right\rvert

holds in
B_{R/2}(X_{0})
. In particular,
\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert\in L^{\infty}(B_{R/2}(X_{0}))
.

Proof.

First, we claim that for
M=M(n,d,C_{0},\lambda,R)>0
chosen large enough and
r_{0}=r_{0}(n,d,C_{0},\lambda,R)>0
small enough, the function
g(x,t)\coloneqq\left\lvert t\right\rvert-M\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}
is a supersolution of (
4.3
) in
B_{r_{0}}(Y)
for any
Y\in B_{R/2}(X_{0})\cap\Gamma
. This is merely a computation which we outline the details of now.

Write
A=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}
as in Definition
4.1
, and denote
\mathcal{A}=(\overline{a}_{ij})
with the block structure

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\begin{pmatrix}J_{1}&\mathcal{A}_{1}\\
J_{2}&\mathcal{A}_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix},

where
J_{1}
is a
d\times d
matrix, and
\mathcal{A}_{2}
is an
(n-d)\times(n-d)
matrix. We compute that
\nabla g=t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert-2Mt
, and thus

\displaystyle\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}\nabla g=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n-d}\overline{a}_{i(k+d)}\left(t_{k}/\left\lvert t\right\rvert-2Mt_{k}\right)\right).

Further computation shows

\displaystyle\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}\nabla g)

\displaystyle=(-n+d+1)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d-1}\left\langle t,\mathcal{A}_{2}(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert-2Mt)\right\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\Tr(\mathcal{A}_{2}D^{2}(\left\lvert t\right\rvert-M\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}))+\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}O(C_{0}(1+M\left\lvert t\right\rvert)),

where the error comes from differentiating the coefficients of
\mathcal{A}
, which are Lipschitz with constant
\leq C_{0}
. Next, recalling the block structure of
\mathcal{A}_{2}(x,0)=h(x,0)I_{n-d}
at the boundary, we can rewrite the above (again using the Lipschitz nature of
\mathcal{A}
) after multiplying by
\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d-1}
:

\displaystyle\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d-1}

\displaystyle\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}\nabla g)

\displaystyle=h(x,0)\left((-n+d+1)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-2}\left\langle t,(t/\left\lvert t\right\rvert-2Mt)\right\rangle+\Tr(D^{2}(\left\lvert t\right\rvert-M\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2})\right)+O(C_{0}(1+M\left\lvert t\right\rvert))

\displaystyle=h(x,0)\left((-n+d+1)(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-1}-2M)+(n-d-1)\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-1}-2M(n-d)\right)+O(C_{0}(1+M\left\lvert t\right\rvert))

\displaystyle=-2Mh(x,0)+O(C_{0}(1+M\left\lvert t\right\rvert))\leq-2M\lambda+O(C_{0}(1+M\left\lvert t\right\rvert)),

since
\Tr(D^{2}\left\lvert t\right\rvert)=(n-d-1)/\left\lvert t\right\rvert
and
\Tr(D^{2}\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2})=2(n-d)
. For
Y\in B_{R/2}(X_{0})\cap\Gamma
, the above clearly implies that for
M
sufficiently large and
r_{0}
sufficiently small,
\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}\nabla g)\leq 0
in
B_{r_{0}}(Y)
which is to say that
g
is a supersolution of (
4.3
). By taking
M
larger and
r_{0}
smaller if necessary, notice that we also have the estimate

(A.4)

\displaystyle g(Y)\simeq\left\lvert t\right\rvert=\delta(Y)\text{ for all }Y\in B_{R/2}(X_{0}),\,\delta(Y)<r_{0},

with implicit constant depending on
M
and
r_{0}
.

Now, fix some
Y\in B_{R/2}(X_{0})\cap\Gamma
. Let
v
be a solution to the problem

\displaystyle-\divv(A\nabla v)

\displaystyle=0\text{ in }B_{r_{0}}(Y)\cap\Omega,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\left\lvert u\right\rvert\text{ on }\partial(B_{r_{0}}(Y)\cap\Omega).

The existence of such a solution is guaranteed, for example, by
[
DFM23b
, Lemma 12.13]
, and the fact that
B_{r_{0}}(Y)\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
belongs to one of the permissible domains for which the elliptic theory of
[
DFM23b
]
holds (we omit the details). Since
v
is a solution, and
\left\lvert u\right\rvert=u_{+}+u_{-}
is a subsolution by
[
DFM21
, Lemma 8.5]
, the comparison principle
[
DFM21
, Lemma 11.14]
then says that
0\leq\left\lvert u\right\rvert\leq v
in
B_{r_{0}}(Y)\cap\Omega
. Essentially the same comparison principle argument allows us to find a nonnegative solution
G
which satisfies
0\leq G\leq g
in
B_{r_{0}}(Y)\cap\Omega
.

Since
v
and
g
are positive solutions of (
4.3
) which vanish on
B_{r_{0}}(Y)\cap\Gamma
, the boundary comparison principle
[
DFM21
, Theorem 11.17]
tells us that

(A.5)

\displaystyle v(X)\leq CG(X)\left(\dfrac{v(Z)}{G(Z)}\right)\text{ in }B_{r_{0}/2}(Y)

for some constant
C=C(n,d,\lambda)>0
, where
Z=Z(Y)
is any corkscrew point associated to the boundary ball
B_{r_{0}}(Y)
, say,
Y+(r_{0}/2)e
where
e
is any unit vector orthogonal to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Notice that
\tau\coloneqq\inf_{Y\in B_{R/2}(X_{0})\cap\Gamma}G(Z(Y))>0
is some positive constant again depending on
n,d,C_{0},\lambda
and
R>0
, but independent of
u
(here, one can show by compactness that
\tau
depends on the operator
A
only through the associated constants
\lambda
and
C_{0}
). Along with our previous estimate
v\leq\sup_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert u\right\rvert
, (
A.4
), (
A.5
) imply for every
X\in B_{r_{0}/2}(Y)
,

\displaystyle 0\leq\left\lvert u(X)\right\rvert\leq v(X)\lesssim G(X)\sup_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert u\right\rvert\leq g(X)\sup_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert u\right\rvert\lesssim\delta(X)\sup_{B_{R}(X_{0})}\left\lvert u\right\rvert.

This gives the first estimate of the Lemma in
B_{r_{0}/2}
, and the second follows quickly from standard Schauder theory. Finally, applying this estimate to smaller balls centered in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{R}
allows one to obtain the full estimate claimed in
B_{R/2}
.
∎

Finally, the following standard estimate says that solutions to elliptic equations of the type (
4.3
) where the coefficients
A
satisfy the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition are well-approximated by solutions to constant coefficient equations at small scales.

Lemma A.4
.

Suppose that
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}(X_{0}))
solves (
4.3
) for
X_{0}\in\Gamma=\mathbb{R}^{d}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
A
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{R}(X_{0})
with constants
C_{0},\lambda>0
, and
\mathcal{A}(X_{0})=I
. Then there is a constant
C
depending only on
n
,
d
,
\lambda
for which the following holds. If
v\in W_{r}(B_{r}(X_{0}))
with
r<R
is the solution of the boundary value problem

(A.6)

\displaystyle-\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\nabla v)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ in }B_{r}(X_{0})\cap\Omega,

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=u

\displaystyle\text{ on }\partial(B_{r}(X_{0})\setminus\Gamma),

then we have the energy estimate

\displaystyle\fint_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla(u-v)\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq CC_{0}r\min\left\{\fint_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm,\fint_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\right\}.

Proof.

Let
v
be such a solution. Integrating by parts and the fact that
u=v
on
\partial B_{r}(X_{0})
gives us

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\nabla(v-u)\cdot\nabla v\;dm

\displaystyle=0,

or in other words,

(A.7)

\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\equiv\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\nabla u\cdot\nabla v\;dm.

Since
A
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{R}(X_{0})
, we may write
A(X)=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}(X)
, with
\left\lvert\mathcal{A}(X)-I\right\rvert\leq C_{0}\left\lvert X-X_{0}\right\rvert
. It follows that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\;dm

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm+\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}(\mathcal{A}(X)-I)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\;dm

\displaystyle=(1+O(C_{0}r))\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm,

and similarly,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm=(1+O(C_{0}r))\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\mathcal{A}\nabla v\cdot\nabla v\;dm.

Recall that
\mathcal{A}
is uniformly elliptic, and so we see that
\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\simeq\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm
rather quickly from the fact that

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\;dm\leq\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\mathcal{A}\nabla v\cdot\nabla v\;dm,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm,

since
u
,
v
are both solutions of (different) elliptic equations with the same trace on
\partial(B_{r}(X_{0})\setminus\Gamma)
. Using this energy minimization again,
we also deduce that the weighted Dirichlet energies of
u
and
v
are sufficiently close:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\mathcal{A}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\;dm+CC_{0}r\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\mathcal{A}\nabla v\cdot\nabla v\;dm+CC_{0}r\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle\leq\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm+CC_{0}r\left(\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\right).

Finally, we combine the previous inequality with (
A.7
) to see

\displaystyle\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla(v-u)\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}-2\nabla v\cdot\nabla u+\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}-\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle\leq CC_{0}r\left(\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\right),

which concludes the proof since
\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\simeq\int_{B_{r}(X_{0})}\left\lvert\nabla v\right\rvert^{2}\;dm
.
∎

Finally, we shall need in Section
5
a Moser-type inequality for solutions to equations of the type (
4.3
) with a right-hand side. Since the proof uses the classical Moser iteration, we omit it, but instead point the reader to the proof of the Moser-type inequality in this higher co-dimension setting without right-hand side found in
[
DFM21
, Lemma 8.12]

Lemma A.5
.

There is an exponent
q_{0}>1
so that the following holds. Let
u
be a solution of

(A.8)

\displaystyle-\divv(A\nabla u)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{div}(\delta^{-n+d+1}f)

\displaystyle\text{ in }

\displaystyle B_{2r}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}),

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{ on }

\displaystyle B_{2r}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
\mathcal{A}=A\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d-1}
is symmetric and uniformly elliptic with constant
\lambda>0
, and
f\in L^{q_{0}}(B_{2r},dm)
. Then there is a constant
C=C(n,d,q_{0},\lambda)>1
for which

\displaystyle\sup_{B_{r}}\left\lvert u\right\rvert\leq C\left\{\left(\fint_{B_{2r}}u^{2}\;dm\right)^{1/2}+r\left(\fint_{B_{2r}}\left\lvert f\right\rvert^{q}\;dm\right)^{1/q}\right\}.

A.1.
Dahlberg’s Theorem in
B_{1}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
L^{2}
solvability of the Dirichlet problem

In this section, we justify the use of Theorem
A.6
, which was applied in Section
5
to obtain quantitative approximations for solutions to (
4.3
) with almost constant frequency. First, let us introduce some notation.

We consider the domain
\Omega\coloneqq(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap B_{1}
whose boundary is composed of two pieces:

\displaystyle\partial\Omega=(\partial\Omega\cap\mathbb{R}^{d})\cup(\partial\Omega\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d})\eqqcolon\Gamma\cup\Sigma.

Moreover, we equip the boundary with the natural choice of surface measure
\mu
on
\partial\Omega
defined by

\displaystyle d\mu(X)\coloneqq d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{\Gamma}(X)+w(X)d\mathcal{H}^{n-1}|_{\Sigma}(X),

where as previously,
w(X)=\delta(X)^{-n+d+1}
. Along with absolutely continuous measure
dm(X)=w(X)dX
on
\Omega
, such a choice of a boundary measure allows one to apply the elliptic theory developed in
[
DFM23b
]
for solutions to the degenerate elliptic equation

\displaystyle Lu=-\divv(\delta^{-n+d+1}\nabla u)

\displaystyle=0\text{ in }\Omega.

In particular, for each
X\in\Omega
we have the existence of a (unique) probability measure
\omega_{L}^{X}
on
\partial\Omega
for which the function

\displaystyle u_{f}(X)\coloneqq\int_{\partial\Omega}f\;d\omega^{X}_{L}

is the unique weak solution to the continuous Dirichlet problem for
f\in C(\partial\Omega)
:

(A.9)

\begin{split}Lu_{f}&=0\text{ in }\Omega,\\
u_{f}&=f\text{ on }\partial\Omega.\end{split}

See
[
DFM23b
]
Section 3.5 and Lemma 12.15.

To state the conclusion of the Theorem we need a few more definitions. Define the non-tangential approach regions
\gamma_{a}(X)
and the non-tangential maximal function
N_{a}(u)(X)
defined for
X\in\partial\Omega
and
a\in(0,1)
by

\displaystyle\gamma_{a}(X)\coloneqq\{Y\in\Omega\;:\;\delta(Y)>a\left\lvert Y-X\right\rvert\},\qquad N_{a}(u)(X)\coloneqq\sup_{\gamma_{a}(X)}\left\lvert u(Y)\right\rvert,

The main result we have for this domain is the following.

Theorem A.6
.

For any
X\in\Omega
,
\mu\ll\omega_{L}^{X}\ll\mu
. Moreover, there is some
\varepsilon=\varepsilon(n,d)>0
so that the density
\tfrac{d\omega_{L}^{X}}{d\mu}
belongs to the reverse Hölder class
\mathrm{RH}_{2+\varepsilon}(\mu)
in the sense that

\displaystyle\left(\fint_{B}\left(\dfrac{d\omega_{L}^{X}}{d\mu}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}d\mu\right)^{1/(2+\varepsilon)}\leq C_{X}\fint_{B}\dfrac{d\omega_{L}^{X}}{d\mu}\;d\mu

holds for each boundary ball
B_{r}(X_{0})
,
X_{0}\in\partial\Omega
and
0<r\leq 1
.

Moreover, the Dirichlet problem (
A.9
) is
L^{2}(\mu)
-solvable in the sense that for each
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, the function
u_{f}\in W_{r}(\Omega)
defined by

\displaystyle u_{f}(X)\coloneqq\int_{\partial\Omega}f\;d\omega_{L}^{X}

satisfies
Lu_{f}=0
in
\Omega
with the corresponding estimates

\displaystyle\left\lVert N_{a}(u_{f})\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu)}\leq C_{a}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{L^{2}(\mu)},\qquad\limsup_{Y\rightarrow X,\;:\;Y\in\gamma_{a}(X)}\left\lvert u(Y)-f(X)\right\rvert=0\text{ for }\mu\text{ a.e. }X\in\partial\Omega.

Proof.

The proof of this Theorem follows from well-developed techniques in elliptic boundary value problems, and the analogous statement for harmonic measure in the upper half ball,
B_{1}^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
. To avoid taking us too far from the current paper, let us provide a sketch of the main steps of the proof of the Theorem.

For
y\in B_{1}^{+}
, we use the notation
\omega_{-\Delta}^{y}
to denote the harmonic measure in
B_{1}^{+}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
with pole at
y
, i.e., the unique probability measure on
\partial B_{1}^{+}
for which solution to the continuous Dirichlet problem for
v_{f}\in C^{2}(B_{1}^{+})\cap C(\overline{\Omega})

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\Delta v_{f}&=0\text{ in }B_{1}^{+},\\
v_{f}&=f\text{ on }\partial B_{1}^{+},\end{split}

is given by the integral representation
v_{f}(y)=\int_{\partial B_{1}^{+}}f\;d\omega_{-\Delta}^{y}
. Since
B_{1}^{+}
is a bounded Lipschitz domain, Dahlberg’s Theorem implies that
\omega_{-\Delta}^{y}\ll\sigma\coloneqq\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{\partial B_{1}^{+}}\ll\omega_{-\Delta}^{y}
, and in fact the density
k^{y}(z)\coloneqq\tfrac{d\omega^{y}_{-\Delta}}{d\sigma}(z)
is in the reverse Hölder class
\mathrm{RH}_{2+\varepsilon}(\sigma)
for some
\varepsilon>0
for each
y\in B_{1}^{+}
. More specifically, from the presentation of Dahlberg’s Theorem in
[
PT24
, Theorem 10.1]
followed by Gehring’s Lemma on the Ahlfors regular set
\partial B_{1}^{+}
, we know that for each boundary ball
B_{r}(y_{0})
with
y_{0}\in\partial B_{1}^{+}
and
0<r<1
and each
y\in B_{1}^{+}
for which
\dist(y,2B_{r}(y_{0})\cap\partial B_{1}^{+})\geq\Lambda^{-1}r
, we have that

\displaystyle\left(\fint_{B_{r}(y_{0})\cap\partial\Omega}\left(\dfrac{d\omega^{y}_{-\Delta}}{d\sigma}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}\;d\sigma\right)^{1/(2+\varepsilon)}\leq C_{\Lambda}\fint_{B_{r}(y_{0})\cap\partial\Omega}\dfrac{d\omega^{y}_{-\Delta}}{d\sigma}\;d\sigma,

with
C_{\Lambda}>1
depending only on the dimension and
\Lambda>1
. Finally, from the uniform doubling property of harmonic measure in
B_{1}^{+}
,
[
PT24
, Lemma 8.17]
, the above improves to the reverse Hölder condition

(A.10)

\displaystyle\left(\fint_{B_{\Lambda r}(y_{0})\cap\partial\Omega}\left(\dfrac{d\omega^{y}_{-\Delta}}{d\sigma}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}\;d\sigma\right)^{1/(2+\varepsilon)}\leq C_{\Lambda}\fint_{B_{r}(y_{0})\cap\partial\Omega}\dfrac{d\omega^{y}_{-\Delta}}{d\sigma}\;d\sigma,

uniformly for
y\in B_{1}^{+}
for which
\dist(y,\partial B_{1}^{+})\geq\Lambda^{-1}r
, where
\Lambda>1
is any fixed number. It is precisely condition (
A.10
) that we wish to transfer to the density
\tfrac{d\omega_{L}^{Y}}{d\mu}
. In fact we prove a much stronger estimate saying these two densities are essentially bounded equivalent (see estimate (
A.14
)). This equivalence shall be a somewhat straightforward consequence of the fact that the measures
\mu
and
\omega^{Y}_{L}
are in some sense just rotations of the measures
\sigma
and
\omega^{y}_{-\Delta}
respectively, which we now justify.

For subsets
F\subset\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
, define the rotation
R(F)\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\displaystyle R(F)\coloneqq\{(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-d}\;:\;(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)\in F\}.

Similarly, if
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we abuse notation and still write
R(E)
for the rotation of
E
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle R(E)\coloneqq\{(x,Bt)\;:\;(x,t)\in E,\;B\in O(n-d)\},

where
O(n-d)
is the orthogonal group on
\mathbb{R}^{n-d}
. Associate to each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
a well-defined choice of a point
\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap R(\{y\})
, and associate for
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
the unique point
\tilde{Y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
for which
Y\in R(\{\tilde{Y}\})
. It is a straight-forward consequence of Remark
3.2
and the definition of the measures
\omega_{-\Delta}^{y}
and
\omega_{L}^{Y}
that the two are essentially the same:

(A.11)

\omega_{L}^{Y}(R(F))\equiv\omega_{-\Delta}^{\tilde{Y}}(F),\qquad\;Y\in\Omega,\;F\subset\partial B_{1}^{+}\text{ Borel.}

Indeed, given
f\in C(\partial B_{1}^{+})
, let
v_{f}
be its harmonic extension inside
B_{1}^{+}
. Defining
v(x,t)=v_{f}(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
, we see that Remark
3.2
implies that
v\in C(\overline{\Omega})
is a solution of (
A.9
) with boundary data
\tilde{f}(x,t)=f(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
, and so if
Y=(x,t)
, then
\tilde{Y}=(x,\left\lvert t\right\rvert)
and we have

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}\tilde{f}\;d\omega^{Y}_{L}=v(Y)=v_{f}(\tilde{Y})

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial B_{1}^{+}}f\;d\omega^{\tilde{Y}}_{-\Delta}.

Choosing an appropriate sequence of continuous functions, one can use the above to show that (
A.11
) holds for
F
open or compact. The claim then follows for Borel
F
using the fact that
\omega^{\tilde{Y}}_{-\Delta},\omega^{Y}_{L}
are inner and outer regular and a routine approximation argument.

Next, we claim that for some dimensional constant
C>1
, we have that

(A.12)

\displaystyle C^{-1}\mu(R(B_{r}(y_{0})))\leq\sigma(B_{r}(y_{0}))\leq C\mu(R(B_{r}(y_{0}))),\qquad y_{0}\in\partial B_{1}^{+},0<r<1.

Notice that when
B_{r}(y_{0})\cap\partial B_{1}^{+}\subset\partial B_{1}^{+}\cap\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
, the above as actually an equality just by the definitions of
\mu
and
\sigma
. On the other hand, for
y_{0}\in\partial B_{1}^{+}\setminus\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+}
and
r>0
sufficiently small, it is easy to check that
\mathcal{H}^{n-1}(R(B_{r}(y_{0}))\cap\Sigma)\simeq r^{d}\dist(y_{0},\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})^{n-d-1}
based on different considerations for when
\dist(y_{0},\partial\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})
is small or large. From the definition of
\mu
, we then see that for
r
sufficiently small,
\mu(R(B_{r}(y_{0})))\simeq r^{d}\simeq\sigma(B_{r}(y_{0}))
in this case. Finally, the last piece of information we need is the rotation invariance of
\omega^{Y}_{L}
and
\mu
; whenever
F\subset\partial\Omega
is Borel and
T
is a linear isometry which fixes
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, then

(A.13)

\displaystyle\mu(T(F))=\mu(F),\qquad\omega^{T(Y)}_{L}(T(F))=\omega^{Y}_{L}(F).

For the measure
\mu
this property is immediate from the definition, while for
\omega^{Y}_{L}
it follows again from Remark
3.2
, since the operator (
A.9
) is invariant under rotations in the
t
variable. In particular, we always know from uniqueness of solutions (Lemma 12.2
[
DFM23b
]
) that for
f\in C(\partial\Omega)
, we have that the solutions
u_{f}
and
u_{f\circ T}
as in the notation from (
A.9
) are related by
u_{f}\circ T=u_{f\circ T}
, since
u_{f}\circ T
is a solution as well, and has boundary data
f\circ T
. Evaluating this equality at
Y
means in terms of elliptic measures that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\Omega}f\;d\omega^{T(Y)}_{L}=\int_{\partial\Omega}f\circ T\;d\omega_{L}^{Y},

from which the second equality in (
A.13
) follows easily by taking a sequence of continuous functions
f_{k}
approximating
\chi_{F}
and recalling that
T
is invertible.

At this stage, we can compare the densities
\tfrac{d\omega_{L}}{d\mu}
and
\tfrac{d\omega_{-\Delta}}{d\sigma}
. Fix a pole
Y_{0}=(0,\dotsc,0,1/2)\in\Omega
,
Z\in\partial\Omega
and
0<r\ll 1
. Notice that as
Y\rightarrow\omega_{L}^{Y}(B_{r}(Z))
is a nonnegative solution of the elliptic equation (
A.9
) and
R(\{Y_{0}\})
is uniformly bounded away from
\partial\Omega
, Harnack’s inequality
[
DFM23b
, Lemma 11.35]
implies that for each
Y\in R(\{Y_{0}\})
we have
\omega^{Y}_{L}(B_{r}(Z))\simeq\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(B_{r}(Z))
. Moreover, we may use Vitali’s covering Lemma to cover
R(B_{r}(Z))
with
N_{r,Z}
balls
\{5B_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N_{r,Z}}
so that the
B_{i}
are disjoint, and moreover, such that the
B_{i}
have radius
r
and are centered in
R(\{Z\})
. Using the doubling property of elliptic measure
[
DFM23b
, Lemma 15.43]
which is valid for, say,
0<r<1/16
, it is easy to check that from our covering that

\displaystyle\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(R(B_{r}(Z)))\simeq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{r,Z}}\omega^{Y_{0}}_{L}(B_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{N_{r,Z}}\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(T_{i}(B_{r}(Z))),

where
T_{i}
is an appropriately chosen linear isometry mapping
B_{r}(Z)
to
B_{i}
fixing
\mathbb{R}^{d}
pointwise. Recalling (
A.13
) and that
\omega_{L}^{Y}\simeq\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}
on
R(\{Y_{0}\})
, the above gives us the comparability

\displaystyle\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(B_{r}(Z))\simeq N_{r,Z}^{-1}\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(R(B_{r}(Z))),

and of course the same argument applies to
\mu
:

\displaystyle\mu(B_{r}(Z))\simeq N_{r,Z}^{-1}\mu(R(B_{r}(Z))).

Combining the previous two inequalities with (
A.11
) and (
A.12
), we conclude for
Z\in\partial\Omega
and all
r>0
small that

\displaystyle\dfrac{\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(B_{r}(Z))}{\mu(B_{r}(Z))}\simeq\dfrac{\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(R(B_{r}(Z)))}{\mu(R(B_{r}(Z)))}\simeq\dfrac{\omega_{-\Delta}^{\tilde{Y_{0}}}(B_{r}(\tilde{Z}))}{\sigma(B_{r}(\tilde{Z}))}.

With the same boundary ball
B_{r}(Z)
with
Z\in\partial\Omega
and
r>0
sufficiently small, let
\Lambda>1
, and suppose that
Y\in B_{2\Lambda r}(Z)
with
\dist(Y,\partial\Omega)>(2\Lambda)^{-1}r
. Notice that by definition,
Y
is a corkscrew point for
B_{4\Lambda r}(Z)
in the sense that
B_{(4\Lambda)^{-1}r}(Y)\subset\Omega\cap B_{4\Lambda r}(Y)
. Hence by the Harnack inequality and the change of pole property for elliptic measure,
[
DFM23b
, Lemma 15.61]
, we see that whenever
Q\in\partial\Omega\cap B_{\Lambda r}(Z)
and
0<\rho<r
,

\displaystyle\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(B_{\Lambda r}(Z))\dfrac{\omega_{L}^{Y}(B_{\rho}(Q))}{\mu(B_{\rho}(Q))}\simeq_{\Lambda}\dfrac{\omega^{Y_{0}}_{L}(B_{\rho}(Q))}{\mu(B_{\rho}(Q))}\simeq_{\Lambda}\dfrac{\omega_{-\Delta}^{\tilde{Y_{0}}}(B_{\rho}(\tilde{Q}))}{\sigma(B_{\rho}(\tilde{Q}))}.

Upon sending
\rho\downarrow 0
, we conclude that with the constant
c_{\Lambda,Z,r}=\omega_{L}^{Y_{0}}(B_{\Lambda r}(Z))
,

(A.14)

\displaystyle c_{\Lambda,Z,r}\dfrac{d\omega_{L}^{Y}}{d\mu}(Q)\simeq_{\Lambda}\dfrac{d\omega_{-\Delta}^{\tilde{Y}_{0}}}{d\sigma}(\tilde{Q}),\qquad Q\in\partial\Omega\cap B_{\Lambda r}(Z).

It is a straight-forward consequence of (
A.14
), the comparability of
\mu
and
\sigma
as in (
A.12
), and the previous Vitali-type argument above that (
A.10
) (which is invariant under multiplication by constants) transfers to
\tfrac{d\omega_{L}}{d\mu}
to give us the reverse-Hölder estimate

\displaystyle\left(\fint_{B_{\Lambda r}(Z)}\left(\dfrac{d\omega_{L}^{Y}}{d\mu}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}\;d\mu\right)^{1/(2+\varepsilon)}\leq C_{\Lambda}\fint_{B_{r}(Z)\cap\Omega}\dfrac{d\omega_{L}^{Y}}{d\mu}\;d\mu

uniformly for
Z\in\partial\Omega
,
0<r<r_{0}\ll 1
, and
Y\in\Omega
such that
\dist(Y,\partial\Omega)\geq\Lambda^{-1}r
. That the above reverse-Hölder type estimate implies that
L^{2}(\mu)
solvability of the Dirichlet problem follows verbatim from the argument in
[
PT24
, Lemma 10.8]
, where boundary Hölder continuity of solutions with zero trace is guaranteed by
[
DFM23b
, Lemma 11.32]
.
∎

In what follows, as in Section
5
, we order the discrete set of frequencies
\mathcal{F}=\{\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\dotsc,\}
with
\Lambda_{1}=1
and
\Lambda_{k}<\Lambda_{k+1}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
. In addition, for each
\Lambda_{k}
with
k\in\mathbb{N}
, denote the linear space of
\Lambda_{k}
-homogeneous solutions
u\in W_{r}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\cap C(\mathbb{R}^{n})
of (
2.1
) which vanish on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
by
\mathrm{H}_{k}
. We shall abuse notation and also write
\mathrm{H}_{k}
for the restriction of such functions to
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. With this notation, the following result is analogous to the fact that the restrictions of homogeneous harmonic polynomials to
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
(i.e., spherical harmonics) are dense in
L^{2}(d\sigma)
.

Corollary A.7
.

The subspaces
\{\mathrm{H}_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
of
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
are finite-dimensional, pairwise orthogonal, and have dense span. In particular, we have the isomorphism

\displaystyle L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)\simeq\bigoplus_{k\geq 1}\mathrm{H}_{k},

and so each element
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
may be written as a series

\displaystyle f

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}a_{k}\phi_{k}

with
\{a_{k}\}\in\ell^{2}
,
\phi_{k}\in\mathrm{H}_{k}
, and
\left\lVert\phi_{k}\right\rVert_{L^{2}(d\mu)}=1
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathrm{H}_{k}
are finite-dimensional was pointed out previously in Remark
3.8
. First, fix
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
. By Theorem
A.6
, we know that there exists a solution
u_{g}\in W_{r}(\Omega)
satisfying
Lu_{g}=0
in
\Omega
with boundary data
g=g_{f}\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega,d\mu)
defined by

\displaystyle g(X)=\begin{cases}f(X)&X\in\Sigma,\\
0&X\in\Gamma.\end{cases}

Of course here, we mean that
u_{g}
has boundary data
g
in the sense that
N_{a}(u_{g})\in L^{2}(\partial\Omega,d\mu)
with the non-tangential convergence as in Theorem
A.6
. Next, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any fixed
0\neq\phi_{k}\in\mathrm{H}_{k}
nonzero, define the function

\displaystyle\psi_{f}^{k}(r)\coloneqq\fint_{\partial B_{r}}u_{g}\phi_{k}\;d\mu\equiv\fint_{\partial B_{1}}u_{g}(rY)\phi_{k}(rY)\;d\mu(Y).

A straight-forward computation shows that for
0<r<1
,

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{dr}\psi_{f}^{k}(r)

\displaystyle=\fint_{\partial B_{r}}(\partial_{n}u_{f})\psi_{k}+u_{f}(\partial_{n}\psi_{k})\;d\mu

\displaystyle=2\fint_{\partial B_{r}}u_{g}(\partial_{n}\phi_{k})\;d\mu=\dfrac{2\Lambda_{k}}{r}\fint_{\partial B_{r}}u_{g}\phi_{k}\;d\mu\equiv\dfrac{2\Lambda_{k}}{r}\psi^{k}_{f}(r).

Indeed the second equality follows from the fact that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{r}}(\partial_{n}u_{f})\psi_{k}-u_{f}(\partial_{n}\psi_{k})\;d\mu=0

since
u_{f},\phi_{k}
are solutions of (
2.1
) with zero trace on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{r}
, and the second from the fact that
\phi_{k}
is homogeneous of degree
\Lambda_{k}
.

In particular, notice that if we take
f=\phi_{j}\in\mathrm{H}_{j}
with
j\neq k
, then
\psi^{k}_{\phi_{j}}=\psi^{j}_{\phi_{k}}
, and the computation above shows us that

\displaystyle\dfrac{r}{2}\dfrac{d}{dr}\psi_{\phi_{j}}^{k}(r)=\Lambda_{k}\psi_{\phi_{j}}^{k}(r)=\Lambda_{j}\psi_{\phi_{j}}^{k}(r).

This shows that
\psi_{\phi_{j}}^{k}(r)\equiv 0
since
\Lambda_{k}\neq\Lambda_{j}
, and in particular this proves the first claim that the spaces
\mathrm{H}_{k}
are pairwise orthogonal in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
. Now let us show by contradiction that the
\{\mathrm{H}_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}
have dense span in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
. Assume on the contrary that there is
0\neq f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
for which

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}}f\phi_{k}\;d\mu=0,\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N},\,\phi_{k}\in\mathrm{H}_{k}.

With the notation as before, we recall that

\displaystyle\dfrac{d}{dr}\psi^{k}_{f}(r)=\dfrac{2\Lambda_{k}}{r}\psi_{f}^{k}(r),

while
\lim_{r\rightarrow 1^{-}}\psi^{k}_{f}(r)=\fint_{\partial B_{1}}f\phi_{k}\;d\mu=0
. This limit can be justified from the fact that the functions
u_{g}(r\,\cdot\,)\rightarrow f
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
as
r\rightarrow 1^{-}
since they are bounded by
N_{a}(u_{g})\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
and converge pointwise
\mu
almost everywhere to
g_{f}=f
on
\partial B_{1}
, as per Theorem
A.6
. It follows from the Picard-Lindelöf Theorem that
\psi^{k}_{f}(r)\equiv 0
, and thus

\displaystyle\fint_{\partial B_{r}}u_{f}\phi_{k}\;d\mu=0,\text{ for all }k\in\mathbb{N},\,\phi_{k}\in\mathrm{H}_{k},\,0<r<1.

However, the above is a clear contradiction to Corollary
4.15
, and so the Corollary is proved.
∎

Remark A.8
.

By Corollary
A.7
, we know that
L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)\simeq\bigoplus_{k\geq 0}\mathrm{H}_{k}
, and each
\mathrm{H}_{k}
is finite-dimensional. Thus we may choose for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
a finite-dimensional orthonormal basis of
\mathrm{H}_{k}
,
\{\phi_{j}^{k}\}_{1\leq j\leq N_{k}}
where
N_{k}=\dim\mathrm{H}_{K}
. Thus each
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1},d\mu)
may be expressed uniquely by a sum

(A.15)

\displaystyle f=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}a_{j}^{k}\phi_{j}^{k}

with convergence in
L^{2}(d\mu)
.

A.2.
More spherical harmonics: some computations

Much like in the case of harmonic functions, there are more homogeneous solutions to the “constant-coefficient” equation (
2.1
) if we consider solutions away from the origin; in such cases, we obtain more solutions which are homogeneous of negative degree. Moreover, such solutions can easily be computed in terms of those we characterized in Theorem
3.3
, which shall turn out to be useful in studying solutions of (
2.1
) in annuli. In this section we write (in usual polar coordinates, as opposed to section
3
),
r=\left\lvert X\right\rvert
and
\theta=X/\left\lvert X\right\rvert
.

Lemma A.9
.

Given
c_{R},c_{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\phi\in\mathrm{H}_{k}
, the unique solution of the boundary value problem

(A.16)

\displaystyle-\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\nabla u)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\,\,\text{ in }

\displaystyle(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho})\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=c_{R}\phi

\displaystyle\,\,\text{ on }

\displaystyle\partial B_{R},

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=c_{\rho}\phi

\displaystyle\,\,\text{ on }

\displaystyle\partial B_{\rho},

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\,\,\text{ on }

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho}),

for
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho})
is given by

\displaystyle u(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=(ar^{\Lambda_{k}}+br^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1})\phi(\theta),

with

\displaystyle a=\dfrac{c_{R}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}+d-1}c_{\rho}}{R^{\Lambda_{k}}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}+d-1}\rho^{\Lambda_{k}}},\qquad b=\dfrac{c_{\rho}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}}c_{R}}{\rho^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}}R^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1}}.

Proof.

Write
\Lambda=\Lambda_{k}
, and
u_{\Lambda}(r,\theta)=r^{\Lambda}\phi(\theta)
. Recalling Remark
3.1
, we know that
u_{\Lambda}
is a
\Lambda
-homogeneous solution of (
2.1
) classically, and a simple computation reveals that
v_{m}(r,\theta)\coloneqq r^{m}u_{\Lambda}(r,\theta)=r^{\Lambda+m}\phi(\theta)
also is a solution away from the origin for
m=-2\Lambda-d+1
. Indeed, using the fact that
\Delta(r^{m})=m(m+n-2)r^{m-2}
and the computation of the operator
\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\nabla\,\cdot\,)
as in the first line of (
3.3
), we see that

\displaystyle\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d+1}

\displaystyle\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\nabla v_{m})

\displaystyle=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}\Delta v_{m}+(-n+d+1)\nabla v_{m}\cdot t

\displaystyle=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}\left(r^{m}\Delta u_{\Lambda}+2\nabla u_{\Lambda}\cdot\nabla(r^{m})+u_{\Lambda}\Delta(r^{m})\right)+(-n+d+1)(r^{m}\nabla u_{\Lambda}+u_{\Lambda}\nabla(r^{m}))\cdot t

\displaystyle=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}\left(2mr^{m-1}\partial_{r}u+m(m+n-2)r^{m-2}u_{\Lambda}\right)+(-n+d+1)m\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}r^{m-2}u_{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}r^{m-2}u_{\Lambda}m\left(m+2\Lambda+d-1\right)

where in the second to last line we use that
u_{\Lambda}
is a solution, and in the last the fact that
u_{\Lambda}
is
\Lambda
-homogeneous. Hence we see that
v_{m}
is a solution for
m=-2\Lambda-d+1
, which is to say that
\tilde{u}_{\Lambda}(r,\theta)\coloneqq v_{-2\Lambda-d+1}(r,\theta)=r^{-\Lambda-d+1}\phi(\theta)
is a
(-\Lambda-d+1)
-homogeneous solution of (
2.1
) away from
0
. Notice now that
u_{\Lambda},\tilde{u}_{\Lambda}
are two linearly independent solutions of (
2.1
) in
B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho}
whose boundary data is proportional to
\phi
on each
\partial B_{r}
,
r\in(0,\infty)
. In particular, a solution of (
A.16
) is given by

\displaystyle u(r,\theta)\coloneqq ar^{\Lambda}+br^{-\Lambda-d+1}

where
a,b
are chosen to satisfy the linear system

\displaystyle aR^{\Lambda}+bR^{-\Lambda-d+1}

\displaystyle=c_{R}

\displaystyle a\rho^{\Lambda}+b\rho^{-\Lambda-d+1}

\displaystyle=c_{\rho},

or in other words,

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\dfrac{c_{R}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda+d-1}c_{\rho}}{R^{\Lambda}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda+d-1}\rho^{\Lambda}},

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\dfrac{c_{\rho}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda}c_{R}}{\rho^{-\Lambda-d+1}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda}R^{-\Lambda-d+1}}.

By the maximum principle (for elliptic equations, or
[
DFM23b
, Lemma 12.8]
),
u
is the only such solution.
∎

Applying the previous Lemma to each component of
L^{2}(d\mu)
boundary data, we obtain the following Corollary.

Corollary A.10
.

Given
f_{\rho},f_{R}\in L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\mu)
, write

\displaystyle f_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}c_{j,t}^{k}\phi_{j}^{k}

for
t=\rho,R
. Then the unique solution of the boundary value problem

(A.17)

\displaystyle-\divv(\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\nabla u)

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\,\,\text{ in }

\displaystyle(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho})\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d},

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=f_{t}

\displaystyle\,\,\text{ on }

\displaystyle\partial B_{t},\,\,t=\rho,R,

\displaystyle u

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\,\,\text{ on }

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho}),

for
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho})
is given by

\displaystyle u(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k}r^{\Lambda_{k}}+b_{j}^{k}r^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1})\phi_{j}^{k}(\theta)

with

\displaystyle a_{j}^{k}=\dfrac{c_{j,R}^{k}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}+d-1}c_{j,\rho}^{k}}{R^{\Lambda_{k}}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}+d-1}\rho^{\Lambda_{k}}},\qquad b_{j}^{k}=\dfrac{c_{j,\rho}^{k}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}}c_{j,R}^{k}}{\rho^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1}-\left(\tfrac{\rho}{R}\right)^{\Lambda_{k}}R^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1}}.

Here the boundary value attainment of
f_{\rho},f_{R}
is in the sense that
u(r\,\cdot\,)\rightarrow f_{t}
in
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\mu)
, as
r\rightarrow t
for
t=\rho,R
.

Proof.

One simply applies Lemma
A.9
to each component
\phi_{j}^{k}
individually to see that the representation given in the Corollary is a formally a solution of (
A.17
). Of course, one ought to check that the sum giving
u
actually converges to a solution, that
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho})
, and the boundary data is attained in
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\mu)
. This can be checked using the definition of the
a_{j}^{k},b_{j}^{k}
.

To check this claim, by scale invariance we may assume
1=\rho<R
. Then, from their definitions, we readily see that

\displaystyle\left\lvert a_{j}^{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{-\Lambda_{k}}\left\lvert c_{j,R}^{k}\right\rvert+R^{-2\Lambda_{k}}\left\lvert c_{j,\rho}^{k}\right\rvert,

\displaystyle\left\lvert b_{j}^{k}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lvert c_{j,\rho}^{k}\right\rvert+R^{-\Lambda_{k}}\left\lvert c_{j,R}^{k}\right\rvert.

It follows that
\{R^{\Lambda_{k}}a_{j}^{k}\},\{b_{j}^{k}\}\in\ell^{2}
. Using the fact that the
\phi_{j}^{k}
are orthonormal and
a_{j,1}^{k}+b_{j,1}^{k}=c_{j,1}^{k}
, it is easy to check from the dominated convergence theorem that for
r>1
,

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{1}}\left(u(r,\theta)-f_{1}\right)^{2}\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\left(a_{j}^{k}r^{\Lambda_{k}}+b_{j}^{k}r^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1}-c_{j,1}^{k}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\left(a_{j}^{k}(r^{\Lambda_{k}}-1)+b_{j}^{k}(r^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1}-1)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k})^{2}(r^{\Lambda_{k}}-1)^{2}+(b_{j}^{k})^{2}(r^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1}-1)^{2}\rightarrow 0

as
r\rightarrow 1^{+}
. A similar computation shows that
\lim_{r\rightarrow R^{-}}u(r,\theta)=f_{R}
in
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\mu)
.

To see that
u\in W_{r}(B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho})
and is a solution of (
A.17
), we simply need to show uniform energy estimates for
u^{M}(r,\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{M}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k}r^{\Lambda_{k}}+b_{j}^{k}r^{-\Lambda_{k}-d+1})\phi_{j}^{k}(\theta)
on compact subsets of
B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho}
that are independent of
M
:

(A.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{K}\left\lvert\nabla u^{M}\right\rvert^{2}\;dm\leq C_{K}<\infty

for
K\subset\subset B_{R}\setminus B_{\rho}
. Indeed, each
u^{M}
is a solution (being a finite sum of solutions, by
Lemma
A.9
), and usual, solutions are maintained under strong limits. To this end, we in fact only need to obtain uniform estimates on

\displaystyle\int_{K}(u^{M})^{2}dm

independent of
M
, since by the Caccioppoli inequality, such estimates on
u^{M}
automatically imply (
A.18
). However, such estimates again follow easily from the bounds on
a_{j}^{k},b_{j}^{k}
and the orthogonality of
\{\phi_{j}^{k}\}
, so we omit the details.
∎

With the representation of solution coming from Corollary
A.10
, we have a simple representation of the frequency
N_{u}
for solutions to constant-coefficient equations. Since the proof is essentially the same as the one for harmonic functions (see, for example
[
HL
]
), we omit it.

Lemma A.11
.

If
u\in W_{r}(B_{R})
is a solution of (
2.1
), then as per Corollary
A.10
, we know that
u
is given in polar coordinates by

\displaystyle u(r,\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}j_{k}^{k}r^{\Lambda_{k}}\phi_{j}^{k}(\theta),

where the
\{\phi_{j}^{k}\}
are an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\partial B_{1},d\sigma_{w})
. With this notation, we have that

\displaystyle r^{-d-1}\int_{B_{r}}\left\lvert\nabla u\right\rvert^{2}\;dm

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\Lambda_{k}(a_{j}^{k})^{2}r^{2\Lambda_{k}},

\displaystyle r^{-d}\int_{\partial B_{r}}u^{2}\;d\sigma_{w}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k})^{2}r^{2\Lambda_{k}},

and consequently,

\displaystyle N_{u}(0,r)

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}\Lambda_{k}(a_{j}^{k})^{2}r^{2\Lambda_{k}}}{\sum_{k\geq 1}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{k}}(a_{j}^{k})^{2}r^{2\Lambda_{k}}}.

Appendix B
The change of variables

In the theory of degenerate elliptic operators outside of sets of low dimension, there are a family of operators that behave in some sense like the Laplacian does in co-dimension one. Namely, if
\Gamma
is
d
-Ahlfors regular set in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define the regularized distance (for a fixed parameter
\beta>0
) by

(B.1)

\displaystyle D_{\beta}(X)\coloneqq\left(\int_{\Gamma}\dfrac{1}{\left\lvert X-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\right)^{-1/\beta}.

A straight-forward computation shows that
D_{\beta}\simeq_{n,d,\beta}\delta
, and
D_{\beta}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\Gamma)
. With this distance, we define the degenerate elliptic operator

(B.2)

\displaystyle L_{\beta}\coloneqq-\divv(D_{\beta}^{-n+d+1}\nabla\,\cdot\,),

which falls into the category of the elliptic operators as in Section
1
.

In the end, our goal in this section is to demonstrate that estimates on the singular set will hold for solutions associated to these operators
L_{\beta}
,
\Gamma
is a
d
-Ahlfors regular graph of
C^{1,1}
function, and
\beta>1
(the restriction on
\beta
will later become clear in the proof of Theorem
B.5
). Afterwords, we will pass such estimates on to the operator
L_{\infty}
, in order to prove Theorem
1.1
(see Remark
B.6
). In practice though, computations are made simpler by flattening the graph
\Gamma
with a bi-Lipschitz change of variables
\rho
and instead performing all of our analysis for solutions associated to a conjugated degenerate elliptic operator
L_{\rho,\beta}=-\divv(A_{\rho,\beta}\nabla\,\cdot\,)
(i.e., the one obtained through the change of variables) in the flat space
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{d}
(see (
B.5
)). In the classical setting (i.e., when
d=n-1
), this change of variables is also
C^{1,1}
, and since the operator
L_{\rho,\beta}
involves the Jacobian of
\rho
, the coefficients of
L_{\rho,\beta}
will be Lipschitz. Hence for this operator, one expects unique continuation results and estimates on the nodal and critical sets of solutions. In the higher co-dimension setting, we have an analogous change of variables, but since the space normal to
\Gamma
is at least 2-dimensional, the estimates are more technical to write down. Let us describe some aspects of this change of variables introduced in
[
DFM19
]
, with the goal of sketching a proof of the fact that the conjugated operator
L_{\rho,\beta}
associated to the change of variables satisfies the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition. See Theorem
B.5

In what follows, suppose that
\Gamma
is a
d
-dimensional,
d
-Ahlfors regular
C^{1,1}
graph parametrized by
\Phi(x)=(x,\phi(x))
, with
\phi\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{n-d})
satisfying the estimate

(B.3)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla\phi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq{C_{2}}.

For
\varepsilon>0
small, notice that (
B.3
) implies that for
0<r_{0}\leq{C_{2}}^{-1}\varepsilon/M
and
X_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\Gamma
, the graph
\Gamma
in
B_{r_{0}}(X_{0})
can be rewritten as the graph of a
C^{1,1}
function
\psi:V\rightarrow V^{\perp}
with
\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}}\leq\varepsilon
for some appropriately chosen
d
-plane
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Here
M\gg 1
is some constant which shall freely make large, but shall depend only on the dimensions
n
and
d
.

Upon translating, localizing, rotating, and relabeling, we abuse notation and rewrite
\psi
as
\phi
. Moreover we assume that
X_{0}=(0,t_{0})
. Hence we have that
\Gamma\cap B_{r_{0}}(X_{0})
coincides with the graph of a
C^{1,1}
function,
\phi:B_{r_{0}}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n-d}
with
