\nabla\phi(x_{0})=0
and the estimates

(B.4)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\nabla\phi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r_{0}})}\leq\varepsilon,\;\left\lVert\nabla\phi\right\rVert_{\mathrm{Lip}(B_{r_{0}})}\leq{C_{2}}.

Of course, this comes at the expense of losing the representation of
\Gamma
as the graph over a single function defined on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, but this will not bother us too much. Now from the estimates (
B.4
) one can follow the procedure in
[
DFM19
]
to construct a bi-Lipschitz mapping

\rho:B_{R_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\rho(B_{R_{0}})\supset B_{r_{0}}(X_{0})

with
R_{0}\simeq r_{0}
that maps
\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap B_{R_{0}}
to
\Gamma\cap\rho(B_{R_{0}})
. Here, the importance of localizing is that the smallness of
\left\lVert\nabla\phi\right\rVert_{L^{\infty}(B_{r_{0}})}
in (
B.4
) allows one to guarantee that the mapping
\rho
constructed is bi-Lipschitz.

It is easy to see by direct computation (see for example
[
DFM19
, Section 4]
) that
u
is a solution of (
B.2
) if and only if
v=u\circ\rho
solves
-\divv(A_{\rho,\beta}\nabla v)=0
in the weak sense, where
A_{\rho,\beta}
is defined by
A_{\rho,\beta}=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-n+d+1}\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}
, and

(B.5)

\begin{split}\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}(x,t)&=\left(\dfrac{\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))}\right)^{n-d-1}\left\lvert\det\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)\right\rvert(\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)^{-1})^{T}\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)^{-1}.\end{split}

Here,
\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)=\nabla\rho(x,t)
is the Jacobian. In view of these facts above, our main goal is to show that the matrix
\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition for some nice bi-Lipschitz map
\rho
(i.e., Theorem
B.5
). A proof of this fact unfortunately requires the recounting the details of the construction and properties of
\rho
, which takes us a bit far from the goals of the current paper. As such we rapidly outline what we need below.

B.1.
The higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition for
A_{\rho,\beta}

Let us first recount in detail the construction of the mapping
\rho
, assuming that we have the condition (
B.4
). In fact, by taking
M
large enough, notice we may assume (
B.4
) holds in an enlarged neighborhood of
B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which allows us to perform the following construction for points
x\in B_{r_{0}}
and scales
0<s<r_{0}
. We also adopt the notation in
[
DFM19
]
and write
\left\lvert t\right\rvert=r
when defining functions depending on
t
.

Take
\eta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be a smooth, nonnegative, radial bump function supported
B_{1}(0)
so that
\int\eta(x)\;dx=1
. Its dilations
\eta_{s}
for
s>0
are defined by
\eta_{s}(x)=s^{-d}\eta\left(\tfrac{x}{s}\right)

and the functions
\phi_{s}:B_{r_{0}}\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n-d}
by
\phi_{s}(x)=\eta_{s}*\phi(x)
for
s>0
small enough so that
\phi
is defined in
B_{s}(x)\cap\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In addition, set
\Phi_{s}(x)=(x,\phi_{s}(x))
. Let
P(x,s)
denote the tangent
d
-plane to
\phi_{s}(x)
at
\Phi_{s}(x)
, and set
P^{\prime}(x,s)=P(x,s)-\Phi_{s}(x)
(so that
P^{\prime}(x,s)
contains the origin). Finally, the mapping
\rho:B_{R_{0}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined by

(B.6)

\displaystyle\rho(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{r}(x)+h(x,r)R_{x,r}(0,t),

where
h(x,r)\simeq 1
is a scalar and
R_{x,r}
is an orthogonal transformation mapping
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
P^{\prime}(x,r)
defined as follows.

Fix a bump function
\theta\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\chi_{B_{1/2}}\leq\theta\leq\chi_{B_{1}}
and define

(B.7)

\displaystyle\theta_{x,r}(Y)\coloneqq\theta\left(\dfrac{\Phi_{r}(x)-Y}{r}\right),\qquad\lambda(x,r)\coloneqq a_{0}^{-1}r^{-d}\int_{\Gamma}\theta_{x,r}(Y)\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y),

where
a_{0}\simeq_{n,d}1
is some constant depending on the dimensions and the particular choice of
\theta
. Notice that
\lambda(x,r)\simeq 1
for
x\in B_{r_{0}}
and
0<r<r_{0}
since
\Gamma
is a
d
-dimensional
C^{1,1}
graph. Finally,
h
is defined by

(B.8)

h(x,t)=h(x,r)\coloneqq(c_{\beta}\lambda(x,r))^{1/\beta},

where
c_{\beta}\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\left\lvert y\right\rvert^{2})^{(-d-\beta)/2}\;dy>0
has the defining property that if
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
d
-plane and then one has

(B.9)

\displaystyle D_{V,\beta}(X)^{-\beta}\coloneqq\int_{V}\left\lvert X-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\equiv c_{\beta}\dist(X,V)^{-\beta},\;X\not\in V.

See for example
[
DFM19
, Section 5.3]
.

As for the rotation
R_{x,r}
, we denote by
\{e^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
the standard basis vectors, and set

(B.10)

\begin{split}\hat{v}^{i}(x,r)&=\partial_{x_{i}}\Phi_{r}(x)=(e^{i},\partial_{x_{i}}\phi_{r}(x)),\;\;i=1,\dotsc,d,\\
\hat{w}^{j}(x,r)&=((-\nabla_{x}\phi_{r}^{j})^{T},e^{j}),\;\;j=d+1,\dotsc,n,\end{split}

so that the
\{\hat{v}^{i}(x,r)\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{\hat{w}^{i}(x,r)\}_{i=d+1}^{n}
form bases for
P^{\prime}(x,r)
and
P^{\prime}(x,r)^{\perp}
respectively. Finally,
\{v_{i}(x,r)\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{w_{i}(x,r)\}_{i=d+1}^{n}
denote orthonormal bases for
P^{\prime}(x,r)
and
P^{\prime}(x,r)^{\perp}
obtained from
\{\hat{v}^{i}(x,r)\}_{i=1}^{d}
and
\{\hat{w}^{i}(x,r)\}_{i=d+1}^{n}
respectively via the Gram-Schmidt procedure (for details, see
[
DFM19
, Section 2]
). With this basis, then we denote by
R_{x,r}
the linear orthogonal mapping that maps the ordered basis
\{e^{1},\dotsc,e^{n}\}
to the ordered basis
\{v^{1}(x,r),\dotsc,v^{d}(x,r),w^{d+1}(x,r),\dotsc,w^{n}(x,r)\}
.

The authors in
[
DFM19
]
show that if
\varepsilon>0
is sufficiently small (or in otherwords, as long as
r_{0}
is sufficiently small) then these choices of
h(x,r)
and
R_{x,r}
give that

(B.11)

\displaystyle\rho\text{ is bi-Lipschitz with constant }1+C\varepsilon

from its domain
B_{R_{0}}
onto its image. Moreover, they give estimates on
\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)
which can be decomposed as

(B.12)

\displaystyle\nabla\rho(x,t)=\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)

\displaystyle=J(x,t)Q(x,t)^{T},

where
Q(x,t)^{T}
is the orthogonal matrix giving the rotation
R_{x,r}
in the sense that
R_{x,r}(y,u)=(y,u)Q(x,t)^{T}
and
J(x,t)
has good block-form structure
5
5

5

See the equations (3.9), (3.10) in
[
DFM19
]
.
. We should remark that in the definition above, we follow the convention in
[
DFM19
]
that Jacobian and matrix
Q(x,t)^{T}
act by
left
multiplication.

As for the matrix block structure of
J(x,t)
, one can prove that there is
d\times d
matrix
J_{1}^{\prime}(x,t)
so that
J(x,t)
asymptotically approaches

(B.13)

\displaystyle J^{\prime}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}J_{1}^{\prime}(x,t)&0\\
0&h(x,t)I_{n-d},\end{pmatrix}

near the boundary,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. More specifically, one can decompose
J=J^{\prime}+M+H
, and by
[
DFM19
, Lemmas 3.15, 3.26, 5.49]
we have the estimates

(B.14)

\begin{split}\left\lvert J^{\prime}_{1}(x,t)-I_{d}\right\rvert&\leq C\varepsilon^{2},\\
\left\lvert M(x,t)\right\rvert&\leq C\left(\left\lvert\partial_{r}\phi_{r}\right\rvert+r\left\lvert\nabla_{x,r}\nabla_{x}\phi_{r}\right\rvert\right),\\
\left\lvert H(x,t)\right\rvert&\leq Cr\left\lvert\nabla_{x,t}h(x,t)\right\rvert\leq C\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),Cr),\end{split}

where
\alpha_{\mu}
are the Tolsa
\alpha
numbers associated to
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{\Gamma}
defined as follows (see also
[
DFM19
]
and
[
Tol09
]
).

Definition B.1
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Flat}(n,d)
the set of measures of the form
c\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P}
where
c>0
and
P\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
d
-plane.
If
\mu
is a
d
-Ahlfors regular measure, then define for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r>0
,

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mu}(x,r)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{\nu\in\mathrm{Flat}(n,d)}\left(\sup_{f\in\Lambda(B_{r}(x))}r^{-d-1}\left\lvert\int f(d\mu-d\nu)\right\rvert\right)

where
\Lambda(B_{r}(x))
is the space of
1
-Lipschitz functions with compact support in
B_{r}(x)
.

When
\phi
is merely Lipschitz, (
B.14
) can be used to show that
\left\lvert M\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert H\right\rvert
satisfy Carleson measure estimates. In our setting, since
\phi
is of class
C^{1,1}
, we can obtain higher order decay.

Lemma B.2
.

In the setting above, if
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{\Gamma}
, then we have the estimates

\displaystyle\alpha_{\mu}(X,r)\leq C{C_{2}}\varepsilon r

for
X\in B_{r}(X_{0})\cap\Gamma
,
r<r_{0}
where
C=C(n,d)>0
.

Proof.

Let
f
be a
1
-Lipschitz function with compact support in
B_{r}(X)
, and set
U=\pi(\Gamma\cap B_{r}(X))\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then the area formula gives us

\displaystyle\int f\;d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{U}f\circ\phi(y)\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(y)\right\rvert^{2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y),

while the estimates on
\nabla^{2}\phi
in (
B.4
) and the mean value theorem for the function
g(s)=\sqrt{1+s^{2}}
for
0\leq s<\varepsilon
gives us

\displaystyle\left\lvert\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(y)\right\rvert^{2}}-\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(x)\right\rvert^{2}}\right\rvert\leq C\varepsilon\left\lvert\left\lvert\nabla\phi(y)\right\rvert-\left\lvert\nabla\phi(x)\right\rvert\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}\varepsilon\left\lvert x-y\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}\varepsilon r.

Recalling that
f
is
1
-Lipschitz, then
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq Cr
, and thus for the flat measure
d\nu(y)=\left(1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(x)\right\rvert^{2}\right)^{1/2}\;dy
, we have

\displaystyle r^{-d-1}\left\lvert\int f\;(d\mu-d\nu)\right\rvert\leq r^{-d-1}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\infty}\int_{U}\left\lvert\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(y)\right\rvert^{2}}-\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(x)\right\rvert^{2}}\right\rvert\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(y)\leq C{C_{2}}\varepsilon r,

which is exactly the claim.
∎

The following estimates are straight-forward consequences of the definition of
\phi_{r}(x)=\eta_{r}*\phi(x)
, the fact that
\eta
is radial, and the
C^{1,1}
regularity of
\phi
. Notice that this one degree of regularity of an improvement of
[
DFM19
, Lemma 3.17]
, since our graph
\Gamma
is locally
C^{1,1}
.

Lemma B.3
.

In the setting above, we have the estimates

\displaystyle r^{-1}\left\lvert\partial_{r}\phi_{r}(x)\right\rvert+\left\lvert\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\phi_{r}(x)\right\rvert+r\left\lvert\nabla_{x,r}^{3}\phi_{r}\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}},\;\left\lvert\nabla_{x}\phi_{r}\right\rvert\leq C\varepsilon

for
x\in B_{r_{0}}
and
0<r<r_{0}
with
C=C(n,d)
.

Proof.

Notice that we may write (by changing variables),

(B.15)

\displaystyle\phi_{r}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\eta(z)\phi(x-rz)\;dz,

so differentiating in
r
and using the fact that
\int\eta(z)z\;dz=0
since
\eta
is radial, we see

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\phi_{r}(x)

\displaystyle=\int\eta(z)\left\langle\nabla\phi(x-rz),-z\right\rangle\;dz

\displaystyle=\int\eta(z)\left\langle\nabla\phi(x-rz)-\nabla\phi(x),-z\right\rangle\;dz.

Using the Lipschitz bound on
\nabla\phi
gives us the desired estimate on
\left\lvert\partial_{r}\phi_{r}\right\rvert
. Differentiating (
B.15
) similarly gives us the bounds on
\nabla_{x}\phi_{r}
and
\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\phi_{r}
since
\left\lvert\nabla\phi\right\rvert\leq\varepsilon
and
\left\lvert\nabla^{2}\phi\right\rvert\leq{C_{2}}
.

As for the estimate on
\nabla_{x,r}^{3}\phi_{r}
, let us demonstrate just how to obtain the estimate on
\nabla_{x}\partial_{r}^{2}\phi_{r}
, since the other derivatives are handled in a similar manner. Using (
B.15
), we can write

\displaystyle\partial_{r}^{2}\phi_{r}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int\eta(z)z_{i}z_{j}\phi_{x_{i}x_{j}}(x-rz)\;dz

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\int\eta^{ij}_{r}(y)\phi_{x_{i}x_{j}}(x-y)\;dy=\eta^{ij}_{r}*\phi_{x_{i}x_{j}}(x)

for
\eta^{ij}(y)=\eta(y)y_{i}y_{j}
and
\eta^{ij}_{r}=r^{-d}\eta^{ij}(y/r)
. Computing one more derivative in
x
then gives us

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}\partial_{r}^{2}\phi_{r}(x)=(\nabla_{x}\eta^{ij}_{r})*\phi_{x_{i}x_{j}}(x),

and so since
\left\lvert\phi_{x_{i}x_{j}}\right\rvert\leq{C_{2}}
and
\left\lvert\nabla_{x}\eta^{ij}_{r}\right\rvert\leq Cr^{-1}
, this gives us the desired bound on
\nabla_{x}\partial_{r}^{2}\phi_{r}(x)
.
∎

In view of the previous Lemmas and (
B.14
), we get the estimate
\left\lvert M(x,t)\right\rvert+\left\lvert H(x,t)\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}r
, and so the errors
M
and
H
vanish linearly at the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

Corollary B.4
.

In the setting above, there exists a decomposition of
J(x,t)=\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)Q(x,t)
of the form
J=J^{\prime}+M+H
where
J^{\prime}
is a uniformly elliptic matrix,

\displaystyle J^{\prime}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}J_{1}^{\prime}(x,t)&0\\
0&h(x,t)I_{n-d}\\
\end{pmatrix},

and
\left\lvert M(x,t)\right\rvert+\left\lvert H(x,t)\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}r
, where
C=C(n,d)>0
.

With these estimates, we can now give a sketch of proof of the claim we made earlier that the conjugated matrix
A_{\rho,\beta}
satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition. Unfortunately, rigorously writing down the appropriate estimates for the matrix
A_{\rho,\beta}
requires us to dig into some of the estimates and techniques from
[
DFM19
]
. We stress that the work done in
[
DFM19
]
provides most of the framework for the Theorem, and that one just needs to check that the improved estimates coming from Lemmas
B.2
and
B.3
lead to higher regularity on the matrix
A_{\rho,\beta}
.

Theorem B.5
.

Let
\Gamma
be the graph of
d
-dimensional
C^{1,1}
function
\phi\in C(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{n-d})
so that (
B.3
) holds, and let
\beta>1
. Then for each
X_{0}=(x_{0},t_{0})\in\Gamma
and
r_{0}
sufficiently small (depending on
{C_{2}}
,
n
, and
d
), the change of variables

\displaystyle\rho:B_{r_{0}}((x_{0},0))\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\rho(B_{r_{0}}((x_{0},0))

detailed as in (
B.6
) is bi-Lipschitz (with constant at most
2
), and moreover, the corresponding conjugated matrix
A_{\rho,\beta}
defined in (
B.5
) satisfies the higher co-dimensional
C^{0,1}
condition in
B_{r_{0}}((x_{0},0))
with constants
\lambda=\lambda(n,d,\beta)>0
and
C_{0}=C_{0}(n,d,\beta,{C_{2}})>0
.

Sketch of proof.

First, one needs to check the Lipschitz nature of the uniformly elliptic part of the matrix
A_{\rho,\beta}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}=\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{n-d-1}A_{\rho,\beta}
which is

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}(x,t)=\left(\dfrac{\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))}\right)^{n-d-1}\left\lvert\det\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)\right\rvert(\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)^{-1})^{T}\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)^{-1}.

Lemma 3.26 in
[
DFM19
]
says that
\det{\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)}\simeq h(x,t)^{n-d}\simeq 1
as long as
\varepsilon
in (
B.4
) is small enough, so clearly
\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)
is invertible. In addition, we have that
\left\lvert t\right\rvert\simeq D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))
by (
B.11
), since
\left\lvert t\right\rvert=\dist((x,t),\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))\simeq\dist(\rho(x,t),\Gamma)
by
[
DFM19
, Lemma 5.1]
, and since
\rho
maps
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
\Gamma
. Moreover, it is easy to see from Corollary
B.4
that
\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)
is uniformly bounded and elliptic, and so the same holds for
\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}
. In view of these facts, it then suffices to show just that

(B.16)

\displaystyle(x,t)\rightarrow\mathrm{Jac}(x,t),\qquad(x,t)\rightarrow\dfrac{\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{D_{\beta}(\rho(x,r))},

are Lipschitz functions in the variables
(x,t)
in order to prove that
\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}
is Lipschitz. Let us start by showing
\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)=\nabla\rho(x,t)
is Lipschitz by estimating
\nabla_{x,t}^{2}\rho(x,t)
.

To this end, recalling the definition
\rho(x,t)=\Phi_{r}(x)+h(x,r)R_{x,r}(0,t)
with
\Phi_{r}(x)=(x,\phi_{r}(x))
, Lemma
B.3
implies that
\left\lvert\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\Phi_{r}(x)\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}
. Thus we need only show that
\left\lvert\nabla_{x,t}^{2}(h(x,r)R_{x,r}(0,t))\right\rvert\leq C
, which is a consequence of the inequalities

(B.17)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{x,t}h(x,r)\right\rvert+r\left\lvert\nabla_{x,t}^{2}h(x,r)\right\rvert+

\displaystyle\leq C,

(B.18)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{x,t}(R_{x,r})\right\rvert+r\left\lvert\nabla_{x,t}^{2}(R_{x,r})\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C.

To be clear, in (
B.18
) we view
R_{x,r}
as a matrix valued function.

Recalling the definition of
h\simeq 1
as in (
B.8
), the estimates (
B.17
) follow from the same inequalities with
\lambda
in place of
h
. The first estimate is then a direct consequence of
[
DFM19
, Lemma 5.49]
which says
\left\lvert\nabla_{x,r}\lambda(x,r)\right\rvert\leq C\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),Cr)/r
and Lemma
B.2
. One may also deduce the appropriate estimates on
\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\lambda(x,r)
using the same arguments as in the proof of that Lemma. Namely, one computes directly the components of

\displaystyle\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\left(r^{-d}\theta_{x,r}(Y)\right)=\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\left(r^{-d}\theta\left(\dfrac{\Phi_{r}(x)-Y}{r}\right)\right),

each of which can be seen to be a
(\tilde{C}r^{-2})
-Lipschitz function by the estimates on
\phi_{r}
in Lemma
B.3
, where
\tilde{C}
is allowed to depend on
{C_{2}}
. Comparing
\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\lambda(x,r)
with the corresponding quantity
\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\tilde{\lambda}(x,r)
,

\displaystyle\tilde{\lambda}(x,r)\coloneqq a_{0}^{-1}r^{-d}\int_{P(x,r)}\theta_{x,r}(Y)\;d\mu_{x,r}(Y)

where
\mu_{x,r}
is an appropriately chosen flat measure
\mu_{x,r}=\lambda(x,r)\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P(x,r)}
allows one to obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla_{x,r}^{2}\lambda(x,r)\right\rvert\leq\tilde{C}r^{-2}\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),Cr)\leq\tilde{C}r^{-1}{C_{2}}\varepsilon.

See the arguments in
[
DFM19
, Lemmas 5.22, 5.49]
. By virtue of Lemma
B.2
, this gives the second estimate in (
B.17
).

The estimates on
R_{x,r}
are slightly simpler. Recall that the rotation
R_{x,r}=R_{x,r}
is given by mapping the standard orthonormal basis
\{e^{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
to another one,

\{v^{1}(x,r),\dotsc,v^{d}(x,r),w^{d+1}(x,r),\dotsc,w^{n}(x,r)\},

as in (
B.10
). From the fact that
\left\lvert\hat{v}^{i}\right\rvert,\left\lvert\hat{w}^{i}\right\rvert\simeq 1
(from the bound (
B.4
) on
\nabla\phi
), and that the
v^{i}
,
w^{i}
are obtained from the
\hat{v}^{i},\hat{w}^{i}
from a bounded number of algebraic manipulations, it is easy to see that
R_{x,r}
is Lipschitz provided that
\hat{v}^{i},\hat{w}^{i}
are. This, however, is an immediate consequence of their definition in (
B.10
), and Lemma
B.3
. Altogether, these arguments show that
\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)
is Lipschitz.

Finally, this leaves us to showing that
(x,t)\rightarrow\left\lvert t\right\rvert/D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))
is Lipschitz. To ease notation, write
D=D_{\beta}
, and notice that it suffices to show

(B.19)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(D(\rho(x,t))^{-\beta}-\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta+1}

(B.20)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla D(\rho(x,t))^{-\beta}-\nabla\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}.

Indeed, the fact that
\left\lvert t\right\rvert\simeq D\circ\rho(x,t)
,
\left\lvert\nabla\left\lvert t\right\rvert\right\rvert+\left\lvert\nabla D\right\rvert\lesssim 1
, and the quotient rule readily imply that
\left\lvert t\right\rvert/(D\circ\rho(x,t))
is Lipschitz when (
B.19
) and (
B.20
) hold. To this end, most of our work is done for us already; notice that since
R_{x,r}
is an isometry and
P(x,r)
is the tangent plane to
\phi_{r}
at
\Phi_{r}(x)
, then the definition of
\rho
readily gives

\displaystyle\dist(\rho(x,t),P(x,r))=\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-\Phi_{r}(x)\right\rvert

\displaystyle=h(x,t)\left\lvert t\right\rvert,

or in other words,
\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}\equiv c_{\beta}\lambda(x,r)\dist(\rho(x,t),P(x,r))^{-\beta}
. Lemma 5.59 in
[
DFM19
]
then gives

(B.21)

\displaystyle\left\lvert(D(\rho(x,t))^{-\beta}-c_{\beta}\lambda(x,r)\dist(\rho(x,t),P(x,r))^{-\beta}\right\rvert\leq Cr^{-\beta}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{-k\beta}\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),C2^{k}r),

where again, the
\alpha_{\mu}
are the Tolsa
\alpha
numbers associated to the measure
\mu=\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{\Gamma}
. Using Lemma
B.2
and the fact that
\alpha_{\mu}\lesssim 1
, we have that
\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),C2^{k}r)\leq C2^{k}r
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
(indeed if
C2^{k}r>r_{0}
, we bound crudely
\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),C2^{k}r)\leq C\leq Cr_{0}^{-1}2^{k}r
). Hence (
B.21
) then gives us

\displaystyle\left\lvert(D(\rho(x,t))^{-\beta}-\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}\right\rvert\leq Cr^{-\beta+1}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{-k(\beta-1)}\leq Cr^{-\beta+1},

since
\beta>1
, which is our first estimate.

As for the term with the gradients, we compute (recalling that the Jacobian acts on the left)

(B.22)

\displaystyle\nabla(D(\rho(x,t))^{-\beta})

\displaystyle=(-d-\beta)\int\dfrac{\nabla\rho(x,t)(\rho(x,t)-Y)}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mu(Y).

On the other hand, we have from (
B.9
) and again the fact that
h(x,t)\left\lvert t\right\rvert=\dist(\rho(x,t),P(x,r))
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta-2}

\displaystyle=h(x,t)^{\beta+2}\dist(\rho(x,t),P(x,r))^{-\beta-2}=\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{c_{\beta+2}^{-1}h(x,t)^{\beta+2}}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y),

and thus,

(B.23)

\displaystyle\nabla\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}

\displaystyle=-\beta t\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{c_{\beta+2}^{-1}h(x,t)^{\beta+2}}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y).

Recalling the definition of
Q(x,t)^{T}
as the matrix that maps
\mathbb{R}^{d}
to
P^{\prime}(x,r)
(by left multiplication), then
h(x,t)t=Q(x,t)^{T}(\rho(x,t)-\Phi_{r}(x))
and so since
\rho(x,t)-\Phi_{r}(x)
is orthogonal to
P(x,r)
, we obtain by symmetry that

(B.24)

\begin{split}\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{h(x,t)t}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)&=\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{Q(x,t)^{T}(\rho(x,t)-\Phi_{r}(x))}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\\
&=\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{Q(x,t)^{T}(\rho(x,t)-Y)}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y).\end{split}

Finally, recalling the decomposition
J=J^{\prime}+H+M
and Corollary
B.4
, we notice that
J^{\prime}(x,t)t=ht
, and thus
\left\lvert J(x,t)t-h(x,t)t\right\rvert\leq C\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{2}
. Hence we combine (
B.23
), (
B.24
) , the definition (
B.12
) and the bound
\int_{P(x,r)}\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta-2}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\leq C\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta-2}
to conclude

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{h(x,t)^{2}t}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)-\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{\nabla\rho(x,t)(\rho(x,t)-Y)}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\right\rvert\leq C\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}.

It can be shown
[
Fen22
, (3.30)]
that
(\beta+d)c_{\beta+2}=\beta c_{\beta}
, and thus from (
B.23
), the above estimate, and the fact that
h^{\beta}\equiv c_{\beta}\lambda
, we obtain the estimate

(B.25)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}-(-d-\beta)\lambda(x,r)\int_{P(x,r)}\dfrac{\nabla\rho(x,t)(\rho(x,t)-Y)}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\right\rvert\leq C\left\lvert t\right\rvert^{-\beta}.

As a final step, one can use the same arguments as in Lemm 5.59 of
[
DFM19
]
, or more directly,
[
FL23
, Corollary 3.8]
to obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\left\lvert\int\dfrac{\nabla\rho(x,t)(\rho(x,t)-Y)}{\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{d+\beta+2}}\;\left(d\mu-\lambda(x,r)d\mathcal{H}^{d}|_{P(x,r)}\right)\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\beta-1}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{-k(\beta+1)}\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),C2^{k}r)

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\beta},

since
\alpha_{\mu}(\Phi(x),Cr)\leq Cr
as argued previously. Indeed, the only essential difference in the proof of the first inequality above and the estimate (
B.21
) in
[
DFM19
]
is the fact that in the annulus
A_{k}=B_{2^{k}r}(\Phi(x))\setminus B_{2^{k-1}r}(\Phi(x))
, the integrand
f(Y)=\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta-2}(\rho(x,t)-Y)
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lvert f(Y)\right\rvert+2^{k}r\left\lvert\nabla f(Y)\right\rvert\leq C(2^{k}r)^{-d-\beta-1}\text{ in }A_{k},

whereas the integrand
g(Y)=\left\lvert\rho(x,t)-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta}
satisfies

\displaystyle\left\lvert g(Y)\right\rvert+2^{k}r\left\lvert\nabla g(Y)\right\rvert\leq C(2^{k}r)^{-d-\beta},\text{ in }A_{k}.

Following the exact argument in
[
DFM19
]
Lemma 5.59 with this replacement then gives the estimate. In conjunction with (
B.22
) and the estimate (
B.25
), our desired estimate (
B.20
) then follows.

To finish the proof, we need to verify the existence of the approximating block-form matrix
\mathcal{B}
for
\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}
as in Definition
4.1
. Recalling that
\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)=J(x,t)Q(x,t)^{T}
with
Q(x,t)
an orthogonal matrix, we may write

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}(x,t)=\left(\dfrac{\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))}\right)^{n-d-1}\left\lvert\det\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)\right\rvert(J(x,t)^{-1})^{T}J(x,t)^{-1}.

By Corollary
B.4
, we know that there is a decomposition
J=J^{\prime}+M+H
where
J^{\prime}
is uniformly elliptic with appropriate block structure, and
\left\lvert M(x,t)\right\rvert+\left\lvert H(x,t)\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}r
. In particular, notice that

\displaystyle J^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(J^{\prime}+M+H\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(J^{\prime})^{-1}\left(I+(J^{\prime})^{-1}M+(J^{\prime})^{-1}H\right)^{-1},

and so the fact that
J^{\prime}
is uniformly elliptic and the bounds on
M
and
H
then give us that

\displaystyle J(x,t)^{-1}

\displaystyle=(J^{\prime}(x,t))^{-1}+E(x,t)

with error satisfying
\left\lvert E(x,t)\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}r
. Recalling the block structure on
J^{\prime}
, we compute

\begin{split}(J(x,t)^{-1})^{T}J(x,t)^{-1}&=((J^{\prime}(x,t))^{-1})^{T}(J^{\prime}(x,t))^{-1}+\tilde{E}(x,t)\\
&=\begin{pmatrix}((J_{1}^{\prime}(x,t))^{-1})^{T}(J_{1}^{\prime}(x,t))^{-1}&0\\
0&h(x,t)^{-2}I\\
\end{pmatrix}+\tilde{E}(x,t),\end{split}

where again the estimate
\left\lvert\tilde{E}(x,t)\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}r
holds.

We now have a natural candidate for the matrix
\mathcal{B}
required in the higher co-dimension
C^{0,1}
condition: define

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(x,t)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\dfrac{\left\lvert t\right\rvert}{D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))}\right)^{n-d-1}\left\lvert\det\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)\right\rvert((J^{\prime}(x,t))^{-1})^{T}(J^{\prime}(x,t))^{-1}.

Notice that since
D_{\beta}(\rho(x,t))\simeq t
and
\det\mathrm{Jac}(x,t)\simeq h^{n-d}\simeq 1
, the above estimate on
\tilde{E}
gives us that

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{A}_{\rho,\beta}(x,t)-\mathcal{B}(x,t)\right\rvert\leq C{C_{2}}r=C{C_{2}}\delta,

which is the one of the required conditions for
\mathcal{B}
. The second required condition is that
\mathcal{B}
be Lipschitz and uniformly elliptic. Uniform ellipticity is clear, again by Corollary
B.4
and the fact that
h\simeq 1
. On the other hand, we see that
\mathcal{B}
is Lipschitz provided the entries of
J_{1}^{\prime}(x,t)
are Lipschitz, since we already have Lipschitz bounds on the other terms defining
\mathcal{B}
in (
B.16
) and (
B.17
). To this end, we invoke the definition of
(J_{1}^{\prime}(x,t)_{k\ell}=\left\langle\hat{v}^{k},v^{\ell}\right\rangle
from
[
DFM19
, Definition 3.12]
, where the
\hat{v}^{k}
and
v^{k}
are those same vectors as coming from (
B.10
). However, as mentioned there, these entries are Lipschitz since the
v^{k}
and
\hat{v}^{k}
are all bounded and Lipschitz by Lemma
B.3
. Altogether, this completes the proof of the Theorem.
∎

Remark B.6
.

It turns out that a
C^{1,1}
smoothness assumption on
\Gamma
is also enough to guarantee that our main result, Theorem
1.1
, holds for operators of the form

(B.26)

L_{a}\coloneqq-\divv(a\,\delta_{\Gamma}^{-n+d+1}\nabla\,\cdot\,),

whenever
a\simeq 1
is uniformly Lipschitz, where
\delta_{\Gamma}(X)=\dist(X,\Gamma)
again is the usual Euclidean distance function to the graph
\Gamma
. At the moment, though, we do not know how to prove this without passing through the same estimates of the proof of Theorem
B.5
(and the regularized distance
D_{\beta}
).

In fact, in light of Corollary
6.5
it suffices to show that when
\Gamma
is
C^{1,1}
and
\beta>2
we have that
X\mapsto\frac{D_{\beta}(X)}{\delta(X)}
is a Lipschitz function (we already know it is uniformly bound away from zero and infinity) in a neighborhood of the boundary.

First, since
\Gamma
is given by the graph of the map
\phi\in C^{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{n-d})
with the bound (
B.3
), we know that there is a small ball
B_{R_{0}}
centered on
\Gamma
for which the tubular neighborhood of
\Gamma
is well-defined (see for example,
[
Fed59
, Remark 4.20]
); for each
Z\in B_{R_{0}}
, there exists a unique point
P(Z)\in\Gamma
such that
\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)=\left\lvert Z-P(Z)\right\rvert
. Of course the scale
R_{0}
depends on the bound (
B.3
). If we denote by
e_{Z}=(Z-P(Z))/\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)
, then we know that
e_{Z}
is a unit vector orthogonal to the tangent plane of
\Gamma
at
P(Z)
, and moreover,

(B.27)

\begin{split}\nabla\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)&=e_{Z},\\
\delta_{\Gamma}(P(Z)+te_{Z})&=t\text{ for }t\in(0,\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)).\end{split}

To show
(D_{\beta}/\delta_{\Gamma})(Z)
is Lipschitz, just like before, it suffices to prove

(B.28)

\displaystyle\left\lvert c_{\beta}\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)^{-\beta}-D_{\beta}(Z)^{-\beta}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)^{-\beta+1},

(B.29)

\displaystyle\left\lvert\nabla(c_{\beta}\delta_{\Gamma}^{-\beta})(Z)-D_{\beta}(Z)^{-\beta}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{\Gamma}(Z).

which are analogous to (
B.19
) and (
B.20
) in the proof of Theorem
B.5
. For the sake of brevity, let us just prove (
B.28
).

Fix
Z\in B_{R_{0}}
, and assume for the sake of notation that
P(Z)=0
, and additionally (up to rotation and translation),
\left\lvert\nabla\phi(0)\right\rvert=\phi(0)=0
. With this choice of notation, we see then that
\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)=\delta_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(Z)
coincides with the distance from
Z
to the tangent plane to
\Gamma
at
0
, which is
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Recalling the defining property of
c_{\beta}
as in (
B.9
) and the fact that
\Gamma
is the graph of
\phi
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we can then write

(B.30)

\begin{split}\left\lvert c_{\beta}\delta_{\Gamma}(Z)^{-\beta}-D_{\beta}(Z)^{-\beta}\right\rvert&=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert Z-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)-\int_{\Gamma}\left\lvert Z-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\right\rvert\\
&=\left\lvert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\lvert Z-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta}\left(1-\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(Y)\right\rvert^{2}}\right)\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\right\rvert\\
&\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap A_{k}}\left\lvert Z-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta}\left\lvert 1-\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(y)\right\rvert^{2}}\right\rvert\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y),\end{split}

where we set
r=\delta(Z)
,
A_{0}\coloneqq B_{2r}
, and
A_{k}\coloneqq B_{2^{k}r}\setminus B_{2^{k-1}r}
for
k\geq 1
. Choose
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
to be the smallest integer with
2^{k_{0}}r>r^{1/2}
. Taking
R_{0}
(and hence
r
) to be small, we know that the fact that
\nabla^{2}\phi
is bounded and
\nabla\phi(0)=0
implies that
\left\lvert\nabla\phi(Y)\right\rvert\leq C\left\lvert Y\right\rvert
for
\left\lvert Y\right\rvert
small, so that
\left\lvert 1-(1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(Y)\right\rvert^{2})^{1/2}\right\rvert\leq C\left\lvert Y\right\rvert^{2}
for
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap A_{k}
,
0\leq k\leq k_{0}
. This allows us to easily estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq k\leq k_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap A_{k}}\left\lvert Z-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta}

\displaystyle\left\lvert 1-\sqrt{1+\left\lvert\nabla\phi(y)\right\rvert^{2}}\right\rvert\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{0\leq k\leq k_{0}}\mathcal{H}^{d}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap A_{k})(2^{k}r)^{2}(2^{k}r)^{-d-\beta}

\displaystyle\leq Cr^{-\beta+2}\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{k(2-\beta)}\leq Cr^{-\beta+2}

provided that
\beta>2
. On the other hand, we crudely estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{k>k_{0}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\cap A_{k}}\left\lvert Z-Y\right\rvert^{-d-\beta}\;d\mathcal{H}^{d}(Y)\leq C\sum_{k>k_{0}}(2^{k}r)^{-\beta}\leq Cr^{-\beta}2^{-k_{0}\beta}\leq Cr^{-\beta+\beta/2}\leq Cr^{-\beta+1}

by the choice of
k_{0}
and since
\beta>2
. Combining the above two estimates (and using that
\Gamma
is
d
-Ahlfors regular) with (
B.30
) then shows (
B.28
). The estimate for (
B.29
) is argued in a similar manner, using also (
B.27
) and the techniques used in proving (
B.20
). We omit the details for the sake of brevity.
